<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prp:prelim:qi}
 Let $(\mathcal{Q},d)$ be a $\mathsf{CAT}(\kappa)${} space, $\kappa\geq0$, and $\alpha\in[1,2]$.
 For $y,z,q,p\in\mathcal{Q}$ with $\overline{yz},\overline{qp}<\pi/\sqrt\kappa$ if $\kappa>0$, so that the segments $[y,z]$ and $[q,p]$ are unique \cite[Proposition II.1.4(1)]{BridsonHaefliger1999NPC-Springer}, put $R := \sqrt{\kappa} \,\mathsf{diam}([y,z]\cup[q,p])$, and assume $R < \pi$.
 Set $C_0 := 1$ and $C_x := x / \sin(x)$ for $0<x<\pi$.
 Then
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:prelim:qi}
    \left| \overline{yq}^\alpha-\overline{yp}^\alpha-\overline{zq}^\alpha+\overline{zp}^\alpha \right|
    \leq 2^{2-\alpha}\alpha C_R\,\overline{qp}\,\overline{yz}\,
    \mathsf{diam}\left\lbrace y,z,q,p \right\rbrace^{\alpha-2}.
 \end{equation}
 The right-hand side is defined as zero if either $\overline{qp}=0$ or $\overline{yz}=0$.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:separability}
  We assume a separable support in the definition of $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Q})$ so that expressions like $d(Y,Y^\prime)$ are measurable, where $Y, Y^\prime$ are random variables in $\mathcal{Q}$ with laws in $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Q})$.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lmm:variances}
    Let $(\mathcal{Q}, d)$ be a metric space, $s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}$, and $\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Q})$.
    Then
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:variances:comparison}
        2^{\min(1-s,0)}\, \mathbf{V}^{s}_{\!M}\!\left( \mu \right)
        \leq
        \mathbf{V}^{s}_{\!F}\!\left( \mu \right)
        \leq
        2\, \mathbf{V}^{s}_{\!M}\!\left( \mu \right)
        \,.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:two:infty:independent}
    Let $(w_i)_{i\in\mathbb{N}} \subseteq [0,1]$ such that $\sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i = 1$.
    Let $(Y_i)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}$ be a sequence of independent random variables with distributions $\mu_1, \mu_2, \dots \in \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Q})$.
    Let $\bar\mu := \sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i \mu_i$ and $\hat\mu := \sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i \delta_{Y_i}$.
    Assume $\bar\mu \in \mathcal{P}^1(\mathcal{Q})$.
    Set $\bar m := m_2(\bar\mu)$ and $\hat m := m_2(\hat\mu)$.
    Then
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:two:infty:independent}
        \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\bar m\hat m}^2 \right]
        \leq
        \sum_{i=1}^{\infty} w_i^2 \mathbf{V_{\!M}}\!\left[ Y_i \right]
        \,.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
\label{ex:two:infinitevariance}
 Set $\mathcal{Q}=\mathbb{R}$, $w_i=2^{-i}$, and $a_i=2^{i/2}$, $i\geq1$.
 \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
  \item Let $\mu_i=\delta_{a_i}$. Then $\bar m=\sum_iw_ia_i=\sum_i2^{-i/2}<\infty$, whereas
  \[
   \sum_{i=1}^{\infty}w_i|a_i-\bar m|^2=\infty.
  \]
  Thus $\bar\mu\in\mathcal{P}^1(\mathbb{R})\setminus\mathcal{P}^2(\mathbb{R})$.
  Every component has zero metric variance and $\hat\mu=\bar\mu$ almost surely, so \eqref{eq:two:infty:independent} becomes $0\leq 0$.
  \item Let the $Y_i$ be independent with $\mu_i=\frac12(\delta_{-a_i}+\delta_{a_i})$.
  Then $\mathbf{E}[Y_i]=0$, $\mathbf{E}[|Y_i|]=2^{i/2}$, and $\mathbf{V_{\!M}}[Y_i]=\mathbf{E}[|Y_i|^2]=2^i$.
  Consequently,
  \begin{align*}
   \sum_{i=1}^{\infty}w_i\mathbf{E}\!\left[ |Y_i| \right]&=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}2^{-i/2}<\infty,\\
   \sum_{i=1}^{\infty}w_i\mathbf{E}\!\left[ |Y_i|^2 \right]&=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}1=\infty,\\
   \sum_{i=1}^{\infty}w_i^2\mathbf{V_{\!M}}\!\left[ Y_i \right]&=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}2^{-i}=1.
  \end{align*}
  Thus, $\bar\mu\in\mathcal{P}^1(\mathbb{R})\setminus\mathcal{P}^2(\mathbb{R})$ and $\bar m=0$, and \eqref{eq:two:infty:independent} becomes $1\leq 1$.
 \end{enumerate}
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rem:weights}
    We collect consequences of \cref{thm:two:infty:independent} for several weight schemes, retaining its independence and mixture-moment assumptions.
	\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item (Equal weights.)
        With $w_i = \frac1n\mathbf{1}_{\{i\leq n\}}$, write $\hat m_n$ for $\hat m$; identically distributed $Y_i$ give
        $\mathbf{E}[\overline{\bar m\hat m_n}^2] \leq \frac1{n} \mathbf{V_{\!M}}[Y_1]$, which is the penultimate step of the proof of \cite[Theorem 3]{BrunelSerres2026FiniteSample-arxiv}; their stated bound follows because $\mathbf{V_{\!M}}[Y_1]\leq2\mathbf{V_{\!F}}[Y_1]$.
        In general we obtain $\mathbf{E}[\overline{\bar m\hat m_n}^2] \leq \frac1{n^2}\sum_{i=1}^{n} \mathbf{V_{\!M}}[Y_i]$, which implies the $\kappa\leq0$ bound of \cite[Theorem 4]{BrunelSerres2026FiniteSample-arxiv}, where all $\mu_i$ are assumed to have the same Fr\'echet mean.
        Under the additional assumption of a curvature lower bound, Le Gouic et al.\ \cite[Theorem 1.3]{LeGouicEtAl2023FastConvergence-JEMS} give $\mathbf{E}[\overline{\bar m\hat m_n}^2]\leq\mathbf{V_{\!F}}[Y_1]/n$ for iid observations. Thus, by \eqref{eq:two:variances}, the Fr\'echet variance bound implied by \cref{thm:two:infty:independent} is off by at most a factor $2$.
        Whether that factor is needed, that is, whether $\mathbf{V_{\!F}}$ may replace $\mathbf{V_{\!M}}$ in every Hadamard space, is an open question.
		\item (Effective sample size.)
        Write $\Vertw\Vert_2^2 := \sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i^2$ and call $n_{\mathrm{eff}} := \Vertw\Vert_2^{-2}$ the \emph{effective sample size}.
		With $v := \sup\{\mathbf{V_{\!M}}[Y_i]: i\in\mathbb{N},\ w_i>0\}$, the bound \eqref{eq:two:infty:independent} implies $\mathbf{E}[\overline{\bar m\hat m}^2] \leq v\, n_{\mathrm{eff}}^{-1}$.
		For $w_i = \frac1n\mathbf{1}_{\{i\leq n\}}$ we get $n_{\mathrm{eff}} = n$.
        \item (Triangular arrays.)
        Since $\Vertw\Vert_2^2 \leq \sup_iw_i$, any triangular scheme of weights $(w_i^{(n)})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}$ with $\sup_iw_i^{(n)}\to0$ and $\sup_{n,i}\mathbf{V_{\!M}}[Y_i^{(n)}]<\infty$ yields $L^2$ consistency for the moving target $m_2(\sum_i w_i^{(n)} \mu_i^{(n)})$, where both the weights and the distributions may vary with $n$.
		\item (Geometric weights.)
		For $\rho \in (0,1)$ and $w_i = (1-\rho)\rho^{i-1}$, $i\in\mathbb{N}$, the estimator $\hat m$ is the Fr\'echet mean of an exponentially weighted infinite independent sequence.
		Here $\Vertw\Vert_2^2 = \frac{1-\rho}{1+\rho}$, so that $n_{\mathrm{eff}} = \frac{1+\rho}{1-\rho}$ and, for iid data, $\mathbf{E}[\overline{\bar m\hat m}^2] \leq \frac{1-\rho}{1+\rho}\mathbf{V_{\!M}}[Y_1]$.
		For non-identically distributed data, e.g., under distributional drift, the target $\bar m$ automatically discounts old (large $i$) distributions geometrically. The bounds hold without stationarity, but still require independence.
		\item (Polynomially weighted windows.)
		For a window of length $n$ and $\lambda \geq 0$, let $w_i \propto (n+1-i)^\lambda\, \mathbf{1}_{\{i\leq n\}}$, with $i = 1$ the most recent observation.
		Then $\Vertw\Vert_2^2 = \frac{(\lambda+1)^2}{(2\lambda+1)\, n}\, (1 + \mathrm{o}(1))$ as $n\to\infty$, so the parametric rate is retained up to the constant $\frac{(\lambda+1)^2}{2\lambda+1}$; for $\lambda = 1$, the linearly weighted moving average common in time series analysis, $\Vertw\Vert_2^2 = \frac{4}{3n}\, (1 + \mathrm{o}(1))$.
		\item (Polynomial weights on all of $\mathbb{N}$.)
		For fixed $\lambda > 0$ and $w_i = i^{-1-\lambda}/\zeta(1+\lambda)$, $i \in \mathbb{N}$, with $\zeta$ the Riemann zeta function, we have $\Vertw\Vert_2^2 = \zeta(2+2\lambda)/\zeta(1+\lambda)^2 > 0$. As $\lambda \searrow 0$, $n_{\mathrm{eff}} = \zeta(1+\lambda)^2/\zeta(2+2\lambda) \sim \frac{6}{\pi^2 \lambda^2}$ diverges. Hence $\mathbf{E}[\overline{\bar m\hat m}^2] \xrightarrow{\lambda\searrow0} 0$ whenever $\sup_i\mathbf{V_{\!M}}[Y_i]<\infty$.
	\end{enumerate}
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:two:localreg}
    Let $(\mathcal{X}, \rho)$ be a metric space.
    Let $\mu_z\in\mathcal{P}^2(\mathcal{Q})$, $z\in\mathcal{X}$, satisfy
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:two:localreg:smooth}
        W_1(\mu_z,\mu_{z^\prime})\leq L\rho(z,z^\prime)^\beta
    \end{equation}
    for some $L>0$ and $\beta\in(0,1]$.
    Set $v := \sup_{z\in\mathcal{X}}\mathbf{V_{\!M}}(\mu_z)$ and assume $v<\infty$.
    Let $Y_i\sim\mu_{x_i}$ be independent, and let the kernel and the estimator be as in \eqref{eq:localfrechet:kernel} and \eqref{eq:localfrechet:weights}.
    Let $x\in\mathcal{X}$.
    Assume that, for some $D \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}$ and $C_{\mathsf{dns}}\in [1,\infty)$, the design satisfies
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:localfrechet:design}
    N_n(x,h/2)\geq C_{\mathsf{dns}}^{-1}nh^D.
    \end{equation}
    Then
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:localfrechet:designbound}
    \left( \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\hat m_h\!(x)\,m_2(\mu_x)}^2 \right] \right)^{1/2}
    \leq L h^\beta+\sqrt{\frac{vC_{\mathsf{Kmi}}C_{\mathsf{Kma}}C_{\mathsf{dns}}}{nh^D}}
    \,.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rem:two:smoothness}
For the local constant estimator with $\beta=1$, the smoothness assumption of \cite{Schoetz2022Regression-EJS} and the local constant version of the assumption of \cite{PetersenMueller2019Regression-AOS} are \emph{density} conditions.
In the setting of \cref{cor:two:localreg} with real predictors, they lead to a probability measure $\nu$ on $\mathcal{Q}$ with $\mu_x\ll\nu$ for all $x$, and densities $f(y\mid x):=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_x}{\mathrm{d}\nu}(y)$ such that $x\mapsto f(y\mid x)$ is Lipschitz with a constant $L(y)$ for $\nu$-almost every $y$.
In \cite[Theorems 1--2]{Schoetz2022Regression-EJS} (assumption \textsc{H\"olderSmoothDensity} with polynomial order zero), $\nu$ may be any probability measure dominating the conditional laws and $\VertL\Vert_{L^2(\nu)}\leq C_{\mathrm{SmD}}$; a second moment $\int\overline{ym_2(\mu_0)}^2\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y)\leq C_{\mathrm{Int}}$ of the reference measure is assumed as well, with $C_{\mathrm{SmD}},C_{\mathrm{Int}}\geq1$.
The published condition (L1) of \cite{PetersenMueller2019Regression-AOS} requires the marginal density $f_X$ of $X$ and the conditional densities $g_y$ of $X$ given $Y=y$ to be twice continuously differentiable, with $\sup_{x,y}\vertg_y^{\prime\prime}(x)\vert<\infty$; it also requires continuity of $x\mapsto\mathbf{P}(Y\in U\mid X=x)$ for every open $U\subseteq\mathcal{Q}$.
For the local constant comparison, the corresponding first-order condition is Lipschitz continuity of $f_X$ and a Lipschitz bound $L_g$ on $g_y$ that is uniform in $y$, retaining the continuity requirement on the conditional laws.
On any covariate interval on which $f_X\geq f_->0$, Bayes' formula with $\nu$ the law of $Y$ gives $f(y\mid x)=g_y(x)/f_X(x)$ and hence a uniform Lipschitz bound on $f(y\mid\cdot)$ depending on $L_g$, $f_-$, and $\operatorname{Lip}(f_X)$; the bound is derived in \cref{app:comparison}.
Provided the reference measure has a finite second moment, this local constant density condition therefore implies, on such an interval, the density smoothness condition of \cite{Schoetz2022Regression-EJS}, which in turn implies \eqref{eq:two:localreg:smooth} with $L=C_{\mathrm{Int}}^{1/2}C_{\mathrm{SmD}}$ and the same $\beta$.

The gap between density and transport smoothness reflects two mechanisms by which $x\mapsto\mu_x$ can vary.
Under \emph{reweighting}, mass is redistributed over a fixed support. Density conditions control this variation and imply a bound on $W_1$, by the common-mass bound $W_1(\mu_x,\mu_{x^\prime})\leq\int\overline{yo}\,\vertf(y\mid x)-f(y\mid x^\prime)\vert\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y)$ used in \cref{app:comparison}.
Under \emph{transport}, the support itself moves: $W_1(\delta_q,\delta_p)=\overline{qp}$, whereas a common dominating probability measure need not exist. When densities exist, they may be discontinuous in $x$, as for translated uniform noise.
Density conditions measure changes at fixed locations and can be much more restrictive than transport smoothness; \cref{ex:two:phase} quantifies the difference.

A third mechanism, neither a density nor a transport condition, is used in \cite[Assumption 4]{TuckerWu2025PartiallyGlobal-SS}: H\"older continuity of the conditional Fr\'echet objective,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:two:localreg:objective}
 F_x(q):=\int\overline{yq}^2\,\mathrm{d}\mu_x(y)
 \,,\qquad
 \sup_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}\left| F_x(q)-F_{x^\prime}(q) \right|\leq L\rho(x,x^\prime)^\beta
 \,.
\end{equation}
If $\mathcal{Q}$ is bounded, as in \cite{TuckerWu2025PartiallyGlobal-SS}, this condition is implied by \eqref{eq:two:localreg:smooth}, with $2\mathsf{diam}(\mathcal{Q})L$ in place of $L$.
On unbounded response spaces it is not: for $\mathcal{Q}=\mathbb{R}$ and $\mu_x:=\delta_x$, \eqref{eq:two:localreg:smooth} holds with $L=\beta=1$, whereas $\sup_{q\in\mathbb{R}}\vert(x-q)^2-(x^\prime-q)^2\vert=\infty$ for $x\neq x^\prime$.
The three conditions are thus ordered, \emph{density} $\Rightarrow$ \emph{transport} $\Rightarrow$ \emph{objective}, the second implication on bounded response spaces, and both implications are strict; this is verified in \cref{app:comparison}.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
\label{ex:two:phase}
Let $\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{Q}$ be an isometric embedding of the real line into the Hadamard space $(\mathcal{Q},d)$, i.e., $\overline{\gamma(s)\gamma(t)}=\verts-t\vert$ for all $s,t\in\mathbb{R}$, let $U$ be a real random variable with $\mathbf{E}[U]=0$ and $\mathbf{E}[U^2]<\infty$, let $\tau\geq0$, and consider real predictors $x\in[0,1]$ with
\begin{equation*}
 Y_x:=\gamma(x+\tau U)\sim\mu_x\,.
\end{equation*}
The noise acts on the parameter of the geodesic, and $\tau=0$ is the noise-free response $Y_x=\gamma(x)$.
(If only a geodesic segment $\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathcal{Q}$ is available and $\vertU\vert\leq M$ almost surely, the statements below remain valid provided $[a,b]\supseteq[-\tau M,1+\tau M]$; the two noise laws in (ii) have $M=1$.)
The following statements are verified in \cref{app:comparison}.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
 \item (Transport smoothness.) For all $\tau\geq0$ and $x,x^\prime\in[0,1]$,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:two:phase:w1}
  W_1(\mu_x,\mu_{x^\prime})=\left| x-x^\prime \right|\,,\qquad m_2(\mu_x)=\gamma(x)\,,\qquad\mathbf{V_{\!M}}\!\left( \mu_x \right)=\tau^2\mathbf{E}\!\left[ U^2 \right]\,.
 \end{equation}
 Thus \eqref{eq:two:localreg:smooth} holds with $L=1$ and $\beta=1$, uniformly in $\tau$. For the design $x_i=i/n$ and $4/n\leq h\leq1$, \eqref{eq:localfrechet:design} holds with $D=1$ and $C_{\mathsf{dns}}=4$, and \cref{cor:two:localreg} gives
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:two:phase:bound}
  \left( \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\hat m_h\!(x)\gamma(x)}^2 \right] \right)^{1/2}\leq h+2\tau \sqrt{\frac{C_{\mathsf{Kmi}}C_{\mathsf{Kma}} \mathbf{E}\!\left[ U^2 \right]}{nh}}\,,
 \end{equation}
 which for $\tau=0$ is the deterministic bound $h$.
 \item (Density smoothness fails or degrades.) Let $\tau>0$. If $U\sim\mathsf{Unif}\!\left( [-1,1] \right)$, then for every probability measure $\nu$ dominating all $\mu_x$, $x\in[0,1]$, there is no family of densities $f(\cdot\mid x)$, $x\in[0,1]$, such that $x\mapsto f(y\mid x)$ is continuous for $\nu$-almost every $y$; the smoothness assumption of \cite[Theorems 1--2]{Schoetz2022Regression-EJS} fails for every $\beta>0$, and so does the local constant analogue of (L1) in \cite{PetersenMueller2019Regression-AOS}, although each $\mu_x$ has a bounded density with respect to arc length on $\gamma$.
 If instead $U$ has the triangular density $u\mapsto(1-\vertu\vert)_+$, the assumption of \cite{Schoetz2022Regression-EJS} holds with $\beta=1$, with $\nu$ the normalized arc-length measure on $\gamma([-\tau,1+\tau])$ and $C_{\mathrm{SmD}}=\max\{1,(1+2\tau)/\tau^2\}$, and this choice is optimal: every admissible choice of $\nu$ and $C_{\mathrm{SmD}}$ with $\beta=1$ satisfies $C_{\mathrm{SmD}}\geq(1+2\tau)/\tau^2$. For $\beta\in(0,1]$ and $\tau\leq\frac12$, the optimal $C_{\mathrm{SmD}}$ lies between $\tau^{-1-\beta}$ and $(1+2\tau)\tau^{-1-\beta}$.
\end{enumerate}
The transport constant remains $L=1$ in both families for every $\tau\geq0$, whereas no admissible density constant exists for uniform noise, the optimal one for triangular noise is of order $\tau^{-1-\beta}$ and diverges as the noise vanishes, and at $\tau=0$ the moving point masses admit no common dominating probability measure.
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
\label{rem:pos:diameter}
    Set $\delta:=\sqrt\kappa\,\mathsf{diam}(\mathcal{C})$. Then $\delta\leq\Delta\leq2\delta$.
    Hence, the diameter condition $\delta<\pi/2$ implies the radius condition $\Delta<\pi$, whereas $\Delta<\pi$ is compatible with every diameter $\delta<\pi$.
    The condition $\delta<\pi$ alone still gives the quadruple inequality, but does not ensure a distribution-uniform variance inequality with a constant depending only on $\delta$ (\cref{prp:pos:diameterobstruction} in the appendix).
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
\label{rem:pos:sharper}
    Recall $C_\delta=\delta/\sin(\delta)$.
    For $0<\delta<\pi/2$, the optimal diameter-only variance and contraction constants are
    \begin{equation*}
        A_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}:=\frac{\theta_\delta^2}{\delta^2-\theta_\delta^2},
        \qquad B_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}:=\frac1{\sqrt{\cos(\delta)}},
        \qquad \theta_\delta:=\arccos\!\left( \sqrt{\cos(\delta)} \right).
    \end{equation*}
    Both tend to $1$ as $\delta\searrow0$ and are set to $+\infty$ for $\delta\geq\pi/2$.
    \Cref{thm:pos:hemisphere:vi,thm:pos:hemisphere:cp} in the appendix give the optimal radius-only constants $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}},B_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}$ implicitly; we do not know elementary closed forms for them.
    Restricting the base point to $\overline{mq}\leq\varrho$ does admit one: \cref{thm:pos:hemisphere:vi}\,(ii) gives the explicit optimal constant $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{near}}$ on that range, which is also an explicit lower bound for $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}$.
    The radius constants satisfy the non-sharp upper bounds $A^{\mathrm{rad}}_\Delta<1/\cos(\Delta/2)<C_\Delta$ and $B^{\mathrm{rad}}_\Delta<\min\{1/\cos(\Delta/2)^2,2C_\Delta\}$ (\cref{thm:pos:hemisphere:vi,thm:pos:hemisphere:cp} and \cref{lmm:pos:cp:scalar} in the appendix), which give the explicit choices
    \begin{align}
        A_{\Delta,\delta}&:=\min\{1/\cos(\Delta/2),A_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}\},\label{eq:pos:vi:best}\\
        B_{\Delta,\delta}&:=\min\{1/\cos(\Delta/2)^2,2C_\Delta,B_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}},C_\delta A_{\Delta,\delta}\}.\label{eq:pos:cp:best}
    \end{align}
    We can replace $C_\Delta$ in \eqref{eq:2fmcatk:qi}, $C_\Delta$ in \eqref{eq:2fmcatk:vi}, and $C_\Delta^2$ in \eqref{eq:2fmcatk:CP} by $C_\delta$, $A_{\Delta,\delta}$, and $B_{\Delta,\delta}$, respectively.
    In particular, the multiplier $C_\delta A_{\Delta,\delta}B_{\Delta,\delta}$ that replaces $C_\Delta^4$ in \cref{thm:pos:infty:independent} is at most $\min\{C_\Delta/\cos(\Delta/2)^3,2C_\Delta^2/\cos(\Delta/2)\}$, which is less than $C_\Delta^4$ and, by its second term, less than $2C_\Delta^3$.
    \Cref{lmm:pos:variances} in the appendix also sharpens \eqref{eq:pos:variances} to $\mathbf{V_{\!F}}[Y]\leq2A_{\Delta,\delta}(1+A_{\Delta,\delta})^{-1}\mathbf{V_{\!M}}[Y]$.

    To gauge what the explicit surrogates cost, here are the optimal radius constants at three values of $\Delta$, computed from \cref{thm:pos:hemisphere:vi,thm:pos:hemisphere:cp}.
    \begin{center}
        \small
        \begin{tabular}{@{}rrrrrrrrr@{}}
            \toprule
            $\Delta$ & $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{near}}$ & $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}$ & $1/\cos(\Delta/2)$ & $C_\Delta$ & $B_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}$ & $1/\cos(\Delta/2)^2$ & $2C_\Delta$ & $C_\Delta^2$\\
            \midrule
            $1$ & $1.094$ & $1.094$ & $1.139$ & $1.188$ & $1.188$ & $1.298$ & $2.377$ & $1.412$\\
            $2$ & $1.601$ & $1.605$ & $1.851$ & $2.200$ & $2.208$ & $3.426$ & $4.399$ & $4.838$\\
            $3$ & $11.15$ & $11.55$ & $14.14$ & $21.26$ & $23.41$ & $199.9$ & $42.52$ & $451.9$\\
            \bottomrule
        \end{tabular}
    \end{center}
    At $\Delta=1$ the pairs $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{near}},A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}$ and $C_\Delta,B_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}$ each agree to the digits shown; nevertheless, $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{near}}<A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}<1/\cos(\Delta/2)<C_\Delta<B_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}<\min\{1/\cos(\Delta/2)^2,2C_\Delta\}$ and $1/\cos(\Delta/2)^2<C_\Delta^2$ for every $0<\Delta<\pi$, by \cref{thm:pos:hemisphere:vi}\,(ii), \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:upper}, and \eqref{eq:pos:cp:radius:upper} in the appendix. The bound $2C_\Delta$ is the smaller one from $\Delta\approx2.33$ on.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:pos:infty:independent}
    In the setting of \cref{ssec:pos:prelim}, let $(w_i)_{i\in\mathbb{N}} \subseteq [0,1]$ such that $\sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i = 1$.
    Let $(Y_i)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}$ be a sequence of independent random variables with distributions $\mu_1, \mu_2, \dots \in \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$.
    Let $\bar\mu := \sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i \mu_i$ and $\hat\mu := \sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i \delta_{Y_i}$. 
    Set $\bar m := m_2(\bar\mu)$ and $\hat m := m_2(\hat\mu)$.
    Then
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:infty:independent}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\bar m\hat m}^2 \right]
		\leq
		C_\Delta^4\sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i^2\, \mathbf{V_{\!M}}\!\left[ Y_i \right]
		\,.
	\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\label{rem:pos:literature}
    A key tool for obtaining \cref{thm:pos:infty:independent} is the variance inequality \eqref{eq:2fmcatk:vi} (see also \cref{thm:pos:hemisphere:vi} in the appendix) in $\mathsf{CAT}(\kappa)${} spaces. This article improves the previously known inequalities of this kind.

    To compare them, work in the notation of \cref{ssec:pos:prelim}, let $\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$, $Y\sim\mu$ and $m:=m_2(\mu)$, and write
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal E_\mu(q):=\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Yq}^2-\overline{Ym}^2 \right]
        \,,\qquad q\in\mathcal{C}
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    for the excess of the objective over its minimum. In this notation the variance inequality with constant $A$ is 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:literature:vi}
        \overline{mq}^2\leq A\,\mathcal E_\mu(q)
        \,.
    \end{equation}

    A function $f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{R}$ is \emph{$k$-strongly convex}, $k\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq0}$, if
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:strongconvexity}
        f(\gamma_t)\leq(1-t)f(\gamma_0)+tf(\gamma_1)-\frac k2\,t(1-t)\,\overline{\gamma_0\gamma_1}^2
    \end{equation}
    for every geodesic $\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathcal{C}$ and every $t\in[0,1]$; for $k=0$ this is convexity. If the objective $q\mapsto\int\overline{yq}^2\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)$ is $k$-strongly convex with $k>0$, then applying \eqref{eq:pos:strongconvexity} along the geodesic from $m$ to $q$, using $f(\gamma_t)\geq f(m)$, and letting $t\searrow0$ gives $\mathcal E_\mu(q)\geq\frac k2\overline{mq}^2$, which is \eqref{eq:pos:literature:vi} with $A=2/k$.

    The following table compares different results in the literature; here $\cot=\cos/\sin$.
    \begin{center}
        \small
        \begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
            \toprule
            source & hypothesis & statement\\
            \midrule
            \cite[Theorem 1.2]{Karcher1977CenterOfMass-CPAM} & Riemannian, $\Delta<\pi/2$ & $q\mapsto\int\overline{yq}^2\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)$ is $2\Delta\cot(\Delta)$-strongly convex\\
            \cite[Proposition 3.1(i)]{Ohta2007Convexities-GD} & $\delta<\pi/2$ & $q\mapsto\overline{yq}^2$ is $2\delta\cot(\delta)$-strongly convex, each $y$\\
            \cite[Corollary 16]{Yokota2017PBarycenter-TJM} & $\delta<\pi/2$ & $\mathcal E_\mu(q)\geq\delta\cot(\delta)\,\overline{mq}^2$\\
            \cite[Proposition 3]{BrunelSerres2026FiniteSample-arxiv} & $\Delta<\pi/2$ & $\mathcal E_\mu(q)\geq\Delta\cot(\Delta)\,\overline{mq}^2$\\
            \cite[Proposition 19]{Yokota2016Barycenter-JMSJ} & $\Delta<\pi$ & $\mathcal E_\mu(q)\geq c\,\overline{mq}^{a}$ for some $a>2$, $c>0$\\
            \cite[Theorem 1.1]{Gietl2026VarianceInequalities-AGMS} & $\Delta<\pi$ & $\overline{mo}^2\leq\mathcal E_\mu(o)+(1-\tfrac\Delta2\cot(\tfrac\Delta2))\mathbf{V_{\!F}}(\mu)$\\
            \Cref{thm:pos:hemisphere:vi} & $\Delta<\pi$ & $\mathcal E_\mu(q)\geq\min\{A^{\mathrm{rad}}_\Delta,A^{\mathrm{diam}}_\delta\}^{-1}\overline{mq}^2$\\
            \bottomrule
        \end{tabular}
    \end{center}
    Each statement is asserted for every $\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$ and every $q\in\mathcal{C}$, with one exception: Gietl's bound is asserted only at the center $o$ of a ball containing $\mathcal{C}$. In the displayed Gietl bound, $o$ is a point with $\mathcal{C}\subseteq\overline B(o,\varrho)$, if one exists, and $\mathcal E_\mu(o)$ is given by the same formula; the coefficient follows by applying the cited theorem to balls about $o$ with radii decreasing to $\varrho$. In \cite[Corollary 16]{Yokota2017PBarycenter-TJM} the modulus is left unspecified; the explicit constant in that row uses Ohta's modulus.
    By the argument above, the strong convexity of Karcher and of Ohta yields \eqref{eq:pos:literature:vi} with $A=\tan(\Delta)/\Delta$ and $A=\tan(\delta)/\delta$, respectively, which are also the constants of the variance inequalities \cite[Proposition 3]{BrunelSerres2026FiniteSample-arxiv} and \cite[Corollary 16]{Yokota2017PBarycenter-TJM}, respectively.
    The inequalities of Yokota \cite[Proposition 19]{Yokota2016Barycenter-JMSJ} and Gietl \cite[Theorem 1.1]{Gietl2026VarianceInequalities-AGMS} are of a different form. In the former, the growth is of order $\overline{mq}^{a}$ with an exponent $a>2$ and a constant depending only on $\kappa$ and the radius, neither made explicit, so it is weaker than quadratic near $m$.
    In the latter, the constant multiplies the variance rather than the excess, and only the center of the ball is admitted as a base point; its Section~1 notes that an arbitrary base point would require a diameter condition.
    Among the earlier results listed, only these two reach past $\delta=\pi/2$.
    \Cref{thm:pos:hemisphere:vi} is a variance inequality on the maximal range of radii (\cref{prp:pos:diameterobstruction}\,\ref{itm:pos:obstruction:boundary} in the appendix) with optimal constants, each optimal among constants depending on $\Delta$ alone, respectively on $\delta$ alone.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
\label{rem:pos:BrunelSerres2026}
    Theorems 3 and 4 of \cite{BrunelSerres2026FiniteSample-arxiv} bound the risk of the empirical $2$-Fr\'echet mean of $n$ observations in $\mathcal{C}$ by
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:bs}
        \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\bar m\hat m_n}^2 \right]\leq\frac{A_{\mathsf{BS}}}{n}\,\mathbf{V_{\!F}}\!\left[ Y_1 \right]
        \qquad\text{and}\qquad
        \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\bar m\hat m_n}^2 \right]\leq\frac{\tilde A_{\mathsf{BS}}}{n^2}\sum_{i=1}^n\mathbf{V_{\!F}}\!\left[ Y_i \right]
        \,,
    \end{equation}
    the first for iid observations, the second for independent observations with a common $2$-Fr\'echet mean.
    Both hold under $\varrho<\pi/(4\sqrt\kappa)$, half the range assumed in \cref{ssec:pos:prelim}, and their constants are
    \begin{equation*}
        A_{\mathsf{BS}}=\frac{16\pi\tan(\Delta)^2}{\Delta^2}
        \qquad\text{and}\qquad
        \tilde A_{\mathsf{BS}}=\frac{8\sqrt2\,\pi\tan(\Delta)^2}{\Delta^2}
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
    The bound \eqref{eq:pos:infty:independent} with $w_i=\frac1n\mathbf{1}_{\{i\leq n\}}$ and \cref{lmm:pos:variances} in the appendix give
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:openquestion}
        \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\bar m\hat m_n}^2 \right]
        \leq\frac{C_\Delta^4}{n^2}\sum_{i=1}^n\mathbf{V_{\!M}}\!\left[ Y_i \right]
        \leq\frac{2C_\Delta^4}{n^2}\sum_{i=1}^n\mathbf{V_{\!F}}\!\left[ Y_i \right]
        \,,
    \end{equation}
    that is, both inequalities of \eqref{eq:pos:bs} with the single constant $2C_\Delta^4$, without the requirement of a common $2$-Fr\'echet mean, on the full range $\varrho<\pi/(2\sqrt\kappa)$, and, by \cref{thm:pos:infty:independent} itself, for weighted and countable samples.
    Already the smaller of the two published constants exceeds ours by a factor of at least $4\sqrt2\,\pi\approx17.8$:
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{\tilde A_{\mathsf{BS}}}{2C_\Delta^4}
        =\frac{4\sqrt2\,\pi\sin(\Delta)^6}{\Delta^6\cos(\Delta)^2}
        \geq4\sqrt2\,\pi,
        \qquad 0<\Delta<\pi/2.
    \end{equation*}
    The ratio tends to $4\sqrt2\,\pi$ as $\Delta\searrow0$ and increases to infinity as $\Delta\nearrow\pi/2$, since its logarithmic derivative has the sign of $h(\Delta):=\Delta(1+2\cos(\Delta)^2)-3\sin(\Delta)\cos(\Delta)$, and $h(0)=0$, $h'(\Delta)=4\sin(\Delta)(\sin(\Delta)-\Delta\cos(\Delta))>0$.
    The main ingredients that allow for the improved result are the variance inequality \eqref{eq:2fmcatk:vi} of \cref{thm:pos:hemisphere:vi}, the quadruple inequality \eqref{eq:2fmcatk:qi} of \cite{Schoetz2026Trapezoid-arxiv}, and the contraction property \eqref{eq:2fmcatk:CP} of \cref{thm:pos:hemisphere:cp}, both theorems in the appendix.
    Theorem 1 of \cite{BrunelSerres2026FiniteSample-arxiv} makes the $n$-point empirical barycenter map $\frac Ln$-Lipschitz from $(\mathcal{C}^n,\ell^1)$ to $\mathcal{C}$ with $L=\pi\tan(\Delta)/(2\Delta)$, which is \eqref{eq:2fmcatk:CP} for pairs of uniform empirical measures on $n$ atoms, whereas \cref{thm:pos:hemisphere:cp} covers all $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$ with the optimal constant $B_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}$.
    On the full range, Yokota \cite[Proposition 24]{Yokota2016Barycenter-JMSJ} shows that $\mu\mapsto m_2(\mu)$ is Lipschitz continuous with respect to the $p$-Wasserstein distance for some $p>1$, with a constant depending only on $\kappa$ and the radius; neither $p$ nor the constant is made explicit.

    Open Question 2 in \cite[Section 4.2]{BrunelSerres2026FiniteSample-arxiv} asks (among other things) whether the constants in \eqref{eq:pos:bs} must diverge as $\varrho\nearrow\pi/(4\sqrt\kappa)$. Since $2C_\Delta^4\leq\pi^4/8$ on that range, \eqref{eq:pos:openquestion} answers this in the negative. At the larger threshold $\varrho\nearrow\pi/(2\sqrt\kappa)$, however, divergence \emph{is} necessary: \cref{prp:pos:diameterobstruction}\,\ref{itm:pos:obstruction:order} in the appendix gives a family of fixed diameter along which the smallest admissible coefficient is of order $(\pi/2-\varrho\sqrt\kappa)^{-2}$.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:pos:concentration}
 In the setting of \cref{ssec:pos:prelim}, let $(Y_i)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}$ be a sequence of independent random variables with laws in $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$, and let $(w_i)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq[0,1]$ be deterministic weights with $\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}w_i=1$.
 Define $\bar m,\hat m$ as in \cref{thm:pos:infty:independent}. For $\eta\in(0,1)$, with probability at least $1-\eta$,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:concentration}
 d(\hat m,\bar m)\leq C_\Delta^2\left( 
 \left( \sum_{i=1}^{\infty} w_i^2\mathbf{V_{\!M}}\!\left[ Y_i \right] \right)^{1/2}
 +\mathsf{diam}(\mathcal{C})\left( \frac{\left\Vertw\right\Vert_2^2}{2}\log\frac1\eta \right)^{1/2} \right).
 \end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:pos:localfrechet}
    In the setting of \cref{ssec:pos:prelim}, let $(\mathcal{X}, \rho)$ be a metric space.
    Let $\mu_z\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$, $z\in\mathcal{X}$, satisfy the transport smoothness condition \eqref{eq:two:localreg:smooth} of \cref{cor:two:localreg} for some $L>0$ and $\beta\in(0,1]$.
    Set $v := \sup_{z\in\mathcal{X}}\mathbf{V_{\!M}}(\mu_z)$.
    Let $Y_i\sim\mu_{x_i}$ be independent, and let the kernel and the estimator be as in \cref{ssec:localfrechet}.
    Let $x\in\mathcal{X}$, and assume that the design satisfies the condition \eqref{eq:localfrechet:design} of \cref{cor:two:localreg} for some $D \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}$ and $C_{\mathsf{dns}}\in [1,\infty)$.
    Then
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:localfrechet}
    \left( \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\hat m_h\!(x)\,m_2(\mu_x)}^2 \right] \right)^{1/2}
    \leq C_\Delta^2\left( L h^\beta+\sqrt{\frac{vC_{\mathsf{Kmi}}C_{\mathsf{Kma}}C_{\mathsf{dns}}}{nh^D}} \right)
    \,.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:power:main}
	In the setting above, there is a constant $c_\alpha$ depending only on $\alpha$ such that
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:rate:sharp}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\bar m\hat m}^\alpha \right]^{\frac1\alpha}
		\leq
		c_\alpha\left( 
			\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\, \Sigma_{2\alpha-2}^{\frac12}
			+
			\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\frac\phi2}\, \Sigma_{\alpha}^{\frac12}
			+
			\Sigma_\alpha^{\frac1\alpha}
		 \right)
		\,.
	\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor:power:rate}
	Let $\alpha\in(1,2]$ and $n\in\mathbb{N}$.
	Let $Y_1,\dots,Y_n$ be independent random variables with distributions $\mu_1,\dots,\mu_n\in\mathcal{P}^{\alpha-1}(\mathcal{Q})$, and set $\bar\mu := \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\mu_i$, $\bar m := m_\alpha(\bar\mu)$, $\hat\mu_n := \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n \delta_{Y_i}$, and $\hat m_n := m_\alpha(\hat\mu_n)$.
	For $s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}$, let
	\begin{equation*}
		\sigma_s := \sigma_\alpha^s(\bar\mu)
        \qquad\text{and}\qquad
        \bar V_s := \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n \mathbf{V}^{s}_{\!M}\!\left[ Y_i \right]
		\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Then, with a constant $c_\alpha$ depending only on $\alpha$, and with $\phi = (2-\alpha)/(\alpha-1)$, 
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:rate:iid}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\bar m\hat m_n}^\alpha \right]^{\frac1\alpha}
		\leq
		c_\alpha\left( 
			\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\, \bar V_{2\alpha-2}^{\frac12}\, n^{-\frac12}
			+
			\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\frac\phi2}\, \bar V_{\alpha}^{\frac12}\, n^{-\frac1{2(\alpha-1)}}
			+
			\bar V_\alpha^{\frac1\alpha}\, n^{-\frac1{\alpha(\alpha-1)}}
		 \right)
		\,.
	\end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
\label{rem:power:weights}
The schemes of \cref{rem:weights} also apply to power Fr\'echet means.
Consider iid observations with law $\mu$.
Then the two weighted moments in \cref{thm:power:main} are
\begin{equation*}
 \Sigma_{2\alpha-2}=\mathbf{V}^{2\alpha-2}_{\!M}\!\left( \mu \right)\left\Vertw\right\Vert_2^2,
 \qquad
 \Sigma_\alpha=\mathbf{V}^{\alpha}_{\!M}\!\left( \mu \right)\left\Vertw\right\Vert_{2+\phi}^{2+\phi}.
\end{equation*}
The first term of the bound \eqref{eq:power:rate:sharp} is proportional to $\Vertw\Vert_2$, while the other two involve the higher weight norm $\Vertw\Vert_{2+\phi}$.
For uniform weights, $\Vertw\Vert_2^2=n^{-1}$ and $\Vertw\Vert_{2+\phi}^{2+\phi}=n^{-1-\phi}$, giving \cref{cor:power:rate}.
For geometric weights $w_i=(1-\rho)\rho^{i-1}$ with $0<\rho<1$,
\[
 \left\Vertw\right\Vert_2^2=\frac{1-\rho}{1+\rho},\qquad
 \left\Vertw\right\Vert_{2+\phi}^{2+\phi}=\frac{(1-\rho)^{2+\phi}}{1-\rho^{2+\phi}}
 \in\left[ \frac{(1-\rho)^{1+\phi}}{2+\phi},(1-\rho)^{1+\phi} \right].
\]
For non-identically distributed observations, each component retains its own pairwise moment in $\Sigma_s$.
Set $w_{\max} := \sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}w_i$.
The bounds $\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}\leq2w_{\max}\sigma_{2\alpha-2}$ and $\Sigma_\alpha\leq2w_{\max}^{1+\phi}\sigma_\alpha$, proved at the start of \cref{app:power}, show $L^\alpha$ consistency when $w_{\max}\to0$ and the relevant mixture moments stay bounded, as in \cref{rem:weights}\,(iii).
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{cor:power:localfrechet}
 Let $(\mathcal{X},\rho)$ be a metric space, let $x\in\mathcal{X}$, and let $\mu_z\in\mathcal{P}^\alpha(\mathcal{Q})$, $z\in\mathcal{X}$.
 Suppose that, for some $L>0$ and $\beta\in(0,1]$, all $z\in\mathcal{X}\setminus\{x\}$ satisfy, with $b:=L\rho(z,x)^\beta$,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:localfrechet:smooth}
 \widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu_z,\mu_x)
 \leq b\,\Lambda_\alpha\!\left( \mu_x;b \right)
 \,.
 \end{equation}
 Set $u:=\sup_{z\in\mathcal{X}}\mathbf{V}^{\alpha}_{\!M}(\mu_z)$ and assume $u<\infty$.
 Let $Y_i\sim\mu_{x_i}$ be independent, let the kernel and weights be as in \cref{ssec:localfrechet}, and set
 $\hat m_h\!(x):=m_\alpha(\sum_iw_{i,h}(x)\delta_{Y_i})$.
 Assume that the design satisfies the condition \eqref{eq:localfrechet:design} of \cref{cor:two:localreg} at $x$ for some $D>0$ and $C_{\mathsf{dns}}\in[1,\infty)$.
 Then, with $c_\alpha$ depending only on $\alpha$,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:localfrechet}
 \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\hat m_h\!(x)m_\alpha(\mu_x)}^\alpha \right]^{1/\alpha}
 \leq c_\alpha\left( Lh^\beta+u^{1/\alpha}
       \sqrt{\frac{C_{\mathsf{Kmi}}C_{\mathsf{Kma}}C_{\mathsf{dns}}}{nh^D}} \right).
 \end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- remark-9: body_md -->
\label{rem:power:smoothness}
At $\alpha=2$ we have $\Lambda_\alpha(\mu;t)=1$ and $\widetilde W_1=W_1$, so \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:smooth} is the smoothness condition \eqref{eq:two:localreg:smooth} of \cref{cor:two:localreg,cor:pos:localfrechet} with $z^\prime=x$.

\emph{The right-hand side.} Condition \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:smooth} bounds the transport cost between $\mu_z$ and the target law $\mu_x$ by the modulus $b\Lambda_\alpha(\mu_x;b)$ of \eqref{eq:power:cp}\xspace at the desired distance scale $b$, and that modulus has two regimes.
It is at most $b^{\alpha-1}$ for every law, and at least $2^{-\phi}b^{\alpha-1}$ as soon as $b^{\alpha-1}\geq\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\mu_x)$, by Jensen's inequality (see \cref{app:power:moment}); thus, for $b$ beyond the spread of $\mu_x$, it is enough that $W_{\alpha-1}(\mu_z,\mu_x)\leq2^{-\phi}b^{\alpha-1}$, a H\"older condition in the plain cost, since $W_{\alpha-1}$ dominates $\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}$.
For small $b$, and assuming $\Lambda_\alpha(\mu_x;0)<\infty$ for simplicity --- a criterion is given in \cref{app:power:moment} --- the modulus is instead of order $b$.
The plain cost $W_{\alpha-1}$ can satisfy this linear bound for smooth families of densities, as in \cref{ex:power:hyperbolic}, but it excludes some elementary families of moving atoms that the location-aware cost admits.

\emph{The left-hand side.} The diameter factor in \eqref{eq:power:Wtilde} reduces the price of moving mass that sits far from the two means, as quantified in \cref{rem:power:diffuse} in the appendix.
For $1<\alpha<2$, replacing $\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}$ by $W_{\alpha-1}$ in \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:smooth} gives a stronger sufficient condition that can fail even for translations of a two-point law: in $\mathbb{R}$, let $\mu_x:=\frac12(\delta_{x-1}+\delta_{x+1})$.
For $x\neq x^\prime$ and $t:=\vertx-x^\prime\vert$, the coupling of corresponding atoms gives
\begin{equation*}
 \widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu_x,\mu_{x^\prime})
 \leq t(1+t)^{\alpha-2}=t\Lambda_\alpha\!\left( \mu_x;t \right),
 \qquad W_{\alpha-1}(\mu_x,\mu_{x^\prime})=t^{\alpha-1}\quad(t\leq1/2),
\end{equation*}
with equality in the first bound for $t\leq1/2$ (\cref{app:power:euclidean}).
Thus \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:smooth} holds at every $x$ with $L=1$ and $\beta=1$, whereas with $W_{\alpha-1}$ in place of $\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}$ it fails for every finite $L$ at $\beta=1$, since $t^{\alpha-1}/(Lt\Lambda_\alpha(\mu_x;Lt))\to\infty$ as $t\searrow0$.

\emph{A moment-only criterion.} Replacing the inverse moment by its Jensen lower bound turns \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:smooth} into a condition on the centered moments alone; \cref{lmm:power:sufficient}\,(i) in the appendix gives the Jensen sufficient form with the same $L$, while part\,(ii) gives a two-regime form with $L$ replaced by $2^{\phi/(\alpha-1)}L$.
The latter is the two-regime description above with the slope $\Lambda_\alpha(\mu_x;0)$ replaced by the smaller $\max\{\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\mu_z),\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\mu_x)\}^{-\phi}$.
It is convenient for verification, as in \cref{ex:power:hyperbolic}, but it can fail for additive noise with an atom at its mean (\cref{app:power:euclidean}).
<!-- end remark-9 -->
<!-- example-3: body_md -->
\label{ex:power:noiseless}
 If $\mu_x=\delta_{\gamma(x)}$ for a map $\gamma\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Q}$, then $\Lambda_\alpha(\mu_x;b)=b^{\alpha-2}$ and $\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu_x,\mu_{x^\prime})=\overline{\gamma(x)\gamma(x^\prime)}^{\alpha-1}$, so \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:smooth} is equivalent to $\overline{\gamma(x)\gamma(x^\prime)}\leq L\rho(x,x^\prime)^\beta$ for all $x^\prime\in\mathcal{X}$.
 Every $\beta$-H\"older response map is included; for real predictors, a unit-speed geodesic has $L=1$, $\beta=1$.
 Here $u=0$, the stochastic term vanishes, and \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet} is the deterministic approximation bound $c_\alpha Lh^\beta$; no noise--bias bandwidth balance is required.
<!-- end example-3 -->
<!-- corollary-6: body_md -->
\label{cor:power:additive}
 Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a Hilbert space, let $\mathcal{Q}\subseteq \mathcal{H}$ be nonempty, closed, and convex, with the induced metric, and let $\alpha\in(1,2]$.
 Let $\gamma\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Q}$ be $\beta$-H\"older with constant $L_\gamma$, and let $\varepsilon$ be an $\mathcal{H}$-valued random variable with $\mathbf{E}[\Vert\varepsilon\Vert^\alpha]<\infty$, with $\alpha$-Fr\'echet mean $m_\alpha[\varepsilon]=0$ in $\mathcal{H}$, and with $\gamma(x)+\varepsilon\in\mathcal{Q}$ almost surely for every $x\in\mathcal{X}$.
 For $\gamma(x)+\varepsilon\sim\mu_x$, we have $m_\alpha(\mu_x)=\gamma(x)$, $\mathbf{V}^{\alpha}_{\!M}(\mu_x)\leq2^{\alpha-1}\mathbf{E}[\Vert\varepsilon\Vert^\alpha]$, and \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:smooth} holds at every $x\in\mathcal{X}$ with $L=2^{\phi/2}L_\gamma$.
 Consequently, under the kernel and design assumptions of \cref{cor:power:localfrechet},
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:additive}
  \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\hat m_h\!(x)\gamma(x)}^\alpha \right]^{1/\alpha}
  \leq c_\alpha\left( L_\gamma h^\beta+\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \left\Vert\varepsilon\right\Vert^\alpha \right]^{1/\alpha}
  \sqrt{\frac{C_{\mathsf{Kmi}}C_{\mathsf{Kma}}C_{\mathsf{dns}}}{nh^D}} \right)
  \,.
 \end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-6 -->
<!-- example-4: body_md -->
\label{ex:power:wasserstein}
 Let $\mathcal{Q} = \mathcal{P}^2(\mathbb{R})$ be the set of probability measures on $\mathbb{R}$ with finite second moment, equipped with the quadratic Wasserstein distance
 \begin{equation*}
  \overline W_2(\mu, \nu) := \left( \inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\int\left| y-z \right|^2\,\mathrm{d}\pi(y,z) \right)^{\frac12}
  \,.
 \end{equation*}
 The quantile map $\nu\mapsto F_\nu^{-1}$ is an isometry of $\mathcal{Q}$ onto the closed convex cone of nondecreasing functions in $L^2(0,1)$ \cite{PanaretosZemel2020Wasserstein-Springer}, so \cref{cor:power:additive} applies with $\mathcal{H}=L^2(0,1)$.
 For instance, let $\gamma\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{P}^2(\mathbb{R})$ be $\beta$-H\"older and let $\xi$ be a real random variable with $m_\alpha[\xi]=0$ and $\mathbf{E}[\vert\xi\vert^\alpha]<\infty$.
 As response in $\mathcal{P}^2(\mathbb{R})$, take $Y_x:=\gamma(x)\ast\delta_\xi$, where $\ast$ denotes the convolution of measures and $\delta_\xi$ is the Dirac measure at $\xi$.
 Convolution with a Dirac measure is a translation, $Y_x(B)=\gamma(x)(B-\xi)$ for Borel sets $B\subseteq\mathbb{R}$, so $Y_x$ is the law $\gamma(x)$ shifted by the random amount $\xi$; equivalently, $Y_x$ is the conditional law of $Z+\xi$ given $\xi$, where $Z\sim\gamma(x)$ is independent of $\xi$.
 Shifting a measure by $\xi$ shifts its quantile function by $\xi$, i.e., $F_{Y_x}^{-1}=F_{\gamma(x)}^{-1}+\xi$ on $(0,1)$.
 In $L^2(0,1)$, the response therefore has the additive form $Y_x=\gamma(x)+\varepsilon$ with the constant function $\varepsilon:=\xi\mathbf{1}_{(0,1)}$; the sum $F_{\gamma(x)}^{-1}+\xi$ is nondecreasing and square integrable, so it lies in the cone of $L^2(0,1)$ identified with $\mathcal{P}^2(\mathbb{R})$.
 The $L^2(0,1)$ norm of a constant function is its absolute value, so $\Vert\varepsilon\Vert=\vert\xi\vert$ and $\mathbf{E}[\Vert\varepsilon\Vert^\alpha]=\mathbf{E}[\vert\xi\vert^\alpha]<\infty$.
 It remains to check $m_\alpha[\varepsilon]=0$.
 Write $f\in L^2(0,1)$ as $f=c\mathbf{1}_{(0,1)}+f^\perp$ with $c\in\mathbb{R}$ and $f^\perp$ orthogonal to the constant functions.
 Then $\Vert\varepsilon-f\Vert^2=(\xi-c)^2+\Vertf^\perp\Vert^2$, so that $\mathbf{E}[\Vert\varepsilon-f\Vert^\alpha]\geq\mathbf{E}[\vert\xi-c\vert^\alpha]$ with equality if and only if $f^\perp=0$.
 Minimizing over $c$ gives $m_\alpha[\varepsilon]=m_\alpha[\xi]\mathbf{1}_{(0,1)}=0$.
 Hence, \cref{cor:power:additive} applies with $\mathbf{E}[\Vert\varepsilon\Vert^\alpha]=\mathbf{E}[\vert\xi\vert^\alpha]$.
 More generally, $\varepsilon$ may be any random element of $L^2(0,1)$ with $\mathbf{E}[\Vert\varepsilon\Vert^\alpha]<\infty$ and $m_\alpha[\varepsilon]=0$ (for instance, symmetric) that keeps $F_{\gamma(x)}^{-1}+\varepsilon$ nondecreasing, e.g., a $\lambda$-Lipschitz perturbation when every $F_{\gamma(x)}^{-1}$ increases with slope at least $\lambda$.
 For $1<\alpha<2$ and $\mathbf{E}[\xi^2]=\infty$, the responses have infinite second moment in $\mathcal{Q}$, and \eqref{eq:power:additive} still gives the rate $n^{-\beta/(2\beta+D)}$; recall from \cref{ssec:localfrechet} that the entropy condition of \cite{PetersenMueller2019Regression-AOS} fails in this space \cite{ImEtAl2026Entropy-arxiv}, and note that the projection route of \cite{PetersenMueller2026Correction-AOS} is tied to $\alpha=2$.
<!-- end example-4 -->
<!-- example-5: body_md -->
\label{ex:power:hyperbolic}
 Let $\mathcal{Q}=\mathbb H^k$, $k\geq2$, be the hyperbolic space of dimension $k$ and constant curvature $-1$, a Hadamard manifold, in its hyperboloid model \cite[Chapter~I.2]{BridsonHaefliger1999NPC-Springer}: with the Minkowski form $\langle p,q\rangle_{1,k}:=-p_0q_0+\sum_{j=1}^kp_jq_j$ on $\mathbb{R}^{k+1}$,
 \begin{equation*}
  \mathbb H^k:=\left\lbrace p\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}\colon\langle p,p\rangle_{1,k}=-1,\ p_0>0 \right\rbrace
  \,,\qquad
  d(p,q):=\operatorname{arcosh}\!\left( -\langle p,q\rangle_{1,k} \right)
  \,.
 \end{equation*}
 The tangent space at $p\in\mathbb H^k$ is $T_p\mathbb H^k:=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}\colon\langle p,v\rangle_{1,k}=0\}$.
 The Minkowski form is positive definite on $T_p\mathbb H^k$, so $(T_p\mathbb H^k,\langle \cdot,\cdot\rangle_{1,k})$ is a $k$-dimensional Euclidean space with norm $\Vertv\Vert:=\langle v,v\rangle_{1,k}^{1/2}$.
 Hence, a linear isometry $I\colon\mathbb{R}^k\to T_p\mathbb H^k$ exists for every $p$; it amounts to choosing an orthonormal basis of $T_p\mathbb H^k$.
 At $o:=(1,0,\dots,0)$, for instance, $T_o\mathbb H^k=\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^k$ and $I(z):=(0,z)$ is a linear isometry.
 The exponential map $\exp_p\colon T_p\mathbb H^k\to\mathbb H^k$ sends $v\neq0$ to the point at distance $\Vertv\Vert$ from $p$ on the geodesic ray from $p$ in direction $v$, and $0$ to $p$; explicitly,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:hyperbolic:exp}
  \exp_p(v)=\cosh\!\left( \left\Vertv\right\Vert \right)\,p+\frac{\sinh\!\left( \left\Vertv\right\Vert \right)}{\left\Vertv\right\Vert}\,v
  \,,
 \end{equation}
 with the quotient read as $1$ at $v=0$.
 It is a diffeomorphism onto $\mathbb H^k$, and $d(\exp_p(v),p)=\Vertv\Vert$ because $\langle p,\exp_p(v)\rangle_{1,k}=-\cosh(\Vertv\Vert)$.
 Let $\alpha\in(1,2)$ and choose $\theta\in(\alpha,2)$.
 Let $Z$ have the spherically symmetric Student distribution in $\mathbb{R}^k$ with $\theta$ degrees of freedom.
 Let $\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb H^k$ and $\zeta\colon[0,1]\to[\zeta_-,\zeta_+]$ be arbitrary $\beta$-H\"older functions, with $\beta\in(0,1]$ and $0<\zeta_-\leq\zeta_+<\infty$; for instance, $\gamma=\exp_o\circ\gamma_0$ for a $\beta$-H\"older function $\gamma_0\colon[0,1]\to T_o\mathbb H^k$, see \cref{app:power:hyperbolic}.
 For every $x\in[0,1]$, fix a linear isometry $I_x\colon\mathbb{R}^k\to T_{\gamma(x)}\mathbb H^k$ and set
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:hyperbolic:model}
  Y_x:=\exp_{\gamma(x)}\!\left( \zeta(x)I_xZ \right)\sim\mu_x\,.
 \end{equation}
 This is the exponential-wrapped construction of \cite{NaganoEtAl2019WrappedNormal-ICML}, where its geometric change of variables is described; by spherical symmetry of $Z$, the law $\mu_x$ does not depend on the choice of $I_x$.
 Rotational symmetry gives $m_\alpha(\mu_x)=\gamma(x)$, and
 $\overline{Y_x\gamma(x)}=\zeta(x)\VertZ\Vert_2$.
 The conditional $\alpha$-moments are uniformly finite, every conditional second moment is infinite, and \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:smooth} holds at every $x\in[0,1]$ with this $\beta$; see \cref{app:power:hyperbolic}.
 Consequently, under the kernel and design assumptions with $D=1$, the $L^\alpha$ risk is $\operatorname{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-\beta/(2\beta+1)})$ for $h\asymp n^{-1/(2\beta+1)}$ as $n\to\infty$.
<!-- end example-5 -->
<!-- corollary-7: body_md -->
\label{cor:power:dirichlet}
 Let $\alpha\in(1,2]$, $a>0$, $\mu\in\mathcal{P}^\alpha(\mathcal{Q})$, and $P\sim\operatorname{DP}(a,\mu)$.
 Then $m_\alpha(P)$ exists almost surely, and, with constants $c_\alpha$ depending only on $\alpha$,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:dirichlet:prior}
 \left( \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{m_\alpha(P)m_\alpha(\mu)}^\alpha \right] \right)^{1/\alpha}
 \leq c_\alpha\mathcal R_\alpha(a,\mu).
 \end{equation}
 If $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ are conditionally iid with law $P$, and $\tilde\mu_n$ is the posterior base measure of \cref{ssec:dirichlet}, then almost surely
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:dirichlet:posterior}
 \left( \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{m_\alpha(P)m_\alpha(\tilde\mu_n)}^\alpha
       \,\middle|\,X_1,\ldots,X_n \right] \right)^{1/\alpha}
 \leq c_\alpha\mathcal R_\alpha(a+n,\tilde\mu_n).
 \end{equation}
 Averaging over the observations under this model gives the simpler bound
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:dirichlet:bayes}
 \left( \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{m_\alpha(P)m_\alpha(\tilde\mu_n)}^\alpha \right] \right)^{1/\alpha}
 \leq c_\alpha\left( \frac{a\,(a+n+1)}{(a+1)(a+n)}\mathbf{V}^{\alpha}_{\!M}\!\left( \mu \right) \right)^{1/\alpha}\frac1{\sqrt{a+n+1}}.
 \end{equation}
 The same bound with $n=0$ holds for the prior, with $\tilde\mu_0:=\mu$.
<!-- end corollary-7 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prp:sphere:hemisphere:vi}
    Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a real Hilbert space and equip its unit sphere
    $\mathcal{Q}:=\{u\in\mathcal{H}:\Vertu\Vert=1\}$ with the spherical distance
    $d(u,v):=\arccos(\left\langleu,v\right\rangle)$.
    Let $\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}$ be nonempty, closed, and geodesically convex, and suppose that
    $\mathcal{C}\subseteq B(o,R)$ for some $o\in\mathcal{Q}$ and $0<R<\pi/2$.
    Define
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:sphere:vi:constant}
        A_{2R}^{\mathrm{rad}}:=\max_{0\leq x\leq R}
        \frac{(R+x)^2}{
        \arccos\!\left( \frac{\cos(R)\cos(R+x)}{\cos(x)} \right)^2
        -\arccos\!\left( \frac{\cos(R)}{\cos(x)} \right)^2}
        \,.
    \end{equation}
    Then $A_{2R}^{\mathrm{rad}} \in [1,\infty)$ and, for every $\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$ and $q\in\mathcal{C}$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:sphere:vi:radius}
        \overline{m_2(\mu)q}^2
        \leq A_{2R}^{\mathrm{rad}}
        \int\left( \overline{yq}^2-\overline{ym_2(\mu)}^2 \right)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
        \,.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Put $m:=m_2(\mu)$ and $F(q):=\int\overline{yq}^2\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)$.
    By \cite[Theorem B and Corollary 16]{Yokota2016Barycenter-JMSJ}, $m$ is the unique mean in the whole sphere and belongs to $\mathcal{C}$.
    Use $g(v)=\arccos(v)^2$ and
    \begin{equation*}
        H(v)=\frac{\sin(\arccos(v))}{\arccos(v)}
        =\frac{\sqrt{1-v^2}}{\arccos(v)}
        \qquad(-1<v<1)\,,\qquad H(1)=1
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    from \cref{lmm:pos:cosine-average}.
    Since $\overline{ym}\leq2R<\pi$, all weights
    $1/H(\left\langley,m\right\rangle)=\overline{ym}/\sqrt{1-\left\langley,m\right\rangle^2}$ below are bounded, with value $1$ at $y=m$.
    Define the normalizing constant $U$ and the probability measure $\lambda$ by
    \begin{equation*}
        U:=\int\frac1{H(\left\langley,m\right\rangle)}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
        \,,\qquad
        \mathrm{d}\lambda(y):=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu(y)}{UH(\left\langley,m\right\rangle)}
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Thus $\mathrm{d}\mu(y)=UH(\left\langley,m\right\rangle)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y)$, and the change of measure gives
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:sphere:vi:change}
        \begin{split}
            F(q)-F(m)
            &=\int\left( g(\left\langley,q\right\rangle)-g(\left\langley,m\right\rangle) \right)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\\
            &=U\int H(\left\langley,m\right\rangle)\left( g(\left\langley,q\right\rangle)-g(\left\langley,m\right\rangle) \right)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y)
            \,.
        \end{split}
    \end{equation}
    Applying the joint Jensen inequality of \cref{lmm:pos:cosine-conversion}\,(i) now yields
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:sphere:vi:jensen}
        \begin{split}
            F(q)-F(m)
            &\geq U H\!\left( \int\left\langley,m\right\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y) \right)\\
            &\qquad{}\times\left( g\!\left( \int\left\langley,q\right\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y) \right)
            -g\!\left( \int\left\langley,m\right\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y) \right) \right)
            \,.
        \end{split}
    \end{equation}

    To evaluate these averages, we use the minimizing property of $m$.
    Put
    \begin{equation*}
        K:=\int\frac{\left\langley,m\right\rangle}{H(\left\langley,m\right\rangle)}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
        \,,\qquad z:=\frac KU
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
    For any unit vector $h\in\mathcal{H}$ with $\left\langlem,h\right\rangle=0$, the great circle
    $\gamma(s):=\cos(s)m+\sin(s)h$ lies on the sphere and satisfies $\gamma(0)=m$ and $\gamma'(0)=h$.
    Since $m$ minimizes $F$ on the whole sphere, the derivative of $F\circ\gamma$ at zero vanishes.
    The bound $\overline{y\gamma(s)}\leq2R+|s|<\pi$ for small $|s|$ justifies differentiation under the integral.
    Using $g'=-2/H$ gives
    \begin{equation*}
        0=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s}F(\gamma(s))\right|_{s=0}
        =-2\int\frac{\left\langley,h\right\rangle}{H(\left\langley,m\right\rangle)}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Thus $\int y/H(\left\langley,m\right\rangle)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)$ is orthogonal to every tangent direction $h\perp m$, so it is a multiple of $m$.
    Taking its inner product with $m$ identifies that multiple as $K$, and hence
    \begin{equation*}
        \int\frac{y}{H(\left\langley,m\right\rangle)}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)=Km
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Taking the Hilbert inner product with $p\in\mathcal{Q}$ yields the identity
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:sphere:vi:comparison}
        \int\frac{\left\langley,p\right\rangle}{H(\left\langley,m\right\rangle)}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
        =K\left\langlem,p\right\rangle
        \,.
    \end{equation}
    By the definition of $z$ and \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:comparison},
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:sphere:vi:averages}
        \int\left\langley,m\right\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y)=z
        \,,\qquad
        \int\left\langley,q\right\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y)=z\left\langlem,q\right\rangle
        \,.
    \end{equation}
    At $p=o$, \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:comparison} implies $\cos(R)U\leq\left\langlem,o\right\rangle\,K$.
    Thus, using $\overline{mo}\geq(\overline{mq}-R)_+$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:sphere:vi:z}
        z\geq\frac{\cos(R)}{\left\langlem,o\right\rangle}
        \geq\frac{\cos(R)}{\cos((\overline{mq}-R)_+)}\geq\cos(R)>0
        \,.
    \end{equation}
    In particular, $\int y\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y)=zm$, so $z\in(0,1]$ is the Hilbert norm of this weighted average.
    Concavity of $H$ gives
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:sphere:vi:concavity}
        1=U\int H(\left\langley,m\right\rangle)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y)\leq UH(z)
        \,.
    \end{equation}
    Substituting \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:averages} into \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:jensen} and using \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:concavity}, we obtain
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:sphere:vi:nonlinear}
        \begin{split}
            F(q)-F(m)
            &\geq UH(z)\left( g(z\left\langlem,q\right\rangle)-g(z) \right)\\
            &\geq g(z\left\langlem,q\right\rangle)-g(z)
            \,,
        \end{split}
    \end{equation}
    where $g(z\left\langlem,q\right\rangle)-g(z)\geq0$ because $z\left\langlem,q\right\rangle\leq z$ and $g$ is decreasing.
    For $-1<a\leq1$, the function $z\mapsto g(az)-g(z)$ is nondecreasing on $(0,1]$:
    its derivative is $2/H(z)-2a/H(az)\geq0$, because for $a\geq0$ concavity of $H$ and $H(0)=2/\pi>0$ give $H(az)\geq aH(z)+(1-a)H(0)\geq aH(z)$, and for $a<0$ the sign is immediate.
    Substitution of \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:z} into \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:nonlinear} therefore yields
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:sphere:vi:master}
        F(q)-F(m)
        \geq g\!\left( \frac{\cos(R)\left\langlem,q\right\rangle}{\cos((\overline{mq}-R)_+)} \right)
        -g\!\left( \frac{\cos(R)}{\cos((\overline{mq}-R)_+)} \right)
        \,.
    \end{equation}

    To obtain \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:radius}, the case $q=m$ is immediate.
    For $0\leq x\leq R$, write
    \begin{equation*}
        E_{2R}(x):=g\!\left( \frac{\cos(R)\cos(R+x)}{\cos(x)} \right)
        -g\!\left( \frac{\cos(R)}{\cos(x)} \right)
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
    This is continuous and positive on $[0,R]$, since $g$ is decreasing and $\cos(R+x)<1$, so the maximum in \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:constant} is finite.
    At $x=R$, we have $E_{2R}(R)=4R^2$, so $A_{2R}^{\mathrm{rad}}\geq1$.
    If $0<\overline{mq}\leq R$, \cref{lmm:pos:cosine-conversion}\,(ii) gives
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{F(q)-F(m)}{\overline{mq}^2}
        \geq\frac{g(\cos(R)\left\langlem,q\right\rangle)-g(\cos(R))}{\overline{mq}^2}
        \geq\frac{E_{2R}(0)}{R^2}
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
    If $R\leq\overline{mq}\leq2R$, put $x:=\overline{mq}-R$.
    Then \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:master} reads
    $F(q)-F(m)\geq E_{2R}(x)$.
    The definition \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:constant} now gives \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:radius} in both ranges.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:pos:hemisphere:vi}
    Let $\kappa\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}$ and let $(\mathcal{Q},d)$ be a complete $\mathsf{CAT}(\kappa)${} space.
    Let $\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}$ be a nonempty bounded closed convex set, and let $\varrho:=\inf_{o\in\mathcal{Q}}\sup_{y\in\mathcal{C}}d(o,y)$ be its circumradius.
    Set $\Delta := 2\varrho\sqrt{\kappa}$.
    Suppose $0<\Delta<\pi$.
    
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item
        Define, for $0\leq x\leq\Delta/2$,
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:vi:radius:functions}
            E_\Delta(x):=
            \arccos\!\left( \cos(\Delta/2)^2-\cos(\Delta/2)\sin(\Delta/2)\tan(x) \right)^2
            -\arccos\!\left( \frac{\cos(\Delta/2)}{\cos(x)} \right)^2
            \,.
        \end{equation}
        There is a unique $x_\Delta\in(0,\Delta/2)$ satisfying
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:vi:radius:root}
            (\Delta/2+x_\Delta)E_\Delta'(x_\Delta)=2E_\Delta(x_\Delta).
        \end{equation}
        Set
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:vi:radius:optimal}
            A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}:=\frac{(\Delta/2+x_\Delta)^2}{E_\Delta(x_\Delta)}
            =\max_{0\leq x\leq\Delta/2}\frac{(\Delta/2+x)^2}{E_\Delta(x)}.
        \end{equation}
        Then, for every $\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$ and $q\in\mathcal{C}$,
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:vi}
            \overline{m_2(\mu)q}^2
            \leq
            A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}
            \int\left( \overline{yq}^2-\overline{ym_2(\mu)}^2 \right)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
            \,.
        \end{equation}
        The constant is optimal among constants depending only on $\Delta$, uniformly over all such spaces, sets, and measures, for each positive value of $\Delta$.
        Moreover,
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:vi:radius:upper}
            A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}<\frac1{\cos(\Delta/2)}<C_\Delta
            \,,
        \end{equation}
        so \eqref{eq:2fmcatk:vi} follows.
        \item 
        On the smaller and mean-dependent range $\overline{m_2(\mu)q}\leq\varrho$, the optimal radius-only constant is explicit and smaller. Writing
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:vi:near:constant}
            A_\Delta^{\mathrm{near}}:=\frac{(\Delta/2)^2}{E_\Delta(0)}
            =\frac{(\Delta/2)^2}{\arccos\!\left( \cos(\Delta/2)^2 \right)^2-(\Delta/2)^2}
            \,,
        \end{equation}
        one has $1\leq A_\Delta^{\mathrm{near}}<A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}$ and
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:vi:near}
            \overline{m_2(\mu)q}^2
            \leq
            A_\Delta^{\mathrm{near}}
            \int\left( \overline{yq}^2-\overline{ym_2(\mu)}^2 \right)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
            \qquad\text{whenever}\quad\overline{m_2(\mu)q}\leq\varrho
            \,,
        \end{equation}
        and $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{near}}$ is optimal among constants depending only on $\Delta$ on that range.
        \item 
        For diameter alone, set $\delta:=\sqrt\kappa\,\mathsf{diam}(\mathcal{C})$ and define
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:vi:constants}
            A_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}:=
            \frac{\theta_\delta^2}{\delta^2-\theta_\delta^2},
            \qquad
            \theta_\delta:=\arccos\!\left( \sqrt{\cos(\delta)} \right)
            \quad(0<\delta<\pi/2).
        \end{equation}
        Set $A_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}:=+\infty$ for $\pi/2\leq\delta<\pi$.
        Then, for $0<\delta<\pi/2$, every $\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$ and $q\in\mathcal{C}$ satisfy
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:vi:diameter}
            \overline{m_2(\mu)q}^2
            \leq
            A_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}
            \int\left( \overline{yq}^2-\overline{ym_2(\mu)}^2 \right)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
            \,.
        \end{equation}
        The constant $A_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}$ is optimal among constants depending only on $\delta$, uniformly over all such spaces, sets, and measures, for each positive value of $\delta$.
        Moreover, no finite diameter-only constant exists at any fixed $\pi/2\leq\delta<\pi$ (\cref{prp:pos:diameterobstruction}\,\ref{itm:pos:obstruction:vi}).
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Rescaling the metric by $\sqrt\kappa$ multiplies the squared distances on both sides of the desired inequality by $\kappa$.
    We may therefore assume $\kappa=1$, so that $\Delta=2\varrho$.
    Fix any $R\in(\varrho,\pi/2)$ and choose $o\in\mathcal{Q}$ such that $\mathcal{C}\subseteq B(o,R)$.
    We first prove the bounds with parameter $2R$ and then let $R\searrow\varrho$ to recover the parameter $\Delta=2\varrho$.
    Put $m:=m_2(\mu)$ and $F(q):=\int\overline{yq}^2\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)$.
    Existence, uniqueness, and $m\in\mathcal{C}\subseteq B(o,R)$ follow from \cite[Theorem B and Corollary 16]{Yokota2016Barycenter-JMSJ}.

    \textbf{Part 1: Nonlinear lower bound.}

    \emph{Change of measure and Jensen.}
    Use $g=\arccos^2$ and $H$ from \cref{lmm:pos:cosine-average}, and define
    \begin{equation*}
        U:=\int\frac1{H(\cos(\overline{ym}))}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
        \,,\qquad
        \mathrm{d}\lambda(y):=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu(y)}{UH(\cos(\overline{ym}))}
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Here $1/H(\cos(\overline{ym}))=\overline{ym}/\sin(\overline{ym})$ is bounded because $\overline{ym}\leq2R<\pi$, with value $1$ at $y=m$.
    The change of measure and joint Jensen steps \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:change}--\eqref{eq:sphere:vi:jensen} apply with each inner product replaced by the cosine of the corresponding distance.
    They give
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:curved:jensen}
        \begin{split}
            F(q)-F(m)
            &\geq UH\!\left( \int\cos(\overline{ym})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y) \right)\\
            &\qquad{}\times\left( g\!\left( \int\cos(\overline{yq})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y) \right)
            -g\!\left( \int\cos(\overline{ym})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y) \right) \right)
            \,.
        \end{split}
    \end{equation}

    \emph{Curved comparison.}
    To bound these averages, put
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:curved:AU}
        K:=\int\frac{\cos(\overline{ym})}{H(\cos(\overline{ym}))}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
        \,,\qquad z:=\frac KU
        \,.
    \end{equation}
    In place of the spherical identity \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:comparison}, \cref{lmm:pos:curved:foc} (a version of \cite[Proposition 23]{Yokota2016Barycenter-JMSJ}) gives, for every $p\in\overline B(o,R)$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:curved:comparison}
        \int\frac{\cos(\overline{yp})}{H(\cos(\overline{ym}))}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
        \leq K\cos(\overline{mp})
        \,.
    \end{equation}
    Thus the averages in \eqref{eq:pos:curved:jensen} satisfy
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:curved:averages}
        \int\cos(\overline{ym})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y)=z
        \,,\qquad
        \int\cos(\overline{yq})\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y)\leq z\cos(\overline{mq})
        \,.
    \end{equation}
    At $p=o$, \eqref{eq:pos:curved:comparison} gives $\cos(R)U\leq K\cos(\overline{mo})$.
    The enclosing-ball argument of \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:z} therefore applies unchanged and yields
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:curved:z}
        z\geq\frac{\cos(R)}{\cos(\overline{mo})}
        \geq\frac{\cos(R)}{\cos((\overline{mq}-R)_+)}\geq\cos(R)>0
        \,.
    \end{equation}
    Also $z\leq1$ by the first identity in \eqref{eq:pos:curved:averages}.
    The concavity argument of \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:concavity} still gives $UH(z)\geq1$.
    Since $g$ is decreasing, the inequality for the second average in \eqref{eq:pos:curved:averages} has the required direction in \eqref{eq:pos:curved:jensen}.
    As in \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:nonlinear}, we conclude that
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:curved:nonlinear}
        \begin{split}
            F(q)-F(m)
            &\geq UH(z)\left( g(z\cos(\overline{mq}))-g(z) \right)\\
            &\geq g(z\cos(\overline{mq}))-g(z)
            \,.
        \end{split}
    \end{equation}

    \textbf{Part 2: Optimal radius constant.}

    The identity
    $\cos(R)\cos(R+x)/\cos(x)=\cos(R)^2-\cos(R)\sin(R)\tan(x)$
    identifies $E_{2R}$ from \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:functions} with the function used in the proof of \cref{prp:sphere:hemisphere:vi}.
    The monotonicity in $z$ and the two distance ranges in that proof apply unchanged to \eqref{eq:pos:curved:nonlinear}, using \eqref{eq:pos:curved:z}.
    They give
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:vi:radius:near}
        F(q)-F(m)\geq\frac{E_{2R}(0)}{R^2}\overline{mq}^2
        \qquad\text{if }\overline{mq}\leq R
        \,.
    \end{equation}
    Both ranges, including the immediate case $q=m$, are covered by
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:vi:radius:enclosing}
        \overline{mq}^2\leq A_{2R}^{\mathrm{rad}}\left( F(q)-F(m) \right)
        \,,\qquad
        A_{2R}^{\mathrm{rad}}=\max_{0\leq x\leq R}\frac{(R+x)^2}{E_{2R}(x)}
        \,,
    \end{equation}
    with the same finite maximum as in \eqref{eq:sphere:vi:constant}.
    After the substitution $x=Ru$, the maximum is taken over the fixed interval $u\in[0,1]$, and the maximum theorem shows that $R\mapsto A_{2R}^{\mathrm{rad}}$ is continuous.
    Letting $R\searrow\varrho$ in \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:enclosing} therefore gives $\overline{mq}^2\leq A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}(F(q)-F(m))$, which is \eqref{eq:pos:vi}.
    If $\overline{mq}\leq\varrho$, then $q$ lies in the first range for every $R>\varrho$.
    Letting $R\searrow\varrho$ in \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:near}, and using the continuity of $R^2/E_{2R}(0)$, gives \eqref{eq:pos:vi:near}.
    Finally, \cref{lmm:pos:vi:root} with parameter $\Delta$ gives the unique-root characterization \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:root} and the maximum formula \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:optimal}.
    
    \textbf{Part 3: Optimal diameter constant.}

    Suppose $0<\delta<\pi/2$; the case $q=m$ is immediate.
    Integrate \eqref{eq:pos:curved:comparison} with $p=r$ against $\frac{\overline{rm}}{\sin(\overline{rm})}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(r)$.
    Since $\cos(\overline{yr})\geq \cos(\delta)$ for $y,r\in\mathcal{C}$, this gives $\cos(\delta)U^2\leq K^2$.
    The same pointwise bound in \eqref{eq:pos:curved:comparison} gives $\cos(\delta)U\leq\cos(\overline{mq})K$ for each $q\in\mathcal{C}$.
    Consequently,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:curved:diameter}
        z\geq b(\overline{mq}):=\max\{\sqrt{\cos(\delta)},\cos(\delta)/\cos(\overline{mq})\}.
    \end{equation}
    By the monotonicity in $z$ used in Part 2, we may replace $z$ in \eqref{eq:pos:curved:nonlinear} by $b(\overline{mq})$.

    For $0<\overline{mq}\leq\theta_\delta$, we have $b(\overline{mq})=\cos(\theta_\delta)$.
    By \cref{lmm:pos:cosine-conversion}\,(ii),
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{g(b(\overline{mq})\cos(\overline{mq}))-g(b(\overline{mq}))}{\overline{mq}^2}
        \geq\frac{g(\cos(\theta_\delta)^2)-\theta_\delta^2}{\theta_\delta^2}
        =\frac{\delta^2-\theta_\delta^2}{\theta_\delta^2}.
    \end{equation*}
    For $\theta_\delta\leq\overline{mq}\leq\delta$, put $t:=\arccos(\cos(\delta)/\cos(\overline{mq}))\leq\overline{mq}$, regarded as a function of $\overline{mq}$.
    The coefficient is now $(\delta^2-t^2)/\overline{mq}^2$, and it is nondecreasing in $\overline{mq}$.
    Indeed, in the interior of this interval, with $u=t^2$ and $v=\overline{mq}^2$,
    \begin{equation*}
        -\frac{\mathrm{d} u}{\mathrm{d} v}=\frac{t\cot(t)}{\overline{mq}\cot(\overline{mq})}\geq1
        \qquad\text{and}\qquad
        \frac{\delta^2-u}{v}\leq1
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
    The first bound follows from $t\leq\overline{mq}$ and the monotonicity of $s\mapsto s\cot(s)$ in \cref{lmm:pos:cosine-average}\,(ii).
    The second follows from \cref{lmm:pos:cosine-conversion}\,(ii), since $\cos(t)\cos(\overline{mq})=\cos(\delta)$.
    Differentiating $(\delta^2-u)/v$ with respect to $v$ shows that its derivative is nonnegative precisely when $-\,\mathrm{d} u/\mathrm{d} v\geq(\delta^2-u)/v$, which follows from the two displayed inequalities.
    By continuity at the endpoints, its minimum therefore again occurs at $\overline{mq}=\theta_\delta$.
    This proves \eqref{eq:pos:vi:diameter}.

    \textbf{Part 4: Sharpness and bounds on the constants.}
    
    Both $\Delta$ and $\delta$ are invariant under rescaling, so examples with $\kappa=1$ suffice.
    \Cref{lmm:pos:vi:sharp:radius} realizes, in a spherical ball of circumradius exactly $\Delta/2$, the ratio $(\Delta/2+x)^2/E_\Delta(x)$ at every $x\in[0,\Delta/2]$, with $\overline{mq}=\Delta/2+x$.
    Taking $x=x_\Delta$ attains $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}$ and proves radius-only optimality.
    Taking $x=0$ gives $\overline{mq}=\Delta/2=\varrho$ and attains $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{near}}$, proving optimality on the restricted range $\overline{mq}\leq\varrho$.
    
    The proof of \cref{thm:pos:hemisphere:cp} uses two further consequences of Part~1. Convexity of $g$ and $g'=-2/H$ applied to the first inequality in \eqref{eq:pos:curved:nonlinear} give
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:curved:intermediate}
        \begin{split}
            F(q)-F(m)
            &\geq UH(z)g'(z)z\left( \cos(\overline{mq})-1 \right)\\
            &=2K\left( 1-\cos(\overline{mq}) \right)
            \,.
        \end{split}
    \end{equation}
    Set $t:=\arccos(\cos(R)/\cos(\overline{mo}))\in[0,R]$, with $t\cot(t)=1$ at $t=0$.
    Since $z\geq\cos(t)$ by \eqref{eq:pos:curved:z}, \cref{lmm:pos:cosine-average}\,(iii), applied to $X=\cos(\overline{Ym})$ with $Y\sim\mu$, gives
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:curved:A-lower}
        K=Uz\geq\frac z{H(z)}\geq t\cot(t)>0
        \,.
    \end{equation}

    For the strict upper bound on $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}$, let $x\in[0,\Delta/2]$, put $u_x:=(\Delta/2+x)/2$, and let $t_x:=t_\Delta(x)$ be as in \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:ts}. Then $\cos(t_x)\cos(2u_x)$ is the argument of the first arccosine in \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:functions}, so $E_\Delta(x)=g(\cos(t_x)\cos(2u_x))-g(\cos(t_x))$.
    Convexity of $g$ and $g'(\cos(t_x))=-2C_{t_x}$ (\cref{lmm:pos:cosine-average}\,(i)) give
    \begin{equation*}
        E_\Delta(x)
        \geq2t_x\cot(t_x)\left( 1-\cos(2u_x) \right)
        =t_x\cot(t_x)\left( \frac{\sin(u_x)}{u_x} \right)^2\left( \frac\Delta2+x \right)^2
        >\cos(\Delta/2)\left( \frac\Delta2+x \right)^2
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    with $t_x\cot(t_x)=1$ if $t_x=0$, where the last inequality is the strict form of \cref{lmm:pos:radius-scalar} with $a=x$ and $\Delta/2>0$ in place of $R$.
    Hence the maximand in \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:optimal} is less than $1/\cos(\Delta/2)$ on the compact interval $[0,\Delta/2]$, so $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}<1/\cos(\Delta/2)$, and $\sin(\Delta/2)<\Delta/2$ turns this into \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:upper}.
    Finally $E_\Delta(\Delta/2)=\Delta^2$ by \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:functions}, so the maximand in \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:optimal} equals $1$ there and $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}\geq1$.
    Together with $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}<1/\cos(\Delta/2)$, this gives its continuous extension with value $1$ at $\Delta=0$.
    For $R=\Delta/2$, \cref{lmm:pos:cosine-conversion}\,(ii) gives $E_\Delta(0)=g(\cos(R)^2)-R^2\leq R^2$, hence $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{near}}\geq1$.
    Since $x_\Delta>0$ is the unique maximizer in \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:optimal}, the value $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{near}}$ of the maximand at $x=0$ is strictly smaller than $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}$; and $1\leq A_\Delta^{\mathrm{near}}\leq A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}$ shows that it also tends to $1$ as $\Delta\searrow0$.
    Diameter-only optimality at every $0<\delta<\pi/2$ is \cref{lmm:pos:vi:sharp:diameter}, and at each fixed $\pi/2\leq\delta<\pi$, \cref{prp:pos:diameterobstruction}\,\ref{itm:pos:obstruction:vi} below shows that no finite variance constant depending only on $\delta$ exists.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lmm:pos:curved:foc}
    Let $(\mathcal{Q},d)$ be a complete $\mathsf{CAT}(1)$ space, $o\in\mathcal{Q}$, $0<R<\pi/2$, let $\mu$ be a probability measure concentrated on $B(o,R)$, and let $m:=m_2(\mu)$. Then, with $t/\sin(t):=1$ at $t=0$, every $p\in\overline B(o,R)$ satisfies
    \begin{equation*}
        \int\cos(\overline{yp})\,\frac{\overline{ym}}{\sin(\overline{ym})}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
        \leq\cos(\overline{mp})\int\cos(\overline{ym})\,\frac{\overline{ym}}{\sin(\overline{ym})}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
The mean $m$ exists, is unique and lies in $B(o,R)$ \cite[Theorem B and Corollary 16]{Yokota2016Barycenter-JMSJ}. Put $a:=\overline{mp}$; if $a=0$ there is nothing to prove. All distances between points of $\overline B(o,R)$ are at most $2R<\pi$, so geodesics between them are unique. By the first variation formula \cite[Corollary II.3.6]{BridsonHaefliger1999NPC-Springer} and dominated convergence (the difference quotients of $\overline{yq}^2$ along $[m,p]$ are bounded by $4R$, since $[m,p]\subseteq\overline B(o,R)$ by convexity of balls of radius less than $\pi/2$ \cite[Proposition II.1.4(3)]{BridsonHaefliger1999NPC-Springer}), the right derivative at $m$ of $q\mapsto\frac12\int\overline{yq}^2\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)$ along $[m,p]$ is $-\int\overline{ym}\cos(\angle_m(p,y))\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)$, with the integrand read as $0$ at $y=m$. Minimality of $m$ gives
    \begin{equation*}
        \int\overline{ym}\cos(\angle_m(p,y))\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\leq0
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Fix $y\neq m$ and put $c:=\overline{ym}$ and $e:=\overline{yp}$, so that $a,c\in(0,\pi)$ and $e\in[0,\pi)$. We claim
    \begin{equation*}
        \sin(a)\sin(c)\cos(\angle_m(p,y))\geq\cos(e)-\cos(a)\cos(c)
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
    If $a+c+e<2\pi$, this is the comparison $\angle_m(p,y)\leq\tilde\angle_m(p,y)$ \cite[Proposition II.1.7(4)]{BridsonHaefliger1999NPC-Springer} together with the spherical law of cosines.
    If $a+c+e\geq2\pi$, then $a+c>\pi$ because $e<\pi$, so $e$ and $2\pi-a-c$ both lie in $[0,\pi]$ with $e\geq2\pi-a-c$; hence $\cos(e)\leq\cos(a+c)=\cos(a)\cos(c)-\sin(a)\sin(c)$, and the claim follows from $\cos(\angle_m(p,y))\geq-1$.
    Multiplying the claim by $c/\sin(c)>0$, integrating, and noting that both integrands vanish at $y=m$, we obtain
    \begin{equation*}
        \int\frac{\overline{ym}}{\sin(\overline{ym})}\left( \cos(\overline{yp})-\cos(a)\cos(\overline{ym}) \right)\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
        \leq\sin(a)\int\overline{ym}\cos(\angle_m(p,y))\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
        \leq0
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    which is the assertion. All integrands are bounded because $\overline{ym}/\sin(\overline{ym})\leq2R/\sin(2R)$.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lmm:pos:cosine-average}
    Use $C_x$ as in \eqref{eq:pos:const:C}.
    Let $g(v):=\arccos(v)^2$ and
    \begin{equation*}
        H(v):=\frac{\sin(\arccos(v))}{\arccos(v)}
        =\frac{\sqrt{1-v^2}}{\arccos(v)}
        \qquad(-1<v<1)\,,
    \end{equation*}
    with $H(1):=1$ and derivatives at $v=1$ understood from the left.
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item $g$ is nonnegative, decreasing, and convex, with $g(1)=0$, $g^\prime=-2/H$ and $g^\prime(\cos(x))=-2C_x$ for $0\leq x<\pi$, and $H$ is positive, increasing, and concave.
        \item The map $v\mapsto v/H(v)$ is increasing, with value $t\cot(t)$ at $v=\cos(t)$, $t\in(0,\pi)$, and it tends to $1$ as $v\nearrow1$.
        \item Let $X$ be a random variable with values in $(-1,1]$ such that $U:=\mathbf{E}[1/H(X)]<\infty$, and put $K:=\mathbf{E}[X/H(X)]$ and $z:=K/U$. Then $z\in(-1,1]$, and if $z>0$, then
        \begin{equation*}
            K\geq\frac z{H(z)}
            \,.
        \end{equation*}
        In particular, $z\geq\cos(t)$ for some $t\in[0,\pi/2)$ implies $K\geq t\cot(t)>0$, with $t\cot(t)=1$ at $t=0$.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
\emph{Proof of (i).}
    Nonnegativity and monotonicity of $g$ follow from those of $\arccos$, and
    $g^\prime(v)=-2\arccos(v)/\sqrt{1-v^2}=-2/H(v)$, which equals $-2C_x$ at $v=\cos(x)$.
    At $v=\cos(t)$ with $t\in(0,\pi)$,
    \begin{equation*}
        g^{\prime\prime}(\cos(t))=\frac{2\left( \sin(t)-t\cos(t) \right)}{\sin(t)^3}>0
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    since $\sin(t)-t\cos(t)$ vanishes at zero and has derivative $t\sin(t)>0$; hence $g$ is convex.
    Next, $H(\cos(t))=\sin(t)/t$ is positive and decreasing in $t\in(0,\pi)$, so $H$ is positive and increasing.
    For concavity,
    \begin{equation*}
        H^{\prime\prime}(\cos(t))=-\frac{G(t)}{t^3\sin(t)^3}
        \,,\qquad
        G(t):=t^2+t\sin(t)\cos(t)-2\sin(t)^2
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    where $G(0)=G^\prime(0)=0$ and $G^{\prime\prime}(t)=4\sin(t)(\sin(t)-t\cos(t))>0$ on $(0,\pi)$, so that $G>0$ and $H^{\prime\prime}<0$ there.

    \emph{Proof of (ii).}
    At $v=\cos(t)$ the quotient equals $\cos(t)\arccos(\cos(t))/\sqrt{1-\cos(t)^2}=t\cot(t)$, whose derivative in $t$ is $(\sin(t)\cos(t)-t)/\sin(t)^2<0$ on $(0,\pi)$ because $\sin(2t)<2t$.
