    As $t\mapsto\cos(t)$ is decreasing, the quotient is increasing in $v$.

    \emph{Proof of (iii).}
    Since $1/H>0$, the law $\lambda$ of $X$ reweighted by the density $1/(UH(X))$ is a probability measure, and $z=\mathbf{E}_\lambda[X]$ is an average of values in $(-1,1]$, hence lies in $(-1,1]$.
    Jensen's inequality for the concave $H$ gives
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac1U=\mathbf{E}_\lambda\!\left[ H(X) \right]\leq H\!\left( \mathbf{E}_\lambda\!\left[ X \right] \right)=H(z)
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    so that $U\geq1/H(z)$ and therefore $K=Uz\geq z/H(z)$ whenever $z>0$.
    By (ii), $z\geq\cos(t)$ with $0\leq t<\pi/2$ gives $z/H(z)\geq\cos(t)/H(\cos(t))=t\cot(t)>0$.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lmm:pos:cosine-conversion}
    Let $g$ and $H$ be as in \cref{lmm:pos:cosine-average}.
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item The map $\Phi(x,v):=H(x)(g(v)-g(x))$ is jointly convex on $(-1,1]^2$.
        Consequently, if $X,V$ are random variables taking values in a compact subinterval of $(-1,1]$, then
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:scalar:jensen}
            \mathbf{E}\!\left[ H(X)\left( g(V)-g(X) \right) \right]
            \geq H\!\left( \mathbf{E}\!\left[ X \right] \right)\left( g\!\left( \mathbf{E}\!\left[ V \right] \right)-g\!\left( \mathbf{E}\!\left[ X \right] \right) \right)
            \,.
        \end{equation}
        \item For $0\leq t<\pi/2$, the function
        $G_t(\ell):=g(\cos(t)\cos(\ell))$ is concave as a function of $\ell^2$ on $0\leq \ell<\pi/2$.
        Concavity is strict for $t>0$.
        Consequently $(G_t(\ell)-t^2)/\ell^2$ is nonincreasing for $0<\ell<\pi/2$, and $G_t(\ell)\leq t^2+\ell^2$ for $0\leq \ell<\pi/2$.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
\emph{A prerequisite for (i).} We show that $\chi:=-HH''/(H')^2$ is decreasing on $(-1,1)$.
    Let $v=\cos(t)$ with $0<t<\pi$, and put $w:=t^2\in(0,\pi^2)$.
    Differentiating $H(\cos(t))=\sin(t)/t$ in $t$ gives $H'(\cos(t))=(1-t\cot(t))/t^2$, and Euler's partial fraction expansion $t\cot(t)=1-2\sum_{n=1}^\infty t^2/(n^2\pi^2-t^2)$ \cite[(4.22.3)]{DLMF2026-NIST} yields
    \begin{equation*}
        H'(\cos(t))=2S(w),\qquad S(w):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac1{n^2\pi^2-w}
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
    This series and its termwise derivatives converge locally uniformly on $(-\infty,\pi^2)$.
    Since $\mathrm{d} w/\mathrm{d} v=-2t/\sin(t)=-2/H(v)$, we get $H''(\cos(t))=-4S'(w)/H(\cos(t))$, hence $\chi(\cos(t))=S'(w)/S(w)^2$, and
    \begin{equation*}
        \begin{aligned}
        \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} w}\frac{S'(w)}{S(w)^2}
        &=\frac{S(w)S''(w)-2S'(w)^2}{S(w)^3}
        \\&=\frac2{S(w)^3}\left( \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac1{n^2\pi^2-w}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac1{(n^2\pi^2-w)^3}-\left( \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac1{(n^2\pi^2-w)^2} \right)^2 \right)
        >0
        \end{aligned}
    \end{equation*}
    by the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, strictly because the numbers $n^2\pi^2-w$ are not all equal.
    Hence $\chi(\cos(t))$ increases with $t$, that is, $\chi$ decreases with its argument.

    \emph{Proof of (i).}
    For $-1<x,v<1$, put $I:=\int_v^xH(u)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d} u$, so that $g(v)-g(x)=2I$ by $g^\prime=-2/H$.
    Since $(1/H')'=-H''/(H')^2=\chi/H$,
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac1{H'(x)}-\frac1{H'(v)}-\chi(x)I
        =\int_v^x\frac{\chi(u)-\chi(x)}{H(u)}\,\mathrm{d} u
        \geq0
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    in both orientations, because $\chi$ is decreasing: for $v\leq x$ the integrand is nonnegative on $[v,x]$, and for $v>x$ it is nonpositive on $[x,v]$ while the orientation of the integral is reversed.
    By \cref{lmm:pos:cosine-average}\,(i), $g^\prime=-2/H$, hence $g''=2H'/H^2$, and $H$ is positive and increasing.
    The second derivatives of $\Phi$ are
    \begin{equation*}
        \Phi_{xx}=2H''(x)I+\frac{2H'(x)}{H(x)}
        \,,\qquad
        \Phi_{xv}=-\frac{2H'(x)}{H(v)}
        \,,\qquad
        \Phi_{vv}=\frac{2H(x)H'(v)}{H(v)^2}>0
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    and $H(x)H''(x)=-\chi(x)H'(x)^2$ gives
    \begin{equation*}
        \begin{split}
        \frac{\det\nabla^2\Phi(x,v)}{g'(v)^2}
        &=H(x)H''(x)H'(v)I+H'(x)H'(v)-H'(x)^2\\
        &=H'(x)^2H'(v)
        \left( \frac1{H'(x)}-\frac1{H'(v)}-\chi(x)I \right)\geq0
        \,.
        \end{split}
    \end{equation*}
    Thus $\nabla^2\Phi$ is positive semidefinite on $(-1,1)^2$.
    Continuity extends convexity to $(-1,1]^2$, including both edges $x=1$ and $v=1$.
    Jensen's inequality for $\Phi$ applied to $(X,V)$ now gives \eqref{eq:pos:scalar:jensen}.

    \emph{Proof of (ii).}
    The case $t=0$ is immediate.
    Otherwise set $s:=\arccos(\cos(t)\cos(\ell))$ and $a(u):=u/\sin(u)$.
    Differentiation yields
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{\mathrm{d} G_t}{\mathrm{d}(\ell^2)}=\cos(t)\,\frac{a(s)}{a(\ell)},\qquad
        \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} \ell}\log\left( \frac{\mathrm{d} G_t}{\mathrm{d}(\ell^2)} \right)
        =\tan(\ell)\left( k(s)-k(\ell) \right),
        \quad k(u):=\frac{\cot(u)}{u}-\cot(u)^2.
    \end{equation*}
    On $(0,\pi/2)$,
    \begin{equation*}
        k'(u)=\frac{\cos(u)}{u^2\sin(u)^3}
        \left( 2u^2-u\tan(u)-\sin(u)^2 \right)<0
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    because
    \begin{equation*}
        u\tan(u)+\sin(u)^2
        \geq u^2\left( 1+\frac{u^2}3 \right)+u^2\left( 1-\frac{u^2}3 \right)
        =2u^2
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    with strict inequality for $u>0$.
    Here $\tan(u)\geq u+u^3/3$ follows by integrating $\tan'(u)=1+\tan(u)^2\geq1+u^2$, strictly for $u>0$, and $(\sin(u)/u)^2\geq(1-u^2/6)^2\geq1-u^2/3$ follows by squaring $\sin(u)\geq u-u^3/6$, whose right-hand side is nonnegative for $0<u<\pi/2$.
    Since $\ell<s<\pi/2$, the logarithmic derivative is strictly negative, proving strict concavity.
    The secant slopes from zero therefore decrease strictly when $t>0$ and are constant when $t=0$.
    Finally the derivative at $\ell^2=0$ is $t\cot(t)\leq1$, so $G_t(\ell)\leq G_t(0)+\ell^2=t^2+\ell^2$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lmm:pos:vi:root}
    Let $0<\Delta<\pi$ and let $E_\Delta$ be as in \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:functions}, so that $E_\Delta=s_\Delta^2-t_\Delta^2$ for
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:vi:radius:ts}
        t_\Delta(x):=\arccos\!\left( \frac{\cos(\Delta/2)}{\cos(x)} \right)
        \,,\qquad
        s_\Delta(x):=\arccos\!\left( \cos(\Delta/2)^2-\cos(\Delta/2)\sin(\Delta/2)\tan(x) \right)
        \,,
    \end{equation}
    defined for $0\leq x\leq\Delta/2$.
    Then \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:root} has a unique solution $x_\Delta\in(0,\Delta/2)$, and $x_\Delta$ is the unique maximizer of $x\mapsto(\Delta/2+x)^2/E_\Delta(x)$ on $[0,\Delta/2]$, so that \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:optimal} holds.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Write $R:=\Delta/2$.
    Derivatives of $E_\Delta$ at the endpoints are understood from within $[0,R]$.

    \emph{Positivity.}
    Differentiating $\cos(s_\Delta(x))=\cos(R)^2-\cos(R)\sin(R)\tan(x)$ and $\cos(t_\Delta(x))=\cos(R)/\cos(x)$ gives
    \begin{equation*}
        s_\Delta'(x)=\frac{\cos(R)\sin(R)}{\cos(x)^2\sin(s_\Delta(x))}
        \,,\qquad
        t_\Delta'(x)=-\cot(t_\Delta(x))\tan(x)
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    and $E_\Delta'/2=s_\Delta s_\Delta'-t_\Delta t_\Delta'$ becomes
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:vi:radius:derivative}
        \frac{E_\Delta'(x)}2
        =\frac{\cos(R)\sin(R)}{\cos(x)^2}\frac{s_\Delta(x)}{\sin(s_\Delta(x))}
        +t_\Delta(x)\cot(t_\Delta(x))\tan(x)
        \,,
    \end{equation}
    where $t\cot(t)=1$ at $t=0$.
    On $[0,R]$ we have $s_\Delta(x)\in(0,\pi)$ and $t_\Delta(x)\in[0,R]\subseteq[0,\pi/2)$, so the first summand is positive and the second is nonnegative; hence $E_\Delta$ is strictly increasing there.
    Since $\cos(R)^2<\cos(R)$, we have $E_\Delta(0)=\arccos(\cos(R)^2)^2-R^2>0$, and therefore $E_\Delta>0$ on $[0,R]$.

    \emph{Monotonicity of $J_\Delta$.}
    Put $J_\Delta(x):=(R+x)E_\Delta'(x)/2-E_\Delta(x)$.
    With $g$ as in \cref{lmm:pos:cosine-average}, the identity
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{\arccos(z)}{\sqrt{1-z^2}}
        =\int_0^\infty\frac{1}{\cosh(u)+z}\,\mathrm{d} u
        \qquad(-1<z\leq1)
    \end{equation*}
    follows by the substitution $v=\tanh(u/2)$, with the value at $z=1$ interpreted continuously.
    Integrating $g^\prime(z)=-2\arccos(z)/\sqrt{1-z^2}$ yields
    \begin{equation*}
        E_\Delta(x)=2\int_0^\infty f_{\cosh(u)}(x)\,\mathrm{d} u,
        \qquad
        f_a(x):=\log\frac{a\cos(x)+\cos(R)}{a\cos(x)+\cos(R)\cos(R+x)}.
    \end{equation*}
    For $a\geq1$, put $N_a:=a\cos(x)+\cos(R)$ and $M_a:=a\cos(x)+\cos(R)\cos(R+x)$.
    Both are positive and nonincreasing on $[0,R]$, with $M_a$ strictly decreasing.
    Moreover $M_a(x)\geq M_1(x)\geq M_1(R)=2\cos(R)^3>0$.
    Differentiation gives
    \begin{equation*}
        f_a'(x)=\frac{N_a'}{N_a}-\frac{M_a'}{M_a}
        =\frac{\cos(R)\left( a\sin(x)+a\sin(R)+\cos(R)\sin(R+x) \right)}{N_aM_a}
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
    With $v:=(R+x)/2$, the identities $\sin(x)+\sin(R)=2\sin(v)\cos((R-x)/2)$, $\sin(R+x)=2\sin(v)\cos(v)$, $2\cos(v)\cos((R-x)/2)=\cos(R)+\cos(x)$, and $2\cos(v)^2=1+\cos(R+x)$ give
    \begin{equation*}
        \begin{split}
        a\left( \sin(x)+\sin(R) \right)+\cos(R)\sin(R+x)
        &=\tan(v)\left( a\left( \cos(R)+\cos(x) \right)+\cos(R)\left( 1+\cos(R+x) \right) \right)\\
        &=\tan(v)\left( M_a+(a+1)\cos(R) \right)
        \,,
        \end{split}
    \end{equation*}
    and therefore
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{f_a'(x)}{\tan((R+x)/2)}
        =\frac{\cos(R)}{N_a}+\frac{\cos(R)^2(a+1)}{N_aM_a}.
    \end{equation*}
    The right-hand side is positive and has a positive derivative in $x$, since $N_a$ and $M_a$ are positive, $N_a'\leq0$, and $M_a'(x)=-a\sin(x)-\cos(R)\sin(R+x)<0$.
    Also $s\mapsto\tan(s/2)/s$ is positive with a positive derivative for $0<s<\pi$.
    Hence $f_a'(x)/(R+x)$, the product of $f_a'(x)/\tan((R+x)/2)$ and $\tan((R+x)/2)/(R+x)$, has a positive derivative in $x$.
    To differentiate under the integral, write $f_a=\log(1+D/M_a)$ with $D(x):=\cos(R)(1-\cos(R+x))$, which does not depend on $a$.
    For $a\geq1$, we have $M_a\geq a\cos(R)^3$: if $\cos(R+x)\geq0$, then $M_a\geq a\cos(x)\geq a\cos(R)$, and otherwise $M_a\geq aM_1(x)\geq2a\cos(R)^3$; moreover, $M_a'$ and $M_a''$ are bounded by $a+1$ in absolute value; since $D$, $D'$, and $D''$ are bounded, $f_a$, $f_a'$, and $f_a''$ are of order $1/a$ as $a\to\infty$, uniformly in $x\in[0,R]$.
    With $a=\cosh(u)\geq\mathrm{e}^u/2$, this is an integrable $\operatorname{\mathcal{O}}(\mathrm{e}^{-u})$ bound, so $E_\Delta'(x)=2\int_0^\infty f_{\cosh(u)}'(x)\,\mathrm{d} u$ and similarly for $E_\Delta''$.
    Consequently, $E_\Delta'(x)/(R+x)=2\int_0^\infty f_{\cosh(u)}'(x)/(R+x)\,\mathrm{d} u$ has a positive derivative, and
    \begin{equation*}
        J_\Delta'(x)=\frac{(R+x)E_\Delta''(x)-E_\Delta'(x)}2
        =\frac{(R+x)^2}2\,\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} x}\frac{E_\Delta'(x)}{R+x}>0.
    \end{equation*}

    \emph{The endpoints.}
    At $x=0$, $t_\Delta'(0)=0$.
    The strict concavity in \cref{lmm:pos:cosine-conversion}\,(ii) for $t=R>0$ compares the secant slope of $v\mapsto g(\cos(R)\cos(\sqrt v))$ on $[0,R^2]$ with its derivative at $R^2$, giving $J_\Delta(0)<0$.
    At the other endpoint, \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:derivative} gives
    \begin{equation*}
        J_\Delta(R)=2R\bigl(\tan(R)-R+R\tan(R)^2\bigr)>0
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    because $\tan(R)>R$.

    \emph{Conclusion.}
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} x}\frac{(R+x)^2}{E_\Delta(x)}
        =-\frac{2(R+x)J_\Delta(x)}{E_\Delta(x)^2}.
    \end{equation*}
    Thus the unique zero $x_\Delta$ of $J_\Delta$ is the unique maximizer of $x\mapsto(R+x)^2/E_\Delta(x)$ on $[0,R]$.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lmm:pos:radius-scalar}
    Let $0\leq a\leq R<\pi/2$, put $t:=\arccos(\cos(R)/\cos(a))$ and $u:=(R+a)/2$. Then
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:scalar:radius}
        t\cot(t)\left( \frac{\sin(u)}u \right)^2\geq\cos(R)
        \,,
    \end{equation}
    with all ratios at zero understood by continuity. The inequality is strict for $R>0$.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Put $v:=(R-a)/2$, so that $a=u-v$ and $R=u+v$ with $0\leq v\leq u<\pi/2$. Then
    \begin{equation*}
        \begin{split}
            \cos(a)\,\frac{\sin(t)}{t}
            &\leq\frac{2\cos(a)+\cos(R)}{3}
            =\frac{3\cos(u)\cos(v)+\sin(u)\sin(v)}{3}\\
            &\leq\frac{\sqrt{1+8\cos(u)^2}}3
            \leq\frac{1+2\cos(u)}{3}
            \leq\left( \frac{\sin(u)}{u} \right)^2.
        \end{split}
    \end{equation*}
    The first step uses $\cos(t)=\cos(R)/\cos(a)$ together with $\sin(t)/t\leq(2+\cos(t))/3$: the difference
    $t(2+\cos(t))-3\sin(t)$ and its first derivative vanish at zero, and its second derivative is $\sin(t)-t\cos(t)\geq0$.
    The next two inequalities follow from Cauchy--Schwarz and squaring, respectively.
    For the last, the alternating Taylor bounds on $[0,\pi/2]$ give
    \begin{equation*}
        \left( \frac{\sin(u)}{u} \right)^2-\frac{1+2\cos(u)}{3}
        \geq
        \left( 1-\frac{u^2}3+\frac{2u^4}{45}-\frac{u^6}{315} \right)
        -\left( 1-\frac{u^2}3+\frac{u^4}{36} \right)
        =u^4\left( \frac1{60}-\frac{u^2}{315} \right)\geq0
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    which is strict for $u>0$, hence for $R>0$.
    For $t>0$, this proves \eqref{eq:pos:scalar:radius} by the identity
    \[
      t\cot(t)=\frac{\cos(R)}{\cos(a)\sin(t)/t}.
    \]
    At $t=0$, that is, at $a=R$ and $u=R$, the required bound reads $\cos(R)\leq(\sin(R)/R)^2$, which follows from $\cos(R)\leq(1+2\cos(R))/3$ and the last inequality of the chain.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lmm:pos:vi:sharp:radius}
    Let $0<\Delta<\pi$, let $0\leq x\leq\Delta/2$, and let $E_\Delta$ be as in \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:functions}.
    On the unit sphere in $\mathbb{R}^3$, a compact $\mathsf{CAT}(1)$ space, there are a closed ball $\mathcal{C}=\overline B(o,\Delta/2)$, which is convex with circumradius exactly $\Delta/2$, a measure $\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$ and a point $q\in\mathcal{C}$ such that $m:=m_2(\mu)$ satisfies $\overline{mo}=x$, $\overline{mq}=\Delta/2+x$, and
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:vi:sharp:radius}
        \overline{mq}^2=\frac{(\Delta/2+x)^2}{E_\Delta(x)}\int\left( \overline{yq}^2-\overline{ym}^2 \right)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
        \,.
    \end{equation}
    In particular, \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:optimal} gives $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}$ at $x=x_\Delta$ and \eqref{eq:pos:vi:near:constant} gives $A_\Delta^{\mathrm{near}}$ at $x=0$.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Write $R:=\Delta/2$ and $t:=t_\Delta(x)$, with $t_\Delta$ and $s_\Delta$ as in \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:ts}, and take the following points on the unit sphere in $\mathbb{R}^3$:
    \begin{equation*}
        \begin{split}
            m&=(0,0,1),\qquad o=(\sin(x),0,\cos(x)),\\
            y_\pm&=(0,\pm\sin(t),\cos(t)),\qquad
            q=(\sin(R+x),0,\cos(R+x)).
        \end{split}
    \end{equation*}
    The closed spherical ball $\mathcal{C}=\overline B(o,R)$ is convex and has circumradius exactly $R$, since its diameter is $2R$.
    The points $y_\pm$ and $q$ lie on its boundary.
    Put $\mu:=(\delta_{y_+}+\delta_{y_-})/2$ and $F(p):=\int\overline{yp}^2\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)$.
    Since $m$ is the midpoint of the segment from $y_-$ to $y_+$ of length $2t<\pi$, it is the mean of $\mu$.
    Indeed, for every $p$ on the sphere, the triangle inequality gives
    \begin{equation*}
        F(p)=\tfrac12\left( \overline{y_+p}^2+\overline{y_-p}^2 \right)
        \geq\tfrac14\overline{y_+y_-}^2=t^2=F(m)
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Equality forces $\overline{y_+p}=\overline{y_-p}=t$, so $p$ is a midpoint of $y_-$ and $y_+$, which is $m$ because the geodesic between them is unique, its length $2t$ being less than $\pi$; when $t=0$, $\mu$ is the point mass at $m$.
    Consequently
    \begin{equation*}
        F(m)=t^2,\qquad F(q)=s_\Delta(x)^2,\qquad
        \frac{\overline{mq}^2}{F(q)-F(m)}
        =\frac{(R+x)^2}{E_\Delta(x)}
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    which is \eqref{eq:pos:vi:sharp:radius}.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lmm:pos:vi:sharp:diameter}
    Let $0<\delta<\pi/2$, and let $\theta_\delta$ and $A_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}$ be as in \eqref{eq:pos:vi:constants}.
    There are compact $\mathsf{CAT}(1)$ spaces $\mathcal{Q}_n$, $n\geq2$, realized in a common Hilbert sphere, each of diameter exactly $\delta$ and circumradius at most $\theta_\delta<\pi/2$, measures $\mu_n\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Q}_n)$, and fixed points $m,q\in\bigcap_{n\geq2}\mathcal{Q}_n$ such that $m_2(\mu_n)=m$ and
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{\overline{mq}^2}{\int\left( \overline{yq}^2-\overline{ym}^2 \right)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_n(y)}
        \longrightarrow A_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}
        \qquad(n\to\infty)
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Write $\theta:=\theta_\delta$ and use $g$ from \cref{lmm:pos:cosine-average}.
    In $\ell^2$, fix orthonormal vectors $m,e$.
    For each $n\geq2$, choose a regular simplex of unit vectors $v_1,\ldots,v_n$ perpendicular to both, with $\sum_i v_i=0$ and $\left\langlev_i,v_j\right\rangle=-1/(n-1)$ for $i\ne j$.
    Put
    \begin{equation*}
        q=\cos(\theta)\,m+\sin(\theta)\,e,\qquad
        y_i=\cos(t_n)\,m+\sin(t_n)\,v_i,\qquad
        \cos(t_n)^2=\frac{1+(n-1)\cos(\delta)}{n},
    \end{equation*}
    where $0<t_n<\theta$.
    Let $\mu_n$ be uniform on the $y_i$, with $F_n(p):=\int\overline{yp}^2\,\mathrm{d}\mu_n(y)$.
    The spherical convex hull of the $y_i$ together with $q$ is a compact $\mathsf{CAT}(1)$ space $\mathcal{Q}_n$.
    All vertex inner products are at least $\cos(\delta)$, and normalization of nonnegative linear combinations preserves this lower bound.
    Hence $\mathsf{diam}(\mathcal{Q}_n)=\delta$, with equality already between two $y_i$.
    Also $\sum_i y_i=n\cos(t_n)m$, so $m\in\mathcal{Q}_n$.
    All vertices lie in $\overline B(m,\theta)$, which is convex, so the circumradius of $\mathcal{Q}_n$ is at most $\theta<\pi/2$.
    Convexity and monotonicity of $g$ give, for every $p\in\mathcal{Q}_n$,
    \begin{equation*}
        F_n(p)=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^{n} g(\left\langley_i,p\right\rangle)
        \geq g(\cos(t_n)\left\langlem,p\right\rangle)\geq t_n^2,
    \end{equation*}
    with equality only if $\left\langlem,p\right\rangle=1$, since $g$ is strictly decreasing; so $m$ is its unique mean, independently of $n$, and the same bound for every $p$ on the whole sphere shows that $m$ is also the unique mean there.
    As $n\to\infty$, $\cos(t_n)^2=\cos(\delta)+(1-\cos(\delta))/n$ decreases to $\cos(\theta)^2$, so $t_n\nearrow\theta$ and therefore
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{\overline{mq}^2}{F_n(q)-F_n(m)}
        =\frac{\theta^2}{g(\cos(t_n)\cos(\theta))-t_n^2}
        \longrightarrow\frac{\theta^2}{g(\cos(\theta)^2)-\theta^2}
        =\frac{\theta^2}{\delta^2-\theta^2}
        =A_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    using $\cos(\theta)^2=\cos(\delta)$ in the last step but one.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lmm:pos:variances}
    In the setting of \cref{ssec:pos:prelim}, let $Y\sim\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$.
    If \eqref{eq:2fmcatk:vi} holds for $\mu$ with a constant $b>0$ in place of $C_\Delta$, then
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbf{V_{\!F}}\!\left[ Y \right]\leq\frac{2b}{1+b}\,\mathbf{V_{\!M}}\!\left[ Y \right]
        \qquad\text{and}\qquad
        \mathbf{V_{\!M}}\!\left[ Y \right]\leq2\,\mathbf{V_{\!F}}\!\left[ Y \right]
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
    In particular, $b=C_\Delta$ gives \eqref{eq:pos:variances}, and $b=A_{\Delta,\delta}$ gives the sharper comparison in \cref{rem:pos:sharper}.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Let $Y^\prime$ be an independent copy of $Y$ and put $m=m_2(\mu)$.
    Integrating the assumed variance inequality with respect to $q=Y^\prime$ gives
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbf{V_{\!F}}\!\left[ Y \right]=\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{mY^\prime}^2 \right]
        \leq b\,\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{YY^\prime}^2-\overline{Ym}^2 \right]
        =b\left( 2\mathbf{V_{\!M}}\!\left[ Y \right]-\mathbf{V_{\!F}}\!\left[ Y \right] \right).
    \end{equation*}
    Rearranging proves the first bound; the second follows from \cref{lmm:variances} with $s=2$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lmm:pos:cp:product}
    In the setting of \cref{ssec:pos:prelim}, all $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$ satisfy
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:cp:product}
        \overline{m_2(\mu)m_2(\nu)}
        \leq C_\delta\min\{A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}},A_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}\}\,W_1(\mu,\nu)
        \,.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Put $m_\mu:=m_2(\mu)$, $m_\nu:=m_2(\nu)$, and $A:=\min\{A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}},A_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}\}$; the case $m_\mu=m_\nu$ is trivial.
    Let $\pi$ be a coupling of $\mu$ and $\nu$.
    \Cref{thm:pos:hemisphere:vi} for $\mu$ at $q=m_\nu$ and for $\nu$ at $q=m_\mu$, added, and \cref{prp:prelim:qi} with $\alpha=2$ give
    \begin{equation*}
        2\,\overline{m_\mum_\nu}^2
        \leq A\int\left( \overline{ym_\nu}^2-\overline{ym_\mu}^2-\overline{zm_\nu}^2+\overline{zm_\mu}^2 \right)\mathrm{d}\pi(y,z)
        \leq 2AC_\delta\,\overline{m_\mum_\nu}\int\overline{yz}\,\mathrm{d}\pi(y,z)
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    because all four points lie in the convex set $\mathcal{C}$, so that the geodesic segments between them have $\sqrt\kappa\,\mathsf{diam}([y,z]\cup[m_\mu,m_\nu])\leq\delta$, and $C_x$ is increasing.
    Dividing by $2\,\overline{m_\mum_\nu}$ and taking the infimum over $\pi$ proves \eqref{eq:pos:cp:product}.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:pos:hemisphere:cp}
    In the setting of \cref{ssec:pos:prelim}, suppose $0<\Delta<\pi$, and let $t_\Delta(x)=\arccos(\cos(\Delta/2)/\cos(x))$ be as in \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:ts}.
    Define
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:cp:radius:optimal}
        B_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}
        :=\max_{0\leq x\leq\Delta/2}
        C_{\Delta/2+x}\,\frac{\tan(t_\Delta(x))}{t_\Delta(x)},
    \end{equation}
    with $\tan(t)/t:=1$ at zero, and
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:cp:diameter:optimal}
        B_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}:=
        \begin{cases}
            1/\sqrt{\cos(\delta)},&0<\delta<\pi/2,\\
            +\infty,&\pi/2\leq\delta<\pi.
        \end{cases}
    \end{equation}
    Set $B_0^{\mathrm{rad}}=B_0^{\mathrm{diam}}:=1$ by continuity.
    Then, for all $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:cp}
        \overline{m_2(\mu)m_2(\nu)}
        \leq\min\{B_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}},B_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}\}\,W_1(\mu,\nu)
        \,.
    \end{equation}
    The two constants are optimal among constants depending only on $\Delta$ and only on $\delta$, respectively, uniformly over all such spaces, sets, and measures, at each positive value of the corresponding parameter.
    The radius constant satisfies
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:cp:radius:upper}
        C_\Delta<B_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}<\min\left\lbrace \frac1{\cos(\Delta/2)^2},\,2C_\Delta \right\rbrace
        \qquad\text{and}\qquad
        \frac1{\cos(\Delta/2)^2}<C_\Delta^2
        \qquad(0<\Delta<\pi).
    \end{equation}
    The maximum in \eqref{eq:pos:cp:radius:optimal} is attained in $(0,\Delta/2)$; its maximizer need not be the $x_\Delta$ in \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:root}.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
For this proof and the following lemmas, we write $C(x):=C_x$ for the factor \eqref{eq:pos:const:C}.
    Rescaling permits $\kappa=1$, so that $\Delta=2\varrho$ and $\delta=\mathsf{diam}(\mathcal{C})<\pi$.
    For $\eta\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$, write $m_\eta:=m_2(\eta)$.
    Choose $R\in(\varrho,\pi/2)$ and $o\in\mathcal{Q}$ such that $\mathcal{C}\subseteq B(o,R)$; the estimates of the proof of \cref{thm:pos:hemisphere:vi} used below refer to this enclosing ball.

    \emph{Transport interpolation and local comparison.}
    Fix a coupling $\varpi$ of $\mu,\nu$ with cost $I:=\int\overline{yz}\,\mathrm{d}\varpi(y,z)$; the case $I=0$ is immediate.
    Let $\gamma_{y,z}$ be the constant-speed geodesic from $y$ to $z$, and let $\eta_s$ be the distribution of $\gamma_{y,z}(s)$ under $\varpi$, for $s\in[0,1]$.
    These geodesics are unique, depend continuously on their endpoints, and stay in $\mathcal{C}$.
    Put $m_s:=m_{\eta_s}$.
    The coupling $(\gamma_{y,z}(s),\gamma_{y,z}(t))_\#\varpi$ of $\eta_s$ and $\eta_t$ gives the first bound below, and the preliminary bound \eqref{eq:pos:cp:product} of \cref{lmm:pos:cp:product}, with $L_0:=C(\delta)\min\{A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}},A_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}\}<\infty$, then gives the second:
    \begin{equation*}
        W_1(\eta_s,\eta_t)\leq I|t-s|,
        \qquad \overline{m_sm_t}\leq L_0I|t-s|.
    \end{equation*}
    As in \eqref{eq:pos:curved:AU}, define
    \begin{equation*}
        K_\eta:=\int\overline{ym_\eta}\cot(\overline{ym_\eta})\,\mathrm{d}\eta(y),
    \end{equation*}
    with $r\cot(r):=1$ at zero.
    The cosine form \eqref{eq:pos:curved:intermediate} of the variance inequality gives
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:cp:cosine}
        \int\left( \overline{yq}^2-\overline{ym_\eta}^2 \right)\,\mathrm{d}\eta(y)
        \geq2K_\eta\left( 1-\cos(\overline{m_\etaq}) \right).
    \end{equation}
    To apply the quadruple inequality to two nearby times, take $0\leq s<t\leq1$.
    Since $I\leq\delta$, the relevant segments satisfy
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathsf{diam}\bigl([\gamma_{y,z}(s),\gamma_{y,z}(t)]
                   \cup[m_s,m_t]\bigr)
        \leq\sup_{v\in\mathcal{C}}\overline{m_sv}+(1+L_0)\delta(t-s)<\pi
    \end{equation*}
    for all sufficiently small $t-s>0$, uniformly in $s,y,z$.
    Apply \eqref{eq:pos:cp:cosine} to $\eta_s,\eta_t$, using each mean as the other measure's comparison point, and add.
    Integrating the quadruple inequality of \cref{prp:prelim:qi} against $\varpi$ then yields
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:cp:local:transport}
        \begin{split}
            &2(K_{\eta_s}+K_{\eta_t})\left( 1-\cos(\overline{m_sm_t}) \right)\\
            &\qquad\leq2C\left( \sup_{v\in\mathcal{C}}\overline{m_sv}+(1+L_0)\delta(t-s) \right)\,\overline{m_sm_t}\,I(t-s).
        \end{split}
    \end{equation}
    The function $r\mapsto r\cot(r)$ is continuously differentiable on $[0,\delta]$, and both the observation paths and the mean path are uniformly Lipschitz.
    Thus $s\mapsto K_{\eta_s}$ is Lipschitz.
    It is positive by \eqref{eq:pos:curved:A-lower}, hence bounded away from zero on $[0,1]$.
    For $m_s\ne m_t$, divide \eqref{eq:pos:cp:local:transport} and apply the half-angle identity
    \begin{equation*}
        2(1-\cos(x))=4\sin(x/2)^2
        =x^2\left( \frac{\sin(x/2)}{x/2} \right)^2
        \qquad(x>0)
    \end{equation*}
    with $x=\overline{m_sm_t}$ to obtain
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:cp:local:ratio}
        \overline{m_sm_t}\leq\left( \frac{C\left( \sup_{v\in\mathcal{C}}\overline{m_sv} \right)}{K_{\eta_s}}+\mathrm{o}(1) \right) I(t-s),
    \end{equation}
    where $\mathrm{o}(1)\to0$ as $t-s\searrow0$, uniformly for $0\leq s<t\leq1$.
    The same bound holds when $m_s=m_t$.

    \emph{Geometric bounds and summation.}
    For $x:=\overline{om_\eta}$, \eqref{eq:pos:curved:A-lower} and the triangle inequality give
    \begin{equation*}
        K_\eta\geq t_{2R}(x)\cot(t_{2R}(x)),\qquad
        \sup_{v\in\mathcal{C}}\overline{m_\etav}\leq R+x.
    \end{equation*}
    Since $C(u)$ increases with $u$, this bounds $C(\sup_{v\in\mathcal{C}}\overline{m_\etav})/K_\eta$ by $B_{2R}^{\mathrm{rad}}$.
    If $\delta<\pi/2$, combine the bound $K\geq z/H(z)$ in \eqref{eq:pos:curved:A-lower} with \eqref{eq:pos:curved:diameter} and the monotonicity of $z/H(z)$ from \cref{lmm:pos:cosine-average} to obtain
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:cp:local:diameter}
        \begin{split}
            K_\eta&\geq T_\delta\!\left( \sup_{v\in\mathcal{C}}\overline{m_\etav} \right)\cot\!\left( T_\delta\!\left( \sup_{v\in\mathcal{C}}\overline{m_\etav} \right) \right),\\
            T_\delta(\ell)&:=\arccos\!\left( \max\{\sqrt{\cos(\delta)},\cos(\delta)/\cos(\ell)\} \right).
        \end{split}
    \end{equation}
    The comparison holds for every $q\in\mathcal{C}$, so taking the supremum of $\overline{m_\etaq}$ gives this bound without requiring a farthest point.
    By \cref{lmm:pos:cp:scalar}, it bounds $C(\sup_{v\in\mathcal{C}}\overline{m_\etav})/K_\eta$ by $B_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}$.
    Therefore \eqref{eq:pos:cp:local:ratio} gives
    \begin{equation*}
        \overline{m_sm_t}
        \leq\left( \min\{B_{2R}^{\mathrm{rad}},B_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}\}+\mathrm{o}(1) \right) I(t-s),
    \end{equation*}
    where again $\mathrm{o}(1)\to0$ as $t-s\searrow0$, uniformly for $0\leq s<t\leq1$.
    This also covers $B_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}=+\infty$.
    Partition $[0,1]$ into equal intervals, sum, and pass to the limit to get
    \begin{equation*}
        \overline{m_\mum_\nu}
        \leq\min\{B_{2R}^{\mathrm{rad}},B_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}\}\,I.
    \end{equation*}
    Take the infimum over couplings and let $R\searrow\varrho$.
    Continuity of the radius maximum follows by writing $x=Ru$, $u\in[0,1]$, as in the variance proof.
    This proves \eqref{eq:pos:cp}.
    The remaining claims follow from \cref{lmm:pos:cp:scalar,lmm:pos:cp:sharp:radius,lmm:pos:cp:sharp:diameter}.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lmm:pos:cp:scalar}
    For $0<\delta<\pi/2$ and $T_\delta$ from \eqref{eq:pos:cp:local:diameter},
    \begin{equation*}
        \max_{0\leq \ell\leq\delta}
        \frac{C(\ell)}{T_\delta(\ell)\cot(T_\delta(\ell))}
        =\frac1{\sqrt{\cos(\delta)}}.
    \end{equation*}
    The radius constant satisfies \eqref{eq:pos:cp:radius:upper}, and every maximizer in \eqref{eq:pos:cp:radius:optimal} lies in $(0,\Delta/2)$.
    Also $B_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}\to1$ as $\Delta\searrow0$ and $B_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}\to1$ as $\delta\searrow0$.
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Recall from the proof of \cref{lmm:pos:cosine-conversion} that
    $k(u):=\cot(u)/u-\cot(u)^2$, with $k(0):=1/3$, is strictly decreasing on $(0,\pi/2)$.

    For the diameter maximum, put $\theta:=\theta_\delta$.
    On $0\leq \ell\leq\theta$, $T_\delta(\ell)=\theta$ and $C(\ell)$ increases, giving the maximum $C(\theta)/(\theta\cot(\theta))=1/\cos(\theta)$.
    For $\theta<\ell<\delta$, write $t:=T_\delta(\ell)$.
    Differentiating $\cos(t)=\cos(\delta)/\cos(\ell)$ gives $t'=-\cot(t)\tan(\ell)$, hence
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} \ell}\log\frac{C(\ell)}{t\cot(t)}
        =\tan(\ell)\,\left( k(\ell)+k(t)-1 \right)<0.
    \end{equation*}
    The sign follows from $k(\ell),k(t)\leq1/3$.
    The maximum is therefore $1/\cos(\theta)=1/\sqrt{\cos(\delta)}$.

    For the radius, put $R:=\Delta/2$ and
    $f_{2R}(x):=C(R+x)/(t_{2R}(x)\cot(t_{2R}(x)))$.
    Since $C(u)$ increases, $t\cot(t)$ decreases, and $0\leq t_{2R}(x)\leq R$,
    \begin{equation*}
        f_{2R}(x)\leq\frac{C(2R)}{R\cot(R)}=\frac1{\cos(R)^2}.
    \end{equation*}
    Equality in the numerator bound requires $x=R$, whereas equality in the denominator bound requires $x=0$.
    For $R>0$, compactness therefore makes the upper bound strict.
    Also $1/\cos(R)^2<C(2R)^2$ follows from $\sin(R)<R$.
    For the second upper bound, $t:=t_{2R}(x)$ satisfies $\cos(t)=\cos(R)/\cos(x)$, so $t\cot(t)\geq\cos(t)$ gives $\tan(t)/t\leq\cos(x)/\cos(R)$; with $\sin(R+x)\geq\sin(R)\cos(x)$, equality only at $x=0$, this yields $f_{2R}(x)\leq(R+x)/(\sin(R)\cos(R))\leq4R/\sin(2R)=2C(2R)$, equality in the last step only at $x=R$. Hence $B_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}<2C_\Delta$.
    Using $t_{2R}'(x)=-\cot(t_{2R}(x))\tan(x)$ as above gives
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{f_{2R}'(x)}{f_{2R}(x)}
        =\frac1{R+x}-\cot(R+x)-\tan(x)\,\left( 1-k(t_{2R}(x)) \right).
    \end{equation*}
    Its continuous endpoint value at $x=0$ is positive, whereas at $x=R$ it is
    $(3-3R\cot(R)-R\tan(R))/(6R)<0$.
    For the latter sign, put $T:=\tan(R)>0$; multiplying by $6RT$ shows that the sign condition is equivalent to $R(T^2+3)>3T$, that is, to $\arctan(T)>3T/(T^2+3)$, and the function
    $\arctan(T)-3T/(T^2+3)$ vanishes at zero and has derivative
    $4T^4/((1+T^2)(T^2+3)^2)>0$.
    Thus every maximizer is interior, and the maximum strictly exceeds $f_{2R}(R)=C(2R)$.
    This proves \eqref{eq:pos:cp:radius:upper}; squeezing gives the limit at zero.
    The diameter limit is immediate from its formula.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lmm:pos:cp:sensitivity}
    Let $n\geq2$ and $N\geq n+2$. On the unit sphere of $\mathbb{R}^N$, let $m,e$ be orthonormal and let $a_1,\ldots,a_n$ be unit vectors orthogonal to $m,e$ that form a regular simplex with zero sum.
    Fix $0\leq t<\pi/2$ and $0<\ell<\pi$, and set
    \begin{equation*}
        y_j:=\cos(t)\,m+\sin(t)\,a_j,
        \qquad \eta_0:=\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n\delta_{y_j}.
    \end{equation*}
    Let $p(u)$ be a smooth spherical curve, defined near zero and orthogonal to every $a_j$, such that
    $p(0)=\cos(\ell)\,m+\sin(\ell)\,e$ and $p'(0)\ne0$ is orthogonal to $m,e$.
    For $0<\epsilon<1$, set $\eta_{\epsilon,u}:=(1-\epsilon)\eta_0+\epsilon\delta_{p(u)}$. Then
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:cp:sensitivity}
        \lim_{\epsilon\searrow0}\lim_{u\to0}
        \frac{\overline{m_2(\eta_{\epsilon,-u})m_2(\eta_{\epsilon,u})}}
             {W_1(\eta_{\epsilon,-u},\eta_{\epsilon,u})}
        =\frac{C(\ell)}{t\cot(t)},
    \end{equation}
    with $t\cot(t):=1$ at zero.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
\emph{Localization.}
    Choose $\omega\in(0,\pi/2)$ such that $\vert\ell-\omega\vert<\pi/2$, and put $c:=\cos(\omega)\,m+\sin(\omega)\,e$.
    Then $\left\langlec,y_j\right\rangle=\cos(\omega)\cos(t)>0$ and $\left\langlec,p(0)\right\rangle=\cos(\ell-\omega)>0$, so for sufficiently small $\vertu\vert$ all observations lie in a closed spherical ball $\overline B(c,r)$ with $r<\pi/2$, which is convex with circumradius at most $r$.
    The means of $\eta_0$ and $\eta_{\epsilon,u}$ are therefore unique and lie in this ball, and \cref{lmm:pos:cp:product} applies there.
    The Jensen argument from \cref{lmm:pos:vi:sharp:diameter} gives $m_2(\eta_0)=m$, and \eqref{eq:pos:cp:product} with $W_1(\eta_{\epsilon,u},\eta_0)\leq\epsilon\pi$ shows that $m_2(\eta_{\epsilon,u})\to m$ as $\epsilon\to0$, uniformly for small $\vertu\vert$.

    \emph{Symmetry reduction.}
    Put $\ell_u:=\overline{mp(u)}$ and $e(u):=(p(u)-\cos(\ell_u)m)/\sin(\ell_u)$, so that $e(u)$ is a unit vector orthogonal to $m$ and to every $a_j$, and $p(u)=\cos(\ell_u)m+\sin(\ell_u)e(u)$.
    Let $V:=\operatorname{span}\{a_1,\ldots,a_n\}$ and $E:=\operatorname{span}\{m,e(u)\}$, which are orthogonal.
    Every orthogonal map of $\mathbb{R}^N$ that fixes $E$ pointwise and maps $V$ onto itself by permuting the $a_j$ preserves $\eta_{\epsilon,u}$, and so does every reflection in a hyperplane containing $V+E$.
    By uniqueness, these maps fix $m_2(\eta_{\epsilon,u})$.
    The permutations of the $a_j$ fix no nonzero vector of $V$, since a fixed vector $w\in V$ has $\left\langlew,a_j\right\rangle$ independent of $j$, hence $\left\langlew,a_j\right\rangle=\frac1n\left\langlew,\sum_ia_i\right\rangle=0$ for all $j$; and the reflections remove every component orthogonal to $V+E$.
    Hence $m_2(\eta_{\epsilon,u})\in E$, that is, $m_2(\eta_{\epsilon,u})=\cos(\vartheta)m+\sin(\vartheta)e(u)$ for an angle $\vartheta$ near zero.

    \emph{Reduced objective.}
    For $q_\vartheta:=\cos(\vartheta)m+\sin(\vartheta)e(u)$, we have $\left\langley_j,q_\vartheta\right\rangle=\cos(t)\cos(\vartheta)$ and $\overline{p(u)q_\vartheta}=\vert\ell_u-\vartheta\vert$, so $\vartheta$ minimizes, near zero,
    \begin{equation*}
        F_{\epsilon,\ell_u}(\vartheta):=(1-\epsilon)\arccos(\cos(t)\cos(\vartheta))^2
        +\epsilon(\ell_u-\vartheta)^2.
    \end{equation*}
    The first term is a smooth even function of $\vartheta$ whose derivative with respect to $\vartheta^2$ at zero is $t\cot(t)$, as in the proof of \cref{lmm:pos:cosine-conversion}\,(ii); so at $\epsilon=\vartheta=0$ the second derivative of $F_{\epsilon,\ell}$ in $\vartheta$ is $2t\cot(t)>0$, while $\partial_\epsilon\partial_\vartheta F_{\epsilon,\ell}(0)=-2\ell$.
    The implicit function theorem applied to $\partial_\vartheta F_{\epsilon,\ell}(\vartheta)=0$ therefore gives a smooth angular coordinate $\vartheta(\epsilon,\ell_u)$, with
    \begin{equation*}
        \vartheta_\epsilon:=\vartheta(\epsilon,\ell)
        =\frac{\epsilon \ell}{t\cot(t)}+\operatorname{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^2)
        \qquad(\epsilon\searrow0).
    \end{equation*}

    \emph{Speed and transport cost.}
    Since $p'(0)$ is orthogonal to $m$ and $e$, the derivative of $u\mapsto\ell_u=\arccos(\left\langlem,p(u)\right\rangle)$ vanishes at zero, and $e'(0)=p'(0)/\sin(\ell)$.
    Consequently the curve of means
    $u\mapsto\cos(\vartheta(\epsilon,\ell_u))m+\sin(\vartheta(\epsilon,\ell_u))e(u)$
    has velocity $\sin(\vartheta_\epsilon)p'(0)/\sin(\ell)$ at zero, so that $\overline{m_2(\eta_{\epsilon,-u})m_2(\eta_{\epsilon,u})}=2\vertu\vert\sin(\vartheta_\epsilon)\Vertp'(0)\Vert/\sin(\ell)+\mathrm o(u)$.
    Moreover,
    \begin{equation*}
        W_1(\eta_{\epsilon,-u},\eta_{\epsilon,u})
        =\epsilon\,\overline{p(-u)p(u)}
        =2\epsilon\left| u \right|\left\Vertp'(0)\right\Vert+\mathrm o(u)
        \,,
    \end{equation*}
    where retaining the common mass gives the upper bound in the first identity, and integrating the $1$-Lipschitz function $q\mapsto\overline{qp(-u)}$ against the two measures gives the lower bound.
    The inner limit in \eqref{eq:pos:cp:sensitivity} is thus $\sin(\vartheta_\epsilon)/(\epsilon\sin(\ell))$.
    Letting $\epsilon\searrow0$ proves the claim, since $\ell/(\sin(\ell)\,t\cot(t))=C(\ell)/(t\cot(t))$.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lmm:pos:cp:sharp:radius}
    For every $0<R<\pi/2$, no constant smaller than $B_{2R}^{\mathrm{rad}}$ can hold in the contraction property for all probability measures on a closed ball of radius $R$ in the unit $3$-sphere.
    This ball has circumradius exactly $R$.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Choose a maximizer $x\in(0,R)$ in \eqref{eq:pos:cp:radius:optimal}, and put $t:=t_{2R}(x)$.
    In \cref{lmm:pos:cp:sensitivity}, take $\ell=R+x$, an opposite pair $a_1=-a_2$, and a unit vector $v$ orthogonal to $m,e,a_1$.
    Put $o:=\cos(x)\,m+\sin(x)\,e$ and $\mathcal{C}:=\overline B(o,R)$.
    The identities $\left\langleo,y_j\right\rangle=\cos(x)\cos(t)=\cos(R)$ and $\left\langleo,p(0)\right\rangle=\cos(R)$ place all observations on the boundary.
    With $w:=-\sin(x)\,m+\cos(x)\,e$, the curve
    \begin{equation*}
        p(u):=\cos(R)\,o+\sin(R)(\cos(u)\,w+\sin(u)\,v)
    \end{equation*}
    stays on that boundary, is orthogonal to $a_1,a_2$, and satisfies $p(0)=\cos(R+x)\,m+\sin(R+x)\,e$ and $p'(0)=\sin(R)\,v$.
    Hence \eqref{eq:pos:cp:sensitivity} approaches $B_{2R}^{\mathrm{rad}}$ within this fixed ball.
    The ball has diameter $2R$, so its circumradius is $R$.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lmm:pos:cp:sharp:diameter}
    For every $0<\delta<\pi/2$ and $L<B_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}$, there are a compact $\mathsf{CAT}(1)$ space $\mathcal{Q}$ of diameter exactly $\delta$ and circumradius less than $\pi/2$, and distinct measures $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Q})$, such that
    $\overline{m_2(\mu)m_2(\nu)}>L\,W_1(\mu,\nu)$.
    The same conclusion holds for every finite $L$ when $\pi/2\leq\delta<\pi$.
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
First suppose $0<\delta<\pi/2$, and put $\theta:=\theta_\delta$.
    In \cref{lmm:pos:cp:sensitivity}, use $n$ simplex directions and choose $t=t_n$ and $\ell=\theta$, where
    \begin{equation*}
        \cos(t_n)^2=\frac{1+(n-1)\cos(\delta)}n,
        \qquad
        p(u):=\cos(\theta)\,m+\sin(\theta)(\cos(u)\,e+\sin(u)\,v),
    \end{equation*}
    with $v$ a unit vector orthogonal to $m,e,a_1,\ldots,a_n$.
    Distinct base observations have distance $\delta$, and
    $\left\langley_j,p(u)\right\rangle=\cos(t_n)\cos(\theta)\geq\cos(\delta)$.
    For sufficiently small $u\ne0$, the spherical convex hull of the base observations and $p(-u),p(u)$ has diameter exactly $\delta$ and lies in $\overline B(m,\theta)$.
    Indeed, all vertex inner products are at least $\cos(\delta)>0$, and normalization of nonnegative combinations preserves this lower bound, as in \cref{lmm:pos:vi:sharp:diameter}.
    The choices are made in this order: first $n$, then $\epsilon$, then $u$; the space $\mathcal{Q}$ is the convex hull for this $u$, which depends on it, and $\mu,\nu$ are $\eta_{\epsilon,-u},\eta_{\epsilon,u}$.
    Since $\sum_jy_j=n\cos(t_n)m$, the hull contains $m$, so its circumradius is at most $\theta<\pi/2$; and by \cite[Theorem B and Corollary 16]{Yokota2016Barycenter-JMSJ}, the spherical means of $\eta_{\epsilon,\pm u}$ are unique and lie in $\mathcal{Q}$, so they are also the means in $\mathcal{Q}$.
    By \eqref{eq:pos:cp:sensitivity}, followed by $n\to\infty$, these choices give contraction ratios approaching
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{C(\theta)}{t_n\cot(t_n)}
        =\frac1{\cos(\theta)}=B_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}.
    \end{equation*}

    Now fix $\pi/2\leq\delta<\pi$ and take these examples at diameters $\delta_0\nearrow\pi/2$, with contraction ratios tending to infinity.
    Choose $\delta_0$ so that $2\theta_{\delta_0}\geq\delta$.
    The preceding convex hull contains $m$; attach an interval of length $\delta-\theta_{\delta_0}$ there.
    By the gluing theorem \cite[Theorem II.11.1]{BridsonHaefliger1999NPC-Springer}, the resulting space is compact $\mathsf{CAT}(1)$.
    It has diameter exactly $\delta$, attained between the new endpoint and $p(u)$, and is contained in the ball of radius $\theta_{\delta_0}<\pi/2$ about $m$.
    At every point of the added interval, each squared-distance objective is at least its value at $m$, so the original means are unchanged.
    Distances between observations, and hence transport costs, are unchanged as well.
    Thus no finite contraction constant exists at this fixed diameter.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prp:pos:diameterobstruction}
    The following hold.
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item\label{itm:pos:obstruction:vi} For every fixed $\delta\in[\pi/2,\pi)$, no finite constant in the quadratic variance inequality holds uniformly over compact $\mathsf{CAT}(1)$ spaces of diameter exactly $\delta$ and circumradius less than $\pi/2$.
        \item\label{itm:pos:obstruction:risk} For such a $\delta$, there is no constant $C$ depending only on $\delta$ with $\mathbf{E}[d(\hat m_n,m_2(\mu))^2]\leq C\mathbf{V_{\!M}}(\mu)/n$ for all $n\in\mathbb{N}$, all such spaces, and all distributions $\mu$ on them, even for iid samples with uniform weights. For each fixed $n$, the best constant over this class is at least $2n$.
        \item\label{itm:pos:obstruction:order} Each $\mathcal{C}_N$ is a compact $\mathsf{CAT}(1)$ space of diameter $\pi/2$ and circumradius $R_N$. As $N\to\infty$, the optimal radius variance constant $A^{\mathrm{rad}}_{2R_N}$ is of order $\epsilon_N^{-1}$; every coefficient $A_N$ with $\mathbf{E}[d(\hat m_n,m_2(\mu))^2]\leq A_N\mathbf{V_{\!M}}(\mu)/n$ for all $n\in\mathbb{N}$ and all $\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C}_N)$, for iid samples with uniform weights, satisfies $A_N\geq N/4\asymp\epsilon_N^{-2}$; and the refined multiplier $C_\delta A_{\Delta,\delta}B_{\Delta,\delta}$ of \eqref{eq:pos:infty:refined} for $\mathcal{C}_N$ is at most $\pi^2/(4\sin(\epsilon_N)^2)\sim\pi^2/(4\epsilon_N^2)$, whereas $C_\Delta A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}B_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}$ is of exact order $\epsilon_N^{-3}$ and $C_\Delta^4$ of order $\epsilon_N^{-4}$.
        \item\label{itm:pos:obstruction:boundary} The space $\mathcal{C}_\infty$ is a complete $\mathsf{CAT}(1)$ space of diameter and circumradius $\pi/2$. Every finitely supported $\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C}_\infty)$ has a unique $2$-Fr\'echet mean, but neither a finite constant in the quadratic variance inequality nor a finite $C$ with $\mathbf{E}[d(\hat m_n,m_2(\mu))^2]\leq C\mathbf{V_{\!M}}(\mu)/n$ holds uniformly over all finitely supported $\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C}_\infty)$ and $n\in\mathbb{N}$.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- remark-10: body_md -->
\label{rem:pos:obstruction}
    In \ref{itm:pos:obstruction:vi} and \ref{itm:pos:obstruction:risk}, the space must vary: in each individual space of the family, \cref{thm:pos:infty:independent} applies with its own finite factor $C_\Delta^4$.
    By \ref{itm:pos:obstruction:order}, the quadratic divergence of the risk coefficient as $\varrho\sqrt\kappa\nearrow\pi/2$ is order-sharp along a family of fixed diameter, and it is attained by the refined multiplier, which uses the diameter; how the best radius-only multiplier behaves is left open.
    By \ref{itm:pos:obstruction:boundary}, the hypothesis $\varrho<\pi/(2\sqrt\kappa)$ of \cref{ssec:pos:prelim} cannot be weakened to $\varrho\leq\pi/(2\sqrt\kappa)$, even in a fixed space.
    Rescaling gives the corresponding examples for every $\kappa>0$.
<!-- end remark-10 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
\emph{The orthant spaces.}
    Each $\mathcal{C}_N$ is a compact geodesically convex subset of the unit sphere, hence a $\mathsf{CAT}(1)$ space, and $\mathsf{diam}(\mathcal{C}_N)=\pi/2$.
    Its circumradius is $R_N<\pi/2$: the normalized all-ones vector $o_N$ gives the upper bound, while for any unit vector at least one coordinate is at most $1/\sqrt{N+1}$.
    Let $e_1,\dots,e_{N+1}$ be the coordinate vectors and
    \begin{equation*}
        \mu_N:=\frac1N\sum_{j=1}^N\delta_{e_j},\qquad
        m_N:=\frac1{\sqrt N}\sum_{j=1}^N e_j,\qquad
        q_t:=\cos(t)\,m_N+\sin(t)\,e_{N+1}.
    \end{equation*}
    The mean of $\mu_N$ is $m_N$, by Jensen's inequality for the decreasing convex function $g(s)=\arccos(s)^2$, followed by $\sum_{j=1}^N x_j\leq\sqrt N$.
    For $0\leq t\leq\pi/2$ we have $q_t\in \mathcal{C}_N$, $d(q_t,m_N)=t$, and
    \begin{equation*}
        F_N(q_t)=\arccos\!\left( \cos(t)/\sqrt N \right)^{2},\qquad
        F_N(m_N)=\arccos\!\left( 1/\sqrt N \right)^{2},
    \end{equation*}
    where $F_N(q):=\int d(y,q)^2\,\mathrm{d}\mu_N(y)$.
    Consequently
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:orthant:margin}
        \lim_{t\searrow0}\frac{F_N(q_t)-F_N(m_N)}{t^2}
        =\frac{\arccos\!\left( 1/\sqrt N \right)}{\sqrt{N-1}}
        \longrightarrow0
        \qquad(N\to\infty).
    \end{equation}

    \emph{Proof of \ref{itm:pos:obstruction:vi}.}
    For $\delta=\pi/2$, \eqref{eq:pos:orthant:margin} rules out a positive quadratic-growth coefficient depending only on the diameter.
    For $\pi/2<\delta<\pi$, choose $N$ large enough that $2R_N\geq\delta$, and attach an interval of length $L_N:=\delta-R_N\leq R_N$ at $o_N$, with the path metric.
    Gluing $\mathsf{CAT}(1)$ spaces at a point preserves the $\mathsf{CAT}(1)$ property, by Reshetnyak's gluing theorem \cite[Theorem II.11.1]{BridsonHaefliger1999NPC-Springer}.
    The resulting compact space has circumradius $R_N$ and diameter $L_N+R_N=\delta$.
    Every observation $e_j$ has distance $R_N$ from $o_N$, so at a new point at distance $h$ along the interval,
    \begin{equation*}
        F_N(h)=(R_N+h)^2\geq F_N(o_N)\geq F_N(m_N).
    \end{equation*}
    Thus the mean and all variance ratios between original points are unchanged, and the same argument shows that the barycenter of every empirical distribution of observations from $\mu_N$ stays in $\mathcal{C}_N$.

    \emph{Proof of \ref{itm:pos:obstruction:risk}.}
    Take $n$ iid observations from $\mu_N$, $1\leq n\leq N$, in $\mathcal{C}_N$ or in the glued space.
    Every realization is supported on $\{e_j:j\in S\}$ for some $S$ with $\#S\leq n$, which lies in the open ball of radius $r$ about the normalized sum of these $e_j$ for every $r\in(\arccos(1/\sqrt{\#S}),\pi/2)$; so \cite[Corollary 16]{Yokota2016Barycenter-JMSJ} places the empirical barycenter in the closed convex hull of its atoms, and hence in the coordinate face $\{x\in \mathcal{C}_N:x_j=0\text{ for }j\notin S\}$. Cauchy--Schwarz therefore gives $\left\langlem_N,\hat m_n\right\rangle\leq\sqrt{n/N}$, regardless of repeated observations. Consequently
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:orthant:risk}
        d(\hat m_n,m_N)^2\geq\arccos\!\left( \sqrt{n/N} \right)^2
        \quad\text{almost surely}.
    \end{equation}
    Taking $N=n^2$ and letting $n\to\infty$ makes the lower bound tend to $\pi^2/4$, whereas $\mathbf{V_{\!M}}(\mu_N)=\frac{\pi^2}{8}(1-1/N)$ stays bounded, so no diameter-only coefficient is possible.
    Keeping $n$ fixed and letting $N\to\infty$ instead, the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:pos:orthant:risk} again tends to $\pi^2/4$, so any admissible coefficient obeys
    \begin{equation*}
      C\geq\frac{n\,\arccos\!\left( \sqrt{n/N} \right)^2}{\frac{\pi^2}8(1-1/N)}\longrightarrow2n
      \qquad(N\to\infty)
      \,.
    \end{equation*}

    \emph{Proof of \ref{itm:pos:obstruction:order}.}
    All asymptotic comparisons are as $N\to\infty$, and $\pi-2R_N=2\epsilon_N$.
    A valid variance constant for $\mathcal{C}_N$ must be at least the reciprocal of \eqref{eq:pos:orthant:margin}, while $A^{\mathrm{rad}}_\Delta<1/\cos(\Delta/2)$ holds for every $\Delta$ by \eqref{eq:pos:vi:radius:upper}. Hence
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:Arad:order}
        \frac{\sqrt{N-1}}{\arccos\!\left( 1/\sqrt N \right)}
        \leq A^{\mathrm{rad}}_{2R_N}
        <\frac1{\sin(\epsilon_N)}
        \,,
    \end{equation}
    and both bounds are of order $\epsilon_N^{-1}$.
    For the risk coefficient, take $n=\lfloor N/2\rfloor$; then \eqref{eq:pos:orthant:risk} is at least $\pi^2/16$, and $\mathbf{E}[d(\hat m_n,m_N)^2]\leq A_N\mathbf{V_{\!M}}(\mu_N)/n$ forces
    \begin{equation*}
      A_N\geq\frac{n}{2(1-1/N)}\geq\frac N4
      \asymp\epsilon_N^{-2}.
    \end{equation*}
    Here $\delta=\pi/2$, so $A_\delta^{\mathrm{diam}}=+\infty$ and $A_{\Delta,\delta}=1/\cos(\Delta/2)$, and $\Delta=\pi-2\epsilon_N$.
    Since $B_{\Delta,\delta}\leq C_\delta A_{\Delta,\delta}$, the refined multiplier is therefore at most
    \begin{equation*}
      C_\delta^2 A_{\Delta,\delta}^2=\frac{C_\delta^2}{\cos(\Delta/2)^2}=\frac{\pi^2}{4\sin(\epsilon_N)^2}
      \sim\frac{\pi^2}{4\epsilon_N^2}.
    \end{equation*}
    Finally, $C_\Delta=(\pi-2\epsilon_N)/\sin(2\epsilon_N)\asymp\epsilon_N^{-1}$, so $C_\Delta^4\asymp\epsilon_N^{-4}$, and $C_\Delta A_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}B_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}$ is of exact order $\epsilon_N^{-3}$, by \eqref{eq:pos:Arad:order} and $C_\Delta<B_\Delta^{\mathrm{rad}}<2C_\Delta$ from \eqref{eq:pos:cp:radius:upper}.

    \emph{Proof of \ref{itm:pos:obstruction:boundary}.}
    Completeness of $\mathcal{C}_\infty$ follows from equivalence of angular and chordal distances on the unit sphere, and the $\mathsf{CAT}(1)$ comparison reduces, for any triangle, to its finite-dimensional linear span.
    Its diameter is $\pi/2$, as all inner products are nonnegative, and so is its circumradius, because $x_j\to0$ as $j\to\infty$ for every $x\in\ell^2$, so that every point has distance arbitrarily close to $\pi/2$ from some $e_j$.
    The atoms of a finitely supported $\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C}_\infty)$ have nonnegative inner products with each other, so they have positive inner product with their normalized sum and lie in an open ball of radius less than $\pi/2$; hence $\mu$ has a unique mean \cite[Theorem B]{Yokota2016Barycenter-JMSJ}.
    Each $\mathcal{C}_N$ embeds isometrically into $\mathcal{C}_\infty$ as a closed convex face, and the argument in the proof of \ref{itm:pos:obstruction:risk}, applied in $\mathcal{C}_\infty$, shows that the means of $\mu_N$ and of its empirical distributions are the same as in $\mathcal{C}_N$.
    Hence \eqref{eq:pos:orthant:margin} and \eqref{eq:pos:orthant:risk} hold in the fixed space $\mathcal{C}_\infty$, which proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lmm:power:vi}
	The property \eqref{eq:power:vi}\xspace holds.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
This is the power case of the renormalized-objective theorem \cite[Theorem 5.4, p.~22]{Schoetz2025VarianceInequalities-EJP}; its hypothesis is $\mathbf{E}[\tau^\prime(d(Y,o))]<\infty$ for $\tau(t)=t^\alpha$, that is, a finite $(\alpha-1)$-moment, and not finiteness of the unrenormalized objective; when $\alpha<2$, $\tau^{\prime\prime}(0)$ is not finite and is not used.
	The cited theorem evaluates the second-derivative modulus $\tau^{\prime\prime}/2$ at $\max\{\overline{ym},\overline{yq}\}$.
	The triangle inequality bounds this argument by $\overline{ym}+\overline{qm}$, and the nonpositive exponent $\alpha-2$ gives, for $q\neq m$,
	\begin{equation*}
		\int\left( \overline{yq}^\alpha-\overline{ym}^\alpha \right)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
		\geq\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)}2\,\overline{qm}^2\int\left( \overline{ym}+\overline{qm} \right)^{\alpha-2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
		=\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)}2\,\overline{qm}^2\,\Lambda_\alpha\!\left( \mu;\overline{qm} \right)
		\,,
	\end{equation*}
	whose left-hand side is finite by \cref{lmm:power:ana:diff}.
	This is \eqref{eq:power:vi}\xspace; at $q=m$, both sides are zero, which is the continuous extension since $t^2\Lambda_\alpha(\mu;t)\leq t^\alpha$ for $t>0$.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lmm:power:Wtilde}
    Let $\alpha\in(1,2]$ and $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}^{\alpha-1}(\mathcal{Q})$.
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item $\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu,\nu)=\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\nu,\mu)\leq W_{\alpha-1}(\mu,\nu)$, with equality $\widetilde W_1=W_1$ at $\alpha=2$; the infimum in \eqref{eq:power:Wtilde} is attained; and $\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu,\nu)=0$ if and only if $\mu=\nu$.
        \item For $\alpha<2$, $\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}$ violates the triangle inequality: on $\mathcal{Q}=\mathbb{R}$, the laws $\mu=\frac12(\delta_{-1}+\delta_1)$, $\eta=\frac12(\delta_0+\delta_1)$ and $\nu=\delta_0$ satisfy
        \begin{equation*}
            \widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu,\eta)+\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\eta,\nu)
            =\frac12\left( \frac23 \right)^{2-\alpha}+\frac12
            <1=\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu,\nu)
            \,.
        \end{equation*}
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
\emph{(i).} The integrand $c(y,z):=\overline{yz}\,\mathsf{diam}\{y,z,m_\alpha(\mu),m_\alpha(\nu)\}^{\alpha-2}$, read as $0$ at $y=z$, is symmetric in $(\mu,\nu)$ and in $(y,z)$. It satisfies $0\leq c\leq\overline{yz}^{\alpha-1}$ because the diameter is at least $\overline{yz}$, with $c=\overline{yz}$ at $\alpha=2$. It is lower semicontinuous, being continuous off the diagonal and zero on it, and it is positive off the diagonal. The laws are concentrated on separable Borel subsets of the complete space $\mathcal{Q}$, hence tight, so $\Pi(\mu,\nu)$ is weakly compact and $\pi\mapsto\int c\,\mathrm{d}\pi$ is lower semicontinuous; the infimum is attained. If it is $0$, an optimal coupling is concentrated on $\{c=0\}$, the diagonal, so $\mu=\nu$; the converse is the diagonal coupling.

    \emph{(ii).} By symmetry and uniqueness, $m_\alpha(\mu)=m_\alpha(\nu)=0$ and $m_\alpha(\eta)=\frac12$. Since $\nu$ is a Dirac measure, $\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu,\nu)=\frac12(1+1)=1$ and $\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\eta,\nu)=\frac12$. The couplings of $\mu$ and $\eta$ put mass $s\in[0,\frac12]$ on each of $(-1,0),(1,1)$ and $\frac12-s$ on each of $(-1,1),(1,0)$, with costs $(\frac32)^{\alpha-2}$, $0$, $2^{\alpha-1}$ and $1$. The total cost is affine in $s$ and minimal at $s=\frac12$, because $(\frac32)^{\alpha-2}\leq1<2^{\alpha-1}+1$; so $\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu,\eta)=\frac12(\frac23)^{2-\alpha}$, which is less than $\frac12$ for $\alpha<2$.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lmm:power:cp}
	For $\eta\in\mathcal{P}^{\alpha-1}(\mathcal{Q})$, define
	\begin{equation*}
		\Psi_\eta(t) := t\,\Lambda_\alpha\!\left( \eta;t \right)\quad(t>0),\qquad\Psi_\eta(0):=0
		\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Then $\Psi_\eta$ is a continuous, strictly increasing bijection of $\mathbb{R}_{\geq0}$ onto itself, and
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:Psi:scaling}
		\Psi_\eta(ct)\geq c^{\alpha-1}\Psi_\eta(t),\qquad c\geq1,\quad t\geq0.
	\end{equation}
	For all $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}^{\alpha-1}(\mathcal{Q})$, \eqref{eq:power:cp}\xspace reads
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:cp:sharp}
	\begin{aligned}
		\Psi_\mu\!\left( \overline{m_\alpha(\mu)m_\alpha(\nu)} \right) + \Psi_\nu\!\left( \overline{m_\alpha(\mu)m_\alpha(\nu)} \right)
		&\leq
		\frac{2^{3-\alpha}}{\alpha-1}\, \widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu, \nu)
		\,,\\
		\overline{m_\alpha(\mu)m_\alpha(\nu)}
		&\leq
		\Psi_\mu^{-1}\!\left( \frac{2^{3-\alpha}}{\alpha-1}\, \widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu, \nu) \right)
		\,.
	\end{aligned}
	\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
\emph{Step 1: the map $\Psi_\eta$.}
	Let $\eta\in\mathcal{P}^{\alpha-1}(\mathcal{Q})$.
	As $\alpha-2\leq0$, the integrand in \eqref{eq:power:Lambda} is bounded by $t^{\alpha-2}$ for $t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}$, so $\Lambda_\alpha(\eta;t)<\infty$ and $\Psi_\eta(t)\leq t^{\alpha-1}$; in particular $\Psi_\eta(t)\to0 = \Psi_\eta(0)$ as $t\searrow0$.
	Since
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} t}\left( t\,(r+t)^{\alpha-2} \right)
		=
		(r+t)^{\alpha-3}\left( r + (\alpha-1)t \right)
		>
		0
		\,,\qquad r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq0},\ t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
		\,,
	\end{equation*}
	the map $\Psi_\eta$ is strictly increasing, and it is continuous on $\mathbb{R}_{>0}$ by dominated convergence.
	By \eqref{eq:power:cp:jensen} and $(\alpha-1)\phi = 2-\alpha$ we have $\Psi_\eta(t)\geq t(\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\eta)+t^{\alpha-1})^{-\phi}\to\infty$ as $t\to\infty$, so $\Psi_\eta$ is a bijection of $\mathbb{R}_{\geq0}$ onto itself.
	For $c\geq1$ and $t>0$, the inequalities $r+ct\leq c(r+t)$ and $\alpha-2\leq0$ give
	$c t(r+ct)^{\alpha-2}\geq c^{\alpha-1}t(r+t)^{\alpha-2}$.
	Integration with $r=\overline{ym_\alpha(\eta)}$ proves \eqref{eq:power:Psi:scaling}; at $t=0$ both sides vanish.

	\emph{Step 2: the inequality \eqref{eq:power:cp:sharp}.}
	Let $\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)$; the case $m_\alpha(\mu)=m_\alpha(\nu)$ is trivial, so assume that the means differ.
	Applying \eqref{eq:power:vi}\xspace for $\mu$ at $q = m_\alpha(\nu)$ and for $\nu$ at $q = m_\alpha(\mu)$ and adding, we obtain
	\begin{equation*}
	\begin{aligned}
		&\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)}{2}\,\overline{m_\alpha(\mu)m_\alpha(\nu)}^2
		\left( \Lambda_\alpha\!\left( \mu;\overline{m_\alpha(\mu)m_\alpha(\nu)} \right) + \Lambda_\alpha\!\left( \nu;\overline{m_\alpha(\mu)m_\alpha(\nu)} \right) \right)
		\\&\qquad\leq
		\int \overline{ym_\alpha(\nu)}^\alpha - \overline{ym_\alpha(\mu)}^\alpha + \overline{zm_\alpha(\mu)}^\alpha - \overline{zm_\alpha(\nu)}^\alpha \,\mathrm{d}\pi(y,z)
		\,.
	\end{aligned}
	\end{equation*}
	Apply \eqref{eq:power:qi} to the integrand, with $q = m_\alpha(\nu)$ and $p = m_\alpha(\mu)$:
	\begin{equation*}
	\begin{aligned}
		&\overline{ym_\alpha(\nu)}^\alpha - \overline{ym_\alpha(\mu)}^\alpha + \overline{zm_\alpha(\mu)}^\alpha - \overline{zm_\alpha(\nu)}^\alpha
		\\&\qquad\leq
		2^{2-\alpha}\alpha\,\overline{m_\alpha(\mu)m_\alpha(\nu)}\,\overline{yz}\ \mathsf{diam}\left\lbrace y, z, m_\alpha(\mu), m_\alpha(\nu) \right\rbrace^{\alpha-2}
		\,,
	\end{aligned}
	\end{equation*}
	which is $2^{2-\alpha}\alpha\overline{m_\alpha(\mu)m_\alpha(\nu)}$ times the integrand of \eqref{eq:power:Wtilde}.
	Dividing by $\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)}{2}\overline{m_\alpha(\mu)m_\alpha(\nu)}$ and taking the infimum over $\pi$ yields the first inequality of \eqref{eq:power:cp:sharp}, which is \eqref{eq:power:cp}\xspace; the second follows by dropping the nonnegative $\Psi_\nu$ term and inverting the increasing map $\Psi_\mu$ of Step 1.
	Exchanging $\mu$ and $\nu$ gives the same bound with $\Psi_\nu^{-1}$, used below.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- remark-11: body_md -->
\label{rem:power:cp:sharp}
 On $\mathbb{R}$, let $\mu:=\frac12(\delta_{-1}+\delta_1)$ and $\nu_w:=\frac{1-w}2\delta_{-1}+\frac{1+w}2\delta_1$ for $w\in(0,1)$.
 Then $m_\alpha(\mu)=0$, and $D_w:=m_\alpha(\nu_w)\in(0,1)$ solves $((1+D_w)/(1-D_w))^{\alpha-1}=(1+w)/(1-w)$, so $D_w\sim w/(\alpha-1)$ as $w\searrow0$.
 Every coupling moves mass at least $w/2$ from $-1$ to $1$, and moving exactly this mass is optimal, so $\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu,\nu_w)=2^{\alpha-2}w$.
 Since $\Lambda_\alpha(\mu;D_w)$ and $\Lambda_\alpha(\nu_w;D_w)$ tend to $1$, the ratio of the left-hand side of \eqref{eq:power:cp}\xspace to its right-hand side tends to $1$ as $w\searrow0$; the same holds for \eqref{eq:power:cp:moment} at each fixed $\alpha$.
<!-- end remark-11 -->
<!-- remark-12: body_md -->
\label{rem:power:diffuse}
	Let $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}^{\alpha-1}(\mathcal{Q})$ and $\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}$, and suppose some $\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)$ moves no mass further than $\epsilon$, i.e., $\overline{yz}\leq\epsilon$ for $\pi$-almost every $(y,z)$. Then
	\begin{equation*}
		\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu,\nu)
		\leq
		\epsilon\int \overline{ym_\alpha(\mu)}^{\,\alpha-2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)
		\,,
	\end{equation*}
	an \emph{inverse} moment of $\mu$ times $\epsilon$, whereas $W_{\alpha-1}(\mu,\nu)$ is only bounded by $\epsilon^{\alpha-1}$.
	Indeed, $\mathsf{diam}\{y,z,m_\alpha(\mu),m_\alpha(\nu)\}\geq\overline{ym_\alpha(\mu)}$ and $\alpha-2\leq0$ make the integrand of \eqref{eq:power:Wtilde} at most $\overline{yz}\,\overline{ym_\alpha(\mu)}^{\,\alpha-2}\leq\epsilon\,\overline{ym_\alpha(\mu)}^{\,\alpha-2}$ for $\pi$-almost every $(y,z)$, and the first marginal of $\pi$ is $\mu$.
	For $\alpha<2$, this bound is infinite if $\mu$ has an atom at its mean. An always finite form is
	\begin{equation*}
		\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu,\nu)\leq2^{2-\alpha}\min\left\lbrace \Psi_\mu(\epsilon),\Psi_\nu(\epsilon) \right\rbrace
		\,,
	\end{equation*}
	with $\Psi$ as in \cref{lmm:power:cp}: for $r:=\overline{ym_\alpha(\mu)}$, the diameter is at least $\max\{\overline{yz},r\}$, the map $a\mapsto a\max\{a,r\}^{\alpha-2}$ is nondecreasing, and $\max\{\epsilon,r\}\geq(\epsilon+r)/2$, so the integrand is at most $2^{2-\alpha}\epsilon(r+\epsilon)^{\alpha-2}$; the same holds with $\nu$ and the second marginal.
	Consequently, by \eqref{eq:power:Psi:scaling}, in the setting of \cref{cor:power:localfrechet}, if for all $z,z^\prime\in\mathcal{X}$ some coupling of $\mu_z$ and $\mu_{z^\prime}$ moves no mass further than $L^\prime\rho(z,z^\prime)^\beta$, then \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:smooth} holds with $L=2^\phi L^\prime$.
<!-- end remark-12 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lmm:power:cp:onesided}
 Let $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}^{\alpha-1}(\mathcal{Q})$. Then
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:cp:onesided}
 \overline{m_\alpha(\mu)m_\alpha(\nu)}
 \leq c_\alpha\left( \sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\nu)^\phi\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu,\nu)
                         +\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu,\nu)^{1/(\alpha-1)} \right).
 \end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Write $T:=\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu,\nu)$ and $S:=\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\nu)$.
 The case $m_\alpha(\mu)=m_\alpha(\nu)$ is immediate. Otherwise, \cref{lmm:power:cp}, keeping only the term associated with $\nu$, and \eqref{eq:power:cp:jensen} give
 \begin{equation*}
 \frac{\overline{m_\alpha(\mu)m_\alpha(\nu)}}{\left( S+\overline{m_\alpha(\mu)m_\alpha(\nu)}^{\alpha-1} \right)^\phi}\leq c_\alpha T.
 \end{equation*}
 If $\overline{m_\alpha(\mu)m_\alpha(\nu)}^{\alpha-1}\leq S$, then $\overline{m_\alpha(\mu)m_\alpha(\nu)}\leq c_\alpha2^\phi S^\phi T$.
 Otherwise $\overline{m_\alpha(\mu)m_\alpha(\nu)}^{\alpha-1}\leq c_\alpha2^\phi T$, since $(\alpha-1)\phi=2-\alpha$.
 Combining the two cases proves \eqref{eq:power:cp:onesided}.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lmm:power:sufficient}
 Let $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}^{\alpha-1}(\mathcal{Q})$ and $b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}$, and set
 \[
  \bar\sigma:=\max\{\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\mu),\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\nu)\}.
 \]
 \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item\label{lmm:power:sufficient:itm:jensen} If
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:localfrechet:jensen}
   \widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu,\nu)\leq\frac b{\left( \bar\sigma+b^{\alpha-1} \right)^{\phi}}
   \,,
  \end{equation}
  then $\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu,\nu)\leq\min\{\Psi_\mu(b),\Psi_\nu(b)\}$.
  In particular, if \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:jensen} holds for $\mu=\mu_z$, $\nu=\mu_{z^\prime}$, and $b=L\rho(z,z^\prime)^\beta$ for all distinct $z,z^\prime\in\mathcal{X}$, then \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:smooth} holds at every $x\in\mathcal{X}$ with the same $L$.
  \item\label{lmm:power:sufficient:itm:tworegime} Reading $\bar\sigma^{-\phi}b$ as $\infty$ if $\bar\sigma=0$ and $\alpha<2$, the two-regime condition
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:localfrechet:sufficient}
   \widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu,\nu)\leq\min\left\lbrace \bar\sigma^{-\phi}\,b,\ b^{\alpha-1} \right\rbrace
   \,,
  \end{equation}
  implies \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:jensen} with $b$ replaced by $2^{\phi/(\alpha-1)}b$, and hence \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:smooth} with $L$ replaced by $2^{\phi/(\alpha-1)}L$.
 \end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Let $\eta\in\{\mu,\nu\}$, so that $\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\eta)\leq\bar\sigma$. Jensen's bound \eqref{eq:power:cp:jensen} gives
 \[
  \Psi_\eta(b)\geq\frac{b}{\left( \sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\eta)+b^{\alpha-1} \right)^\phi}\geq\frac{b}{\left( \bar\sigma+b^{\alpha-1} \right)^\phi}
  \,,
 \]
 which is \ref{lmm:power:sufficient:itm:jensen}.
 For \ref{lmm:power:sufficient:itm:tworegime}, $\bar\sigma+b^{\alpha-1}\leq2\max(\bar\sigma,b^{\alpha-1})$ and $1-(\alpha-1)\phi=\alpha-1$ give
 \[
 b\left( \bar\sigma+b^{\alpha-1} \right)^{-\phi}
 \geq2^{-\phi}\min\{\bar\sigma^{-\phi}b,b^{\alpha-1}\}.
 \]
 For $c\geq1$, moreover,
 \[
 \frac{cb}{\left( \bar\sigma+(cb)^{\alpha-1} \right)^\phi}
 \geq c^{\alpha-1}\frac{b}{\left( \bar\sigma+b^{\alpha-1} \right)^\phi}.
 \]
 Taking $c=2^{\phi/(\alpha-1)}$ proves the stated rescaling. Conversely, $\bar\sigma+b^{\alpha-1}\geq\max(\bar\sigma,b^{\alpha-1})$ shows that \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:jensen} implies \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:sufficient} without rescaling.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{lmm:power:cp:mixture}
 Let $\nu,\mu_1,\ldots,\mu_N\in\mathcal{P}^{\alpha-1}(\mathcal{Q})$, and let $a_i\geq0$ with $\sum_i a_i=1$.
 Set $\eta:=\sum_i a_i\mu_i$ and
 $\epsilon:=\max_{i:a_i>0}\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu_i,\nu)$.
 With $\Psi_\nu$ as in \cref{lmm:power:cp},
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:cp:mixture}
 \max_{i:a_i>0}\overline{m_\alpha(\mu_i)m_\alpha(\nu)}
 +\overline{m_\alpha(\eta)m_\alpha(\nu)}
 \leq c_\alpha\Psi_\nu^{-1}(\epsilon).
 \end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Write $m_0:=m_\alpha(\nu)$, $m_i:=m_\alpha(\mu_i)$, $m_*:=m_\alpha(\eta)$,
 and $R:=\max_{i:a_i>0}\overline{m_im_0}$.
 By \cref{lmm:power:cp},
 $R\leq\Psi_\nu^{-1}(\frac{2^{3-\alpha}}{\alpha-1}\epsilon)\leq c_\alpha\Psi_\nu^{-1}(\epsilon)$,
 where the second inequality follows from \eqref{eq:power:Psi:scaling}.
 If $\overline{m_*m_0}\leq R$, this proves the assertion.
 Otherwise $\overline{m_*m_0}>R$, and, for every active $i$ and every $y,z$,
 \begin{equation*}
 \mathsf{diam}\left\lbrace y,z,m_i,m_0 \right\rbrace
 \leq\mathsf{diam}\left\lbrace y,z,m_*,m_0 \right\rbrace+R
 \leq2\mathsf{diam}\left\lbrace y,z,m_*,m_0 \right\rbrace.
 \end{equation*}
 Since $\alpha-2\leq0$, mixing approximately optimal couplings of $\mu_i$ and $\nu$ gives
 \begin{equation*}
 \widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\eta,\nu)
 \leq2^{2-\alpha}\sum_{i=1}^{N} a_i\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu_i,\nu)
 \leq2^{2-\alpha}\epsilon.
 \end{equation*}
 Applying \cref{lmm:power:cp} to $\eta,\nu$ gives
 $\overline{m_*m_0}\leq\Psi_\nu^{-1}(\frac{2^{5-2\alpha}}{\alpha-1}\epsilon)\leq c_\alpha\Psi_\nu^{-1}(\epsilon)$,
 again by \eqref{eq:power:Psi:scaling}.
 Combining the bounds on $\overline{m_*m_0}$ and $R$ proves \eqref{eq:power:cp:mixture}.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
\emph{Upper bound.}
	For every $q\in\mathcal{Q}$ we have $\mathbf{V}^{s}_{\!F}(\mu)\leq\int\overline{yq}^{s}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)$.
	Integrating over $q$ with respect to $\mu$ gives $\mathbf{V}^{s}_{\!F}(\mu)\leq\iint\overline{yq}^{s}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(q) = 2\mathbf{V}^{s}_{\!M}(\mu)$.

	\emph{Lower bound.}
	Let $q\in\mathcal{Q}$. By the triangle inequality and \cref{lmm:power:subadd:gen},
	$\overline{yy^\prime}^{s}\leq(\overline{yq} + \overline{y\prq})^{s}\leq2^{\max(0,s-1)}(\overline{yq}^{s} + \overline{y\prq}^{s})$.
	Integrating over $\mu\otimes\mu$ yields $2\mathbf{V}^{s}_{\!M}(\mu)\leq2^{\max(0,s-1)}\cdot2\int\overline{yq}^{s}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)$, and taking the infimum over $q$ gives $\mathbf{V}^{s}_{\!M}(\mu)\leq2^{\max(0,s-1)}\mathbf{V}^{s}_{\!F}(\mu)$, which is the first inequality of \eqref{eq:variances:comparison} because $-\max(0,s-1) = \min(1-s,0)$.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- remark-13: body_md -->
\label{rem:app:variances:sharp}
 The upper constant is attained on the two-point space $\{a,b\}$ with $\overline{ab}=1$ and $\mu$ uniform, where $\mathbf{V}^{s}_{\!F}(\mu)=\frac12$ and $\mathbf{V}^{s}_{\!M}(\mu)=\frac14$.
 For $s\geq1$, the lower constant is approached on a star $\mathbb{R}$-tree with $n\geq2$ unit-length edges and $\mu$ uniform on the leaves. For a point at distance $r\in[0,1]$ from the center, Jensen's inequality gives $\frac1n(1-r)^s+\frac{n-1}n(1+r)^s\geq(1+\frac{n-2}nr)^s\geq1$, so the center minimizes the $s$-objective, and the ratio $\mathbf{V}^{s}_{\!F}(\mu)/\mathbf{V}^{s}_{\!M}(\mu)=2^{1-s}(1-1/n)^{-1}$ tends to $2^{1-s}$ as $n\to\infty$.
 For $0<s<1$, take $\mu=(1-\epsilon)\delta_a+\epsilon\delta_b$ on the two-point space, with $0<\epsilon<1/2$. The ratio is then $(1-\epsilon)^{-1}\to1$ as $\epsilon\searrow0$, proving sharpness of the lower constant in this range as well.
<!-- end remark-13 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{lmm:two:variances}
 For $Y\sim\mu\in\mathcal{P}^2(\mathcal{Q})$ in a Hadamard space,
 $\mathbf{V_{\!F}}[Y]\leq\mathbf{V_{\!M}}[Y]\leq2\mathbf{V_{\!F}}[Y]$. If $\mathcal{Q}$ is a Hilbert space, then $\mathbf{V_{\!F}}[Y]=\mathbf{V_{\!M}}[Y]$.
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Put $m:=m_2(\mu)$ and let $Y^\prime$ be an independent copy of $Y$.
 Integrating \eqref{eq:cat0:2fm:vi} at $q=Y^\prime$ gives
 $\mathbf{V_{\!F}}[Y]\leq2\mathbf{V_{\!M}}[Y]-\mathbf{V_{\!F}}[Y]$, proving the first inequality.
 The second is \cref{lmm:variances} at $s=2$.
 In a Hilbert space, $m=\mathbf{E}[Y]$, and independence gives
 $\mathbf{E}[\VertY-Y^\prime\Vert^2]=2\mathbf{E}[\VertY-m\Vert^2]$ by expanding the squared norm; hence the two variances agree.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- notation-1: body_md -->
\label{ntt:power:proof}
 We write $\sigma_s:=\sum_jw_j\mathbf{E}[\overline{Y_j\bar m}^s]$ for the $s$-th moment of $\bar\mu$ about $\bar m$, as in \eqref{eq:power:sigma}.
	For $i\in\mathbb{N}$, let
	\begin{equation*}
		\hat\mu^i := w_i\, \delta_{Y_i^\prime} + \sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\left\lbrace i \right\rbrace} w_j\, \delta_{Y_j}
		\qquad\text{and}\qquad
		\hat m^i := m_\alpha(\hat\mu^i)
		\,,
	\end{equation*}
	the sample and the sample mean after resampling the $i$-th observation, and define the empirical moments
 \begin{align*}
  \hat\sigma_s&:=\sum_{j=1}^\infty w_j\,\overline{Y_j\bar m}^s,\\
  \hat\sigma_s^i&:=w_i\,\overline{Y_i^\prime\bar m}^s
       +\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\left\lbrace i \right\rbrace}w_j\,\overline{Y_j\bar m}^s,\\
  \hat\sigma_s^{-i}&:=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\left\lbrace i \right\rbrace}w_j\,\overline{Y_j\bar m}^s,
 \end{align*}
	the last of which is independent of $(Y_i, Y_i^\prime)$.
	Note $\mathbf{E}[\hat\sigma_s] = \mathbf{E}[\hat\sigma_s^i] = \sigma_s$ and $\hat\sigma_s^{-i} \leq \hat\sigma_s$.
<!-- end notation-1 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lmm:loo}
	Assume $\bar\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{\alpha}(\mathcal{Q})$ and one of the following.
	\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
		\item\label{lmm:loo:itm:hadamard} $(\mathcal{Q},d)$ is a Hadamard space; put $C_{\mathrm{QI}}:=2^{2-\alpha}\alpha$.
		\item\label{lmm:loo:itm:catk} $\alpha=2$ and, in the setting of \cref{ssec:pos:prelim}, all $\mu_i$ lie in $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$; put $C_{\mathrm{QI}}:=2C_\Delta$, or $C_{\mathrm{QI}}:=2C_\delta$ by \cref{rem:pos:sharper}.
	\end{enumerate}
	Then $\bar m$, $\hat m$, and $\hat m^i$, $i\in\mathbb{N}$, exist and are unique almost surely, and
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:loo}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \mathcal E_{\bar\mu}(\hat m) \right]+\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \mathcal E_{\hat\mu}(\bar m) \right]
		\leq
		\frac{C_{\mathrm{QI}}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i\, \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{\alpha-1}\, \overline{\hat m\hat m^i} \right]
		\,.
	\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
\emph{Existence and integrability.}
	In case \ref{lmm:loo:itm:catk}, all distances are bounded by $\mathsf{diam}(\mathcal{C})$, so every sum and expectation below is finite and may be interchanged freely, and $\bar\mu,\hat\mu,\hat\mu^i\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$ have unique means in $\mathcal{C}$ by \cref{ssec:pos:prelim}.
	In case \ref{lmm:loo:itm:hadamard}, we have $\sigma_{\alpha-1}<\infty$, as $\bar\mu\in\mathcal{P}^\alpha(\mathcal{Q})$, and $\bar m$ exists uniquely.
	As $\mathbf{E}[\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}] = \sigma_{\alpha-1}$, we have $\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1} < \infty$ almost surely, i.e., $\hat\mu \in \mathcal{P}^{\alpha-1}(\mathcal{Q})$ almost surely; the separability requirement in the definition of $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Q})$ holds, as all $Y_j$ with $w_j > 0$ almost surely take values in the union of the separable Borel sets on which the corresponding $\mu_j$ are concentrated, cf.\ \cref{rem:separability}.
	Hence, $\hat m$ and, analogously, $\hat m^i$ exist and are unique almost surely.
	They are also measurable functions of the observations, as required by the independence and conditioning steps below.
	Indeed, replacing every coordinate after the $N$-th by a reference point $o$, with $o\in\mathcal{C}$ in case \ref{lmm:loo:itm:catk}, gives means that depend on finitely many observations and are continuous in them, by \eqref{eq:cat0:2fm:CP} in case \ref{lmm:loo:itm:hadamard} with $\alpha=2$, by \eqref{eq:2fmcatk:CP}\xspace in case \ref{lmm:loo:itm:catk}, and by \cref{lmm:power:cp:onesided} in case \ref{lmm:loo:itm:hadamard} with $\alpha<2$. The truncation couplings send the tail to $o$, so the corresponding transport costs tend to zero almost surely; in case \ref{lmm:loo:itm:catk}, \eqref{eq:2fmcatk:CP}\xspace turns this directly into almost sure convergence of the finite-coordinate means to $\hat m$, respectively $\hat m^i$, and in case \ref{lmm:loo:itm:hadamard} so does \cref{lmm:power:cp:onesided}, applied with $\nu=\hat\mu$, respectively $\nu=\hat\mu^i$, fixed. Step 7 of the proof of \cref{prp:power:excess} carries out the same computation in detail.
	In case \ref{lmm:loo:itm:hadamard}, \eqref{eq:power:dirac} applied to $\hat\mu$ and $\hat\mu^i$ with $q = \bar m$ gives
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:rb}
		\overline{\hat m\bar m}^{\alpha-1} \leq c_\alpha\, \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}
		\qquad\text{and}\qquad
		\overline{\hat m^i\bar m}^{\alpha-1} \leq c_\alpha\, \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^i
		\qquad\mathsf{a.s.}
	\end{equation}
	As $\frac{1}{\alpha-1} \geq 1$ and $\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1} = \phi + 2 \geq 2$, Jensen's inequality with weights $(w_j)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}$ yields
	$\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\frac{1}{\alpha-1}} \leq \sum_{j=1}^\infty w_j\, \overline{Y_j\bar m}$ and $\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}} \leq \sum_{j=1}^\infty w_j\, \overline{Y_j\bar m}^{\alpha}$.
	Together with \eqref{eq:power:rb} and $\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}\, \overline{\hat m\bar m} \leq c_\alpha\, \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}}$, this implies
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:integra}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\hat m\bar m} \right] \leq c_\alpha\, \sigma_1
		\,,\qquad
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\hat m\bar m}^{\alpha} \right] \leq c_\alpha\, \sigma_\alpha
		\,,\qquad
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}\, \overline{\hat m\bar m} \right] \leq c_\alpha\, \sigma_\alpha
		\,,
	\end{equation}
	all finite; the same bounds hold with $\hat m^i$ and $\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^i$ in place of $\hat m$ and $\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}$.
	By the mean value theorem, the triangle inequality, and \cref{lmm:power:subadd:gen}, for $y \in \mathcal{Q}$ and $u \in \{\hat m, \hat m^i\}$,
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:mvt}
		\left| \overline{yu}^{\alpha} - \overline{y\bar m}^{\alpha} \right|
		\leq
		\alpha\, \overline{u\bar m} \left( \overline{y\bar m}^{\alpha-1} + \overline{u\bar m}^{\alpha-1} \right)
		=
		\alpha \left( \overline{u\bar m}\, \overline{y\bar m}^{\alpha-1} + \overline{u\bar m}^{\alpha} \right)
		\,.
	\end{equation}
	Combining \eqref{eq:power:integra} and \eqref{eq:power:mvt}, and using that $(Y_j^\prime)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}$ is independent of $\hat m$,
	\begin{align}
		\sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i\, \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \left| \overline{Y_i^\prime\hat m}^{\alpha} - \overline{Y_i^\prime\bar m}^{\alpha} \right| \right]
		&\leq
		\alpha \left( \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\hat m\bar m} \right]\, \sigma_{\alpha-1} + \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\hat m\bar m}^{\alpha} \right] \right)
		< \infty
		\,,
		\label{eq:power:dom1}
		\\
		\sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i\, \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \left| \overline{Y_i\hat m}^{\alpha} - \overline{Y_i\bar m}^{\alpha} \right| \right]
		&\leq
		\alpha \left( \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}\, \overline{\hat m\bar m} \right] + \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\hat m\bar m}^{\alpha} \right] \right)
		< \infty
		\,.
		\label{eq:power:dom2}
	\end{align}
	Moreover, for every $i \in \mathbb{N}$ with $w_i > 0$ --- the only indices that contribute to any sum below --- using $\overline{Zu}^{\alpha} \leq 2^{\alpha-1}(\overline{Z\bar m}^{\alpha} + \overline{u\bar m}^{\alpha})$, \eqref{eq:power:integra}, and $w_i\, \mathbf{E}[\overline{Y_i\bar m}^{\alpha}] \leq \sigma_\alpha$,
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:individ}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Zu}^{\alpha} \right] < \infty
		\qquad\text{for all } Z \in \left\lbrace Y_i, Y_i^\prime \right\rbrace \text{ and } u \in \left\lbrace \hat m, \hat m^i \right\rbrace
		\,.
	\end{equation}
	In case \ref{lmm:loo:itm:catk}, the bounds \eqref{eq:power:dom1}, \eqref{eq:power:dom2}, and \eqref{eq:power:individ} hold trivially.

	\emph{The main part.}
	By the definition of excess risk, using that $(Y_i^\prime)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}$ is independent of $(Y_j)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}$,
	\begin{equation*}
