		\mathcal E_{\bar\mu}(\hat m)
		=
		\sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i\, \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_i^\prime\hat m}^{\alpha} - \overline{Y_i^\prime\bar m}^{\alpha} \,\middle|\, (Y_j)_{j\in\mathbb{N}} \right]
		\qquad\text{and}\qquad
		\mathcal E_{\hat\mu}(\bar m)
		=
		\sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i \left( \overline{Y_i\bar m}^{\alpha} - \overline{Y_i\hat m}^{\alpha} \right)
		\,,
	\end{equation*}
	where the second series converges absolutely almost surely by \eqref{eq:power:dom2}.
	Taking expectations --- the interchange of expectation and summation is justified by \eqref{eq:power:dom1} and \eqref{eq:power:dom2} --- and using $\mathbf{E}[\overline{Y_i^\prime\bar m}^{\alpha}] = \mathbf{E}[\overline{Y_i\bar m}^{\alpha}]$, we obtain
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:afterVI}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \mathcal E_{\bar\mu}(\hat m) \right]+\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \mathcal E_{\hat\mu}(\bar m) \right]
		=
		\sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i\, \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_i^\prime\hat m}^{\alpha} - \overline{Y_i\hat m}^{\alpha} \right]
		\,.
	\end{equation}
	It remains to bound the individual summands.
	We apply the quadruple inequality with $q = \hat m$, $p = \hat m^i$, $y = Y_i^\prime$, and $z = Y_i$: in case \ref{lmm:loo:itm:hadamard} this is \eqref{eq:power:qi}\xspace, where we bound $\mathsf{diam}\{Y_i, Y_i^\prime, \hat m, \hat m^i\}\geq \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}$ and use $\alpha-2\leq0$; in case \ref{lmm:loo:itm:catk} it is \eqref{eq:2fmcatk:qi}, all four points lying in $\mathcal{C}$.
	Either way,
	\begin{equation*}
		\overline{Y_i^\prime\hat m}^\alpha - \overline{Y_i\hat m}^\alpha
		\leq
		\overline{Y_i^\prime\hat m^i}^\alpha - \overline{Y_i\hat m^i}^\alpha
		+ C_{\mathrm{QI}}\, \overline{\hat m\hat m^i}\, \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{\alpha-1}
		\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Exchanging $Y_i$ and $Y_i^\prime$ leaves the joint distribution of the two sequences invariant and maps $(\hat m, \hat m^i)$ to $(\hat m^i, \hat m)$; hence $(Y_i^\prime, \hat m^i)$ has the same law as $(Y_i, \hat m)$, and $(Y_i, \hat m^i)$ the same law as $(Y_i^\prime, \hat m)$.
	Taking expectations --- all terms are integrable by \eqref{eq:power:individ} --- therefore yields
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_i^\prime\hat m}^{\alpha} - \overline{Y_i\hat m}^{\alpha} \right]
		\leq
		\frac{C_{\mathrm{QI}}}{2}\, \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{\alpha-1}\, \overline{\hat m\hat m^i} \right]
		\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Inserting this into \eqref{eq:power:afterVI} proves \eqref{eq:loo}.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
Fix $o\in\mathcal{Q}$. Tonelli's theorem and $\bar\mu\in\mathcal{P}^1(\mathcal{Q})$ give
    \[
     \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \sum_{i=1}^{\infty}w_i\overline{Y_io} \right]=\int\overline{yo}\,\mathrm{d}\bar\mu(y)<\infty.
    \]
    Hence $\hat\mu\in\mathcal{P}^1(\mathcal{Q})$ almost surely and both means exist.
    The empirical mean is measurable: replace all coordinates after $N$ by $o$; the resulting finite-coordinate means are continuous in the observations by $W_1$ contraction and converge almost surely to $\hat m$ by the tail estimate below.
    If $\sum_iw_i^2\mathbf{V_{\!M}}[Y_i]=\infty$, the claimed inequality is immediate.

    Assume the right-hand side is finite. Every active component then has a finite second moment, since $\mathbf{V_{\!F}}[Y_i]\leq2\mathbf{V_{\!M}}[Y_i]<\infty$ whenever $w_i>0$ by \cref{lmm:variances}.
    Put $r_N:=\sum_{i>N}w_i$ and
    \[
     \bar\mu_N:=\sum_{i=1}^Nw_i\mu_i+r_N\delta_o,\qquad
     \hat\mu_N:=\sum_{i=1}^Nw_i\delta_{Y_i}+r_N\delta_o,
    \]
    omitting zero-weight terms. Then $\bar\mu_N\in\mathcal{P}^2(\mathcal{Q})$.
    For independent copies $Y_i^\prime$, let $\hat\mu_N^i$ replace the $i$-th atom of $\hat\mu_N$ by $Y_i^\prime$.
    Apply \cref{lmm:loo}\,\ref{lmm:loo:itm:hadamard} to this finite sample, including the deterministic coordinate at $o$ whose resampling displacement is zero.
    With $\alpha=2$, so that $C_{\mathrm{QI}}=2$, the two variance inequalities \eqref{eq:cat0:2fm:vi} give
    \[
     2\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{m_2(\bar\mu_N)m_2(\hat\mu_N)}^2 \right]
     \leq\sum_{i=1}^Nw_i\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}\,
                    \overline{m_2(\hat\mu_N)m_2(\hat\mu_N^i)} \right].
    \]
    The coupling that moves only the $i$-th atom and \eqref{eq:cat0:2fm:CP} imply
    $\overline{m_2(\hat\mu_N)m_2(\hat\mu_N^i)}\leq w_i\overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}$.
    Therefore
    \[
     \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{m_2(\bar\mu_N)m_2(\hat\mu_N)}^2 \right]
     \leq\frac12\sum_{i=1}^Nw_i^2\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^2 \right]
     =\sum_{i=1}^Nw_i^2\mathbf{V_{\!M}}\!\left[ Y_i \right].
    \]
    As $N\to\infty$, the couplings that send the tail to $o$ give
    \[
     W_1(\bar\mu_N,\bar\mu)\leq\sum_{i=N+1}^{\infty}w_i\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_io} \right]\longrightarrow0,\qquad
     W_1(\hat\mu_N,\hat\mu)\leq\sum_{i=N+1}^{\infty}w_i\overline{Y_io}\longrightarrow0
     \quad\text{almost surely}.
    \]
    Contraction implies convergence of the two means.
    Fatou's lemma proves \eqref{eq:two:infty:independent} with constant one.

    In a Hilbert space, $\sum_iw_iY_i$ converges absolutely almost surely and in $L^1$.
    If the weighted variance sum is finite, the centered partial sums converge in $L^2$ and their second moments equal $\sum_{i\leq N}w_i^2\mathbf{V_{\!M}}[Y_i]$, proving equality.
    If the variance sum is infinite, write $S=\hat m-\bar m$.
    Were $\mathbf{E}[\VertS\Vert^2]<\infty$, conditional Jensen applied to
    $\mathbf{E}[S\mid Y_1,\ldots,Y_N]=\sum_{i\leq N}w_i(Y_i-\mathbf{E}[Y_i])$ would bound every partial variance sum by $\mathbf{E}[\VertS\Vert^2]$, a contradiction.
    Thus equality holds also with extended values.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Write $w_i:=w_{i,h}(x)$ and $\bar m(x):=m_2(\sum_iw_i\mu_{x_i})$.
 By \eqref{eq:localfrechet:design}, $N_n(x,h/2)>0$, so the weights are well-defined, sum to one, and vanish unless $\rho(x_i,x)\leq h$.
 The contraction property \eqref{eq:cat0:2fm:CP}, the coupling that couples $\mu_{x_i}$ with $\mu_x$ optimally in each component, and \eqref{eq:two:localreg:smooth} give
 \[
  \overline{\bar m(x)m_2(\mu_x)}
  \leq W_1\!\left( \sum_{i=1}^{n}w_i\mu_{x_i},\mu_x \right)
  \leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_iW_1(\mu_{x_i},\mu_x)
  \leq L\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_i\rho(x_i,x)^\beta
  \leq Lh^\beta.
 \]
 By \cref{thm:two:infty:independent},
 $\mathbf{E}[\overline{\bar m(x)\hat m_h\!(x)}^2]\leq\sum_iw_i^2\mathbf{V_{\!M}}[Y_i]\leq v\Vertw\Vert_2^2$, and the kernel bounds \eqref{eq:localfrechet:kernel} together with \eqref{eq:localfrechet:design} give
 \[
  \left\Vertw\right\Vert_2^2
  =\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}K(\rho(x_i,x)/h)^2}{\left( \sum_{j=1}^{n}K(\rho(x_j,x)/h) \right)^2}
  \leq\frac{\max_iK(\rho(x_i,x)/h)}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}K(\rho(x_j,x)/h)}
  \leq\frac{C_{\mathsf{Kma}}}{C_{\mathsf{Kmi}}^{-1}N_n(x,h/2)}
  \leq\frac{C_{\mathsf{Kmi}}C_{\mathsf{Kma}}C_{\mathsf{dns}}}{nh^D}.
 \]
 The triangle inequality in $L^2$ proves \eqref{eq:localfrechet:designbound}.
 For $v>0$, the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:localfrechet:designbound} is $h\mapsto Lh^\beta+Ah^{-D/2}$ with $A:=\sqrt{vC_{\mathsf{Kmi}}C_{\mathsf{Kma}}C_{\mathsf{dns}}/n}$; its derivative is negative for small and positive for large $h$ and vanishes only at the $h_*$ determined by $\beta Lh_*^\beta=\frac D2Ah_*^{-D/2}$, which gives \eqref{eq:localfrechet:bandwidth}.
 At $h_*$, the stochastic term $Ah_*^{-D/2}$ equals $\frac{2\beta}D$ times the bias term $Lh_*^\beta$, so the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:localfrechet:designbound} equals $\frac{2\beta+D}{D}Lh_*^\beta$; squaring and inserting \eqref{eq:localfrechet:bandwidth} proves \eqref{eq:localfrechet:optimal}.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
Let $(Y_i^\prime)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}$, $\hat\mu^i$ and $\hat m^i$ be as in \cref{ntt:power:proof}.
	By \cref{lmm:loo}\,\ref{lmm:loo:itm:catk} with $C_{\mathrm{QI}}=2C_\Delta$, and by \eqref{eq:2fmcatk:vi}\xspace applied to both excess risks in \eqref{eq:loo},
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:pos:first}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\bar m\hat m}^2 \right]
		\leq
		\frac{C_\Delta^2}2\sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i\, \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}\, \overline{\hat m\hat m^i} \right]
		\,.
	\end{equation}
	The coupling that is the identity on all points except the $i$-th gives $W_1(\hat\mu,\hat\mu^i)\leq w_i\overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}$, so that \eqref{eq:2fmcatk:CP}\xspace yields $\overline{\hat m\hat m^i}\leq C_\Delta^2\, w_i\, \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}$.
	Hence
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\bar m\hat m}^2 \right]
		\leq
		\frac{C_\Delta^4}2\sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i^2\, \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^2 \right]
		=
		C_\Delta^4\sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i^2\, \mathbf{V_{\!M}}\!\left[ Y_i \right]
		\,.
	\end{equation*}
    To obtain \eqref{eq:pos:infty:refined}, use $A_{\Delta,\delta}$ in both variance inequalities and $C_{\mathrm{QI}}=2C_\delta$ in \cref{lmm:loo}, so that the prefactor in \eqref{eq:pos:first} becomes $C_\delta A_{\Delta,\delta}/2$.
    The refined contraction bound gives $\overline{\hat m\hat m^i}\leq B_{\Delta,\delta}w_i\overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}$, and the same calculation yields the multiplier $C_\delta A_{\Delta,\delta}B_{\Delta,\delta}$.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
Replacing observation $i$ changes $\overline{\hat m\bar m}$ by at most $r_i:=C_\Delta^2w_i\mathsf{diam}(\mathcal{C})$, by \eqref{eq:2fmcatk:CP}.
    The Doob martingale $M_N:=\mathbf{E}[\overline{\hat m\bar m}\mid Y_1,\ldots,Y_N]$, with $M_0:=\mathbf{E}[\overline{\hat m\bar m}]$, has conditional increment ranges of lengths at most $r_i$. Iterating the conditional Hoeffding bound gives
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \exp\left( \lambda(M_N-\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\samfm\popfm} \right]) \right) \right]
        \leq\exp\left( \frac{\lambda^2}{8}\sum_{i=1}^N r_i^2 \right),
        \qquad \lambda>0,\quad N\in\mathbb{N}.
    \end{equation*}
    Since $0\leq M_N\leq \mathsf{diam}(\mathcal{C})$ and $M_N\to \overline{\hat m\bar m}$ almost surely as $N\to\infty$, dominated convergence gives the same bound with $M_N$ replaced by $\overline{\hat m\bar m}$ and the sum extended to infinity.
    Here $\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}r_i^2<\infty$ because $\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}w_i^2\leq1$.
    Exponential Markov and optimization over $\lambda$ yield
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbf{P}\!\left( \overline{\hat m\bar m}-\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\hat m\bar m} \right]\geq t \right)
        \leq\exp\left( -\frac{2t^2}{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty} r_i^2} \right),
        \qquad t>0.
    \end{equation*}
    Finally, \cref{thm:pos:infty:independent} gives
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\hat m\bar m} \right]\leq\left( \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\hat m\bar m}^2 \right] \right)^{1/2}
        \leq C_\Delta^2\left( \sum_{i=1}^{\infty} w_i^2\mathbf{V_{\!M}}\!\left[ Y_i \right] \right)^{1/2}.
    \end{equation*}
    Since $\sum_{i=1}^{\infty} r_i^2=C_\Delta^4\mathsf{diam}(\mathcal{C})^2\Vertw\Vert_2^2$, taking
    $t=C_\Delta^2\mathsf{diam}(\mathcal{C})\sqrt{\Vertw\Vert_2^2\log(1/\eta)/2}$ proves \eqref{eq:pos:concentration}.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
Write $w_i:=w_{i,h}(x)$ and $\bar m(x) := m_2(\sum_i w_i\mu_{x_i})$; by \eqref{eq:localfrechet:design}, the weights are well-defined and sum to one.
	By \eqref{eq:2fmcatk:CP}\xspace, the coupling that maps $\mu_{x_i}$ to $\mu_x$ optimally in each component, and \eqref{eq:two:localreg:smooth},
	\begin{equation*}
	\begin{aligned}
		\overline{\bar m(x)m_2(\mu_x)}
		&\leq
		C_\Delta^2\, W_1\!\left( \sum_{i=1}^{n} w_i\mu_{x_i},\ \mu_x \right)
		\leq
		C_\Delta^2\sum_{i=1}^{n} w_i\, W_1(\mu_{x_i},\mu_x)
		\\&\leq
		C_\Delta^2\, L\sum_{i=1}^{n} w_i\rho(x_i,x)^{\beta}
		\leq
		C_\Delta^2\, L\, h^{\beta}
		\,,
	\end{aligned}
	\end{equation*}
	because $w_i = 0$ unless $\rho(x_i,x)\leq h$.
	By \cref{thm:pos:infty:independent}, $\mathbf{E}[\overline{\bar m(x)\hat m_h\!(x)}^2]\leq C_\Delta^4\sum_i w_i^2\mathbf{V_{\!M}}[Y_i]\leq C_\Delta^4 v\Vertw\Vert_2^2$, and $v\leq\mathsf{diam}(\mathcal{C})^2/2<\infty$.
	The triangle inequality in $L^2$ and the bound $\Vertw\Vert_2^2\leqC_{\mathsf{Kmi}}C_{\mathsf{Kma}}C_{\mathsf{dns}}/(nh^D)$ from the proof of \cref{cor:two:localreg} give \eqref{eq:pos:localfrechet}.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prp:power:excess}
	In the setting of \cref{ssec:main:bound}, there is a constant $c_\alpha$ depending only on $\alpha$ such that
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:excess:bound}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \mathcal E_{\bar\mu}(\hat m) \right]
		\leq c_\alpha\left( \sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\,\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}+\Sigma_\alpha \right).
	\end{equation}
	Consequently, with the inverse moment \eqref{eq:power:Lambda},
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:main}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ g(\overline{\bar m\hat m}) \right]
		\leq \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\bar m\hat m}^2\Lambda_\alpha\!\left( \bar\mu;\overline{\bar m\hat m} \right) \right]
		\leq
		c_\alpha \left( \sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\, \Sigma_{2\alpha-2} + \Sigma_\alpha \right)
		\,.
	\end{equation}
	The product $\overline{\bar m\hat m}^2\Lambda_\alpha(\bar\mu;\overline{\bar m\hat m})$ is zero at $\overline{\bar m\hat m}=0$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{lmm:power:interpol}
	In the setting of \cref{ssec:main:bound}, let $s_0,s_1>0$ and $\lambda\in[0,1]$. Then
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:interpol}
		\Sigma_{\lambda s_0 + (1-\lambda)s_1}
		\leq
		\Sigma_{s_0}^{\lambda}\; \Sigma_{s_1}^{1-\lambda}
		\,,
	\end{equation}
	i.e., the moment family is log-convex on every interval of finite positive moments. If all moments vanish, read the assertion in its displayed multiplicative form.
	In particular,
	\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
		\item \label{lmm:power:interpol:itm:main}
		$\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}^{1+\phi} \leq \Sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\, \Sigma_{\alpha}$;
		\item \label{lmm:power:interpol:itm:chord}
		if $\phi \geq 2$, then $\Sigma_{2-\alpha}^{\phi}\, \Sigma_{2\alpha-2}^{\phi-1} \leq \Sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\, \Sigma_{\alpha}^{\phi-1}$.
	\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
For all $s>0$, $\Sigma_s=\frac12\sum_{i=1}^\infty\mathbf{E}[(w_i^{1/(\alpha-1)}\overline{Y_iY_i^\prime})^s]$.
	Writing $s := \lambda s_0 + (1-\lambda)s_1$ and applying H\"older's inequality first within each expectation and then to the sum over $i$ with the weights $\frac12$,
 \begin{align*}
  \Sigma_s
  &=\frac12\sum_{i=1}^\infty
       w_i^{s/(\alpha-1)}\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{\lambda s_0+(1-\lambda)s_1} \right]\\
  &\leq\frac12\sum_{i=1}^\infty
       \left( w_i^{s_0/(\alpha-1)}\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{s_0} \right] \right)^{\lambda}
       \left( w_i^{s_1/(\alpha-1)}\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{s_1} \right] \right)^{1-\lambda}\\
  &\leq\Sigma_{s_0}^{\lambda}\,\Sigma_{s_1}^{1-\lambda}
  \,,
 \end{align*}
	which is \eqref{eq:power:interpol}; the endpoint cases $\lambda=0,1$ are identities.
	No finiteness hypothesis is needed: the H\"older chain is valid in $[0,\infty]$, and the only indeterminate product it could produce is excluded, since $\Sigma_{s_0}=0$ or $\Sigma_{s_1}=0$ forces $w_i^{1/(\alpha-1)}\overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}=0$ almost surely for every $i$ and hence $\Sigma_s=0$ for every $s>0$.
	In the applications below, $\Sigma_{\alpha-1}$ is always finite by \eqref{eq:power:sigmaw:bound}, whereas finiteness of $\Sigma_\alpha$ is established separately at each use.

	\ref{lmm:power:interpol:itm:main}:
	With $\lambda := 2-\alpha \in [0,1)$ we have $\lambda(\alpha-1) + (1-\lambda)\alpha = (\alpha-1)(2-\alpha) + (\alpha-1)\alpha = 2\alpha-2$, so that \eqref{eq:power:interpol} gives $\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}\leq \Sigma_{\alpha-1}^{2-\alpha}\,\Sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}$.
	Raising this to the power $\frac1{\alpha-1} = 1 + \phi$ yields the assertion.

	\ref{lmm:power:interpol:itm:chord}:
	Note that $\phi \geq 2$ is equivalent to $\alpha \leq \frac43$, in which case $\alpha - 1 \leq 2\alpha-2 \leq 2-\alpha \leq \alpha$ and $2\alpha-2\in[0,1]$.
	Hence, $2-\alpha$ and $2\alpha-2$ are both convex combinations of $\alpha-1$ and $\alpha$, namely with the weights $\lambda = 2\alpha-2$ and $\lambda = 2-\alpha$ on $\alpha-1$, respectively, and \eqref{eq:power:interpol} gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\Sigma_{2-\alpha} \leq \Sigma_{\alpha-1}^{2\alpha-2}\, \Sigma_\alpha^{3-2\alpha}
		\,,\qquad
		\Sigma_{2\alpha-2} \leq \Sigma_{\alpha-1}^{2-\alpha}\, \Sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}
		\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Multiplying the $\phi$-th power of the first with the $(\phi-1)$-th power of the second gives the assertion, because $\phi(2\alpha-2)+(\phi-1)(2-\alpha)=\phi$ and $\phi(3-2\alpha)+(\phi-1)(\alpha-1)=\phi-1$, as $\phi=\frac{2-\alpha}{\alpha-1}$ and $\phi-1=\frac{3-2\alpha}{\alpha-1}$.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\label{lmm:power:absorb}
	In the setting of \cref{ssec:main:bound}, set
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:B}
		B_1 := \Sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\, \Sigma_{2\alpha-2}
		\,,\qquad
		B_2 := \Sigma_\alpha
		\,.
	\end{equation}
	Then
	\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
		\item \label{lmm:power:absorb:itm:var}
		$\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}^{1+\frac\phi2}\leq\sqrt{B_1B_2}\leq\max(B_1,B_2)$;
		\item \label{lmm:power:absorb:itm:ros}
		if $\phi\geq2$, then $\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}\Sigma_{2-\alpha}\leq B_1^{1/\phi}B_2^{1-1/\phi}\leq\max(B_1,B_2)$.
	\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Multiply \cref{lmm:power:interpol}\,\ref{lmm:power:interpol:itm:main} by $\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}$ and take a square root to obtain the first geometric-mean bound.
 For $\phi\geq2$, multiply \cref{lmm:power:interpol}\,\ref{lmm:power:interpol:itm:chord} by $\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}$ and take the $\phi$-th root to obtain the second.
 Each geometric mean is at most the maximum of its factors. Zero cases follow directly, with the exponent-zero convention when $\phi=0$.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- lemma-25: body_md -->
\label{lmm:power:empmom}
	In the setting of \cref{ssec:main:bound}, set
	\begin{equation*}
		V^{-i} := \left| \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{-i} - \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{-i} \right] \right|
		\,,\quad i\in\mathbb{N}
		\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Let $s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}$. Then
	\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
		\item \label{lmm:power:empmom:itm:low}
		if $s\leq2$, then $\mathbf{E}[(V^{-i})^{s}]\leq\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}^{s/2}$;
		\item \label{lmm:power:empmom:itm:high}
		if $s\geq2$, then $\mathbf{E}[(V^{-i})^{s}] \leq c_s(\Sigma_{(\alpha-1)s} + \Sigma_{2\alpha-2}^{\frac s2})$, with a finite constant $c_s$ depending only on $s$.
	\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-25 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Write $\xi_j := \overline{Y_j\bar m}^{\alpha-1}$, $\xi_j^\prime := \overline{Y_j^\prime\bar m}^{\alpha-1}$, and $X_j := \xi_j - \mathbf{E}[\xi_j]$ for $j\in\mathbb{N}$ with $w_j>0$.
	The $X_j$ are independent and centered. For each $i$, the partial sums of $\sum_{j\ne i}w_jX_j$ converge almost surely to $\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{-i}-\mathbf{E}[\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{-i}]$, because $\mathbf{E}[\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}]=\sigma_{\alpha-1}<\infty$ implies $\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}<\infty$ almost surely.

	\emph{Individual moments.}
	For every $r\geq1$,
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:empmom:individual}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \left| X_j \right|^{r} \right]
		\leq
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_jY_j^\prime}^{(\alpha-1)r} \right]
		\,.
	\end{equation}
	Indeed, independence and centering give $X_j = \mathbf{E}[\xi_j - \xi_j^\prime\mid Y_j]$, so that conditional Jensen, \cref{lmm:power:holder}, and the triangle inequality yield
	$\mathbf{E}[\vertX_j\vert^{r}] \leq \mathbf{E}[\vert\xi_j - \xi_j^\prime\vert^{r}] \leq \mathbf{E}[\vert\overline{Y_j\bar m} - \overline{Y_j^\prime\bar m}\vert^{(\alpha-1)r}] \leq \mathbf{E}[\overline{Y_jY_j^\prime}^{(\alpha-1)r}]$.

	\ref{lmm:power:empmom:itm:low}:
	If $\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}=\infty$ there is nothing to prove, so assume $\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}<\infty$.
	The independent-copy identity gives
 \[
 \mathbf{E}\!\left[ X_j^2 \right]=\frac12\mathbf{E}\!\left[ (\xi_j-\xi_j^\prime)^2 \right]\leq\frac12\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_jY_j^\prime}^{2\alpha-2} \right]=\mathbf{V}^{2\alpha-2}_{\!M}\!\left[ Y_j \right].
 \]
 Hence $\sum_{j\ne i}w_j^2\mathbf{E}[X_j^2]\leq\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}<\infty$, so the centered partial sums converge in $L^2$ to their almost sure limit. Therefore $\mathbf{E}[(V^{-i})^2]=\sum_{j\ne i}w_j^2\mathbf{E}[X_j^2]\leq\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}$. Jensen gives the stated bound for $s\leq2$.

	\ref{lmm:power:empmom:itm:high}:
	Apply the Rosenthal inequality, \cref{lmm:rosenthal} (bounding the maximum by the sum), to $(w_jX_j)_{j\leq N,\,j\ne i}$ and insert \eqref{eq:power:empmom:individual} with $r = s$ and $r = 2$; since $w_j^{s} = w_j^{(\alpha-1)s/(\alpha-1)}$, the two resulting series are bounded by $2\Sigma_{(\alpha-1)s}$ and $2\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}$.
	Fatou's lemma applied to $\vert\sum_{j\leq N,\,j\ne i}w_jX_j\vert^{s}$ transfers the bound to the almost sure limit as $N\to\infty$.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
The means exist under $\bar\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{\alpha-1}(\mathcal{Q})$ by Tonelli's theorem; the continuity argument in Step 7 also proves measurability under this assumption.
    If $\Sigma_\alpha=\infty$, the claims are immediate. Otherwise $\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}<\infty$ by interpolation and $\Sigma_{\alpha-1}\leq\sigma_{\alpha-1}<\infty$.
    In Steps 1--6, first suppose $\bar\mu\in\mathcal{P}^\alpha(\mathcal{Q})$.

	Fix $i \in \mathbb{N}$ with $w_i > 0$; indices with $w_i = 0$ do not contribute to any of the sums below.

	\emph{Step 1 (leave-one-out bound).}
	We show
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:loo}
		\overline{\hat m\hat m^i} \leq c_\alpha\, w_i\, \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{\alpha-1} \left( \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\,\phi} + \left( \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^i \right)^{\phi} \right)
		\,.
	\end{equation}
	By the triangle inequality, \cref{lmm:power:subadd:gen}, $\sum_j w_j = 1$, and \eqref{eq:power:rb},
	\begin{equation*}
		\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\hat\mu)
		=
		\sum_{j=1}^\infty w_j\, \overline{Y_j\hat m}^{\alpha-1}
		\leq
		\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1} + \overline{\hat m\bar m}^{\alpha-1}
		\leq
		c_\alpha \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}
		\,,
	\end{equation*}
	and analogously $\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\hat\mu^i) \leq c_\alpha\,\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^i$, as well as
	\begin{equation*}
		\overline{\hat m\hat m^i}^{\alpha - 1}
		\leq
		\overline{\hat m\bar m}^{\alpha-1} + \overline{\bar m\hat m^i}^{\alpha-1}
		\leq
		c_\alpha\left( \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1} + \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^i \right)
		\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Hence, using $(x + y)^\phi \leq \max(1, 2^{\phi-1})(x^\phi + y^\phi)$,
	\begin{equation*}
		\left( \sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\hat\mu) + \overline{\hat m\hat m^i}^{\alpha-1} \right)^\phi + \left( \sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\hat\mu^i) + \overline{\hat m\hat m^i}^{\alpha-1} \right)^\phi
		\leq
		c_\alpha \left( \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\,\phi} + \left( \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^i \right)^{\phi} \right)
		\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Since the coupling of $\hat\mu$ and $\hat\mu^i$ that is the identity on all atoms except the $i$-th one yields $W_{\alpha-1}(\hat\mu, \hat\mu^i)\leq w_i\, \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{\alpha-1}$, \eqref{eq:power:cp:moment} gives \eqref{eq:power:loo} whenever $\overline{\hat m\hat m^i}>0$; for $\overline{\hat m\hat m^i}=0$, \eqref{eq:power:loo} holds trivially.

	\emph{Step 2 (symmetrization).}
	Inserting \eqref{eq:power:loo} into \eqref{eq:loo}, after dropping the nonnegative second excess risk there, yields
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \mathcal E_{\bar\mu}(\hat m) \right]
		\leq
		c_\alpha \sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i^2\, \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{2\alpha - 2} \left( \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\,\phi} + \left( \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^i \right)^{\phi} \right) \right]
		\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Exchanging $Y_i$ and $Y_i^\prime$ leaves $\overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}$ invariant, maps $\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^i$ to $\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}$, and does not change the joint distribution.
	Hence, $\mathbf{E}[\overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{2\alpha-2}(\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^i)^\phi] = \mathbf{E}[\overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{2\alpha-2}\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\,\phi}]$ and
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:sym}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \mathcal E_{\bar\mu}(\hat m) \right]
		\leq
		c_\alpha \sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i^2\, \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{2\alpha - 2}\, \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\,\phi} \right]
		\,.
	\end{equation}

	\emph{Step 3 (three-way split of the modulus).}
	For $\alpha = 2$ we have $\phi = 0$ and $\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\,\phi} = 1$, so \eqref{eq:power:sym} already proves \eqref{eq:power:excess:bound}; the variance inequality and Jensen step below give \eqref{eq:power:main}, so Steps 3--6 may be skipped; assume therefore $\alpha\in(1,2)$, so that $\phi\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}$.
	Fix $i\in\mathbb{N}$ with $w_i>0$ and set
	\begin{equation*}
		V^{-i} := \left| \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{-i} - \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{-i} \right] \right|
		\,,\qquad
		X_j := \overline{Y_j\bar m}^{\alpha-1} - \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_j\bar m}^{\alpha-1} \right]
		\,,\quad j\in\mathbb{N} \text{ with } w_j>0
		\,.
	\end{equation*}
	From $\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1} - \sigma_{\alpha-1} = (\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{-i} - \mathbf{E}[\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{-i}]) + w_i\, X_i$ and the three-term case of \cref{lmm:power:subadd:gen}, $(x+y+z)^{\phi}\leq\max(1, 3^{\phi-1})(x^{\phi} + y^{\phi} + z^{\phi})$,
	\begin{equation*}
		\hat\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\,\phi}
		\leq
		c_\alpha\left( \sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi} + \left( V^{-i} \right)^{\phi} + w_i^{\phi}\, \left| X_i \right|^{\phi} \right)
		\,,
	\end{equation*}
	and inserting this into \eqref{eq:power:sym} yields
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:split}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \mathcal E_{\bar\mu}(\hat m) \right]
		\leq
		c_\alpha\sum_{i=1}^\infty w_i^{2}\left( 
			\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\, \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{2\alpha-2} \right]
			+
			\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{2\alpha-2}\left( V^{-i} \right)^{\phi} \right]
			+
			w_i^{\phi}\, \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{2\alpha-2}\, \left| X_i \right|^{\phi} \right]
		 \right)
		\,.
	\end{equation}
	The first summands add up to $2\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\, \Sigma_{2\alpha-2}$.

	\emph{Step 4 (leave-one-out fluctuation).}
	Since $V^{-i}$ is independent of $(Y_i, Y_i^\prime)$, the expectation $\mathbf{E}[\overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{2\alpha-2}(V^{-i})^{\phi}]$ factorizes into $\mathbf{E}[\overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{2\alpha-2}]\, \mathbf{E}[(V^{-i})^{\phi}]$.
	By \cref{lmm:power:empmom}\,\ref{lmm:power:empmom:itm:low} and \ref{lmm:power:empmom:itm:high}, applied to $V^{-i}$ with $s = \phi$ and using $(\alpha-1)\phi = 2-\alpha$,
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \left( V^{-i} \right)^{\phi} \right]
		\leq
		\begin{cases}
			\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}^{\frac\phi2} & \text{if } \phi\leq2\,,\\[0.5ex]
			c_\alpha\left( \Sigma_{2-\alpha} + \Sigma_{2\alpha-2}^{\frac\phi2} \right) & \text{if } \phi\geq2\,.
		\end{cases}
	\end{equation*}
	Hence, the second summands of \eqref{eq:power:split} add up to at most $c_\alpha(\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}^{1+\frac\phi2} + \mathbf{1}_{\{\phi>2\}}\, \Sigma_{2\alpha-2}\, \Sigma_{2-\alpha})$.

	\emph{Step 5 (diagonal term).}
	H\"older's inequality with the conjugate exponents $\frac{\alpha}{2\alpha-2}$ and $\frac{\alpha}{2-\alpha}$, followed by \eqref{eq:power:empmom:individual} with $r=\phi\cdot\frac{\alpha}{2-\alpha}=\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}\geq1$, yields
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{2\alpha-2}\, \left| X_i \right|^{\phi} \right]
		\leq
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{\alpha} \right]^{\frac{2\alpha-2}{\alpha}}\, \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \left| X_i \right|^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}} \right]^{\frac{2-\alpha}{\alpha}}
		\leq
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{\alpha} \right]
		\,,
	\end{equation*}
	so that the third summands of \eqref{eq:power:split} add up to at most $\sum_{i} w_i^{2+\phi}\, \mathbf{E}[\overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{\alpha}] = 2\Sigma_{\alpha}$.

	\emph{Step 6 (absorption).}
	By \cref{lmm:power:absorb}\,\ref{lmm:power:absorb:itm:var} and, for $\phi\geq2$, \ref{lmm:power:absorb:itm:ros},
	\begin{equation*}
		\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}^{1+\frac\phi2}
		\leq
		\max\!\left( \Sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\, \Sigma_{2\alpha-2},\ \Sigma_{\alpha} \right)
		\quad\text{and}\quad
		\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}\, \Sigma_{2-\alpha}
		\leq
		\max\!\left( \Sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\, \Sigma_{2\alpha-2},\ \Sigma_{\alpha} \right)
		\,,
	\end{equation*}
	and $\Sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\leq\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}$, since $\Sigma_{\alpha-1}\leq\sigma_{\alpha-1}$.

	Combining Steps 2--6 proves \eqref{eq:power:excess:bound}.
    Finally, \eqref{eq:power:vi}\xspace and Jensen's inequality \eqref{eq:power:cp:jensen} give, pointwise,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:excess:conversion}
        g(\overline{\bar m\hat m})\leq \overline{\bar m\hat m}^2\Lambda_\alpha\!\left( \bar\mu;\overline{\bar m\hat m} \right)
        \leq\frac{2}{\alpha(\alpha-1)}\,\mathcal E_{\bar\mu}(\hat m).
    \end{equation}
    At $\overline{\bar m\hat m}=0$ all three quantities vanish by convention. Taking expectations proves \eqref{eq:power:main}. Centered-moment upper bounds may subsequently be obtained from $\mathbf{E}[\overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^s]\leq2^{\max(s,1)}\mathbf{E}[\overline{Y_i\bar m}^s]$ for $s\in\{2\alpha-2,\alpha\}$.

    \emph{Step 7 (minimal mixture moment).}
 Fix $o\in\mathcal{Q}$ and put $r_N=\sum_{i>N}w_i$,
 \[
 \bar\mu_N=\sum_{i=1}^Nw_i\mu_i+r_N\delta_o,
 \qquad
 \hat\mu_N=\sum_{i=1}^Nw_i\delta_{Y_i}+r_N\delta_o.
 \]
 Finiteness of a pairwise moment of order $a>0$ implies finiteness of the corresponding ordinary moment: by Fubini there is a fixed $z$ with $\int d(y,z)^a\,\mathrm{d}\mu_i(y)<\infty$, and the triangle inequality then permits the reference point $o$.
 Thus every active component of each truncated problem has a finite $\alpha$-moment, because $\Sigma_\alpha<\infty$.
 The added coordinate at $o$ is deterministic and has zero pairwise fluctuation.
 The case just proved applies to each truncated problem, with the corresponding partial pairwise sums: writing $\Sigma_s^{(N)}:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_i^{s/(\alpha-1)}\mathbf{V}^{s}_{\!M}[Y_i]$ for the truncated weighted moments,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:excess:truncated}
  \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \mathcal E_{\bar\mu_N}\!\left( m_\alpha(\hat\mu_N) \right) \right]
  \leq c_\alpha\left( \left( \sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\bar\mu_N) \right)^{\phi}\,\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}^{(N)}+\Sigma_{\alpha}^{(N)} \right)
  \,,
 \end{equation}
 with one constant $c_\alpha$ for all $N$; the coordinate added at $o$ is deterministic, so $\Sigma_s^{(N)}\leq\Sigma_s$ for every $s$.

 Let $\eta,\eta_N\in\mathcal{P}^{\alpha-1}(\mathcal{Q})$ with $W_{\alpha-1}(\eta_N,\eta)\to0$ as $N\to\infty$. All limits in this step are taken as $N\to\infty$.
 The one-sided contraction \eqref{eq:power:cp:onesided} with $\mu=\eta_N$ and $\nu=\eta$, together with $\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\eta)<\infty$ and $\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}\leq W_{\alpha-1}$, gives $m_\alpha(\eta_N)\to m_\alpha(\eta)$.
 Moreover, \cref{lmm:power:holder}, the triangle inequality, and \cref{lmm:power:subadd:gen} give $|d(y,q)^{\alpha-1}-d(z,q^\prime)^{\alpha-1}|\leq d(y,z)^{\alpha-1}+d(q,q^\prime)^{\alpha-1}$ for all $y,z,q,q^\prime\in\mathcal{Q}$; integrating against a coupling of $\eta_N$ and $\eta$ shows
 \[
 |\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\eta_N)-\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\eta)|
 \leq W_{\alpha-1}(\eta_N,\eta)+d(m_\alpha(\eta_N),m_\alpha(\eta))^{\alpha-1}\longrightarrow0.
 \]
 For $\alpha=2$, the usual $W_1$ contraction also gives the mean convergence directly.

 In the present truncation,
 \[
 \begin{aligned}
 W_{\alpha-1}(\bar\mu_N,\bar\mu)&\leq\sum_{i=N+1}^{\infty}w_i\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{Y_io}^{\alpha-1} \right]\to0,\\
 W_{\alpha-1}(\hat\mu_N,\hat\mu)&\leq\sum_{i=N+1}^{\infty}w_i\overline{Y_io}^{\alpha-1}\to0
 \quad\text{almost surely}.
 \end{aligned}
 \]
 The latter follows from Tonelli. Hence both truncated means converge and the population centered $(\alpha-1)$-moments converge. Finite-coordinate means are measurable by continuity, so the limiting empirical mean is measurable as well. This continuity argument uses only $\bar\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{\alpha-1}(\mathcal{Q})$ and also applies when $\Sigma_\alpha=\infty$.
 Here $\Sigma_{\alpha-1}\leq\sigma_{\alpha-1}<\infty$ and H\"older interpolation makes $\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}<\infty$ from $\Sigma_\alpha<\infty$.
 To pass the excess risk to the limit, work on an outcome where the empirical means converge and put
 $q_N:=m_\alpha(\hat\mu_N)$, $m_N:=m_\alpha(\bar\mu_N)$,
 $h_N(y):=d(y,q_N)^\alpha-d(y,m_N)^\alpha$.
 The two sequences of centers are bounded. The mean value theorem and the power triangle inequality therefore give
 \[
  |h_N(y)|\leq C(1+d(y,o)^{\alpha-1})
 \]
 with a finite outcome-dependent $C$ independent of $N,y$.
 This envelope is $\bar\mu$-integrable. Dominated convergence yields
 $\int h_N\,\mathrm{d}\bar\mu\to\mathcal E_{\bar\mu}(\hat m)$, and the omitted coordinates satisfy
 \[
  \left|\int h_N\,\mathrm{d}\bar\mu_N-\int h_N\,\mathrm{d}\bar\mu\right|
  \leq r_N|h_N(o)|+
       C\sum_{i=N+1}^{\infty}w_i\int(1+d(y,o)^{\alpha-1})\,\mathrm{d}\mu_i(y)\longrightarrow0.
 \]
 Hence $\mathcal E_{\bar\mu_N}(q_N)\to\mathcal E_{\bar\mu}(\hat m)$ almost surely. These excess risks are nonnegative, so Fatou's lemma applied to \eqref{eq:power:excess:truncated}, together with $\Sigma_s^{(N)}\leq\Sigma_s$ and $\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\bar\mu_N)\to\sigma_{\alpha-1}$, proves \eqref{eq:power:excess:bound} in the limit.
 The pointwise comparison \eqref{eq:power:excess:conversion}, valid under an $(\alpha-1)$-moment, then gives both bounds in \eqref{eq:power:main}, without requiring continuity of the inverse moment at zero.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
If $\Sigma_\alpha=\infty$, the bound is immediate; otherwise interpolation gives $\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}<\infty$.

	If $\sigma_{\alpha-1} = 0$, then $Y_i = \bar m$ almost surely for every $i$ with $w_i>0$, hence $\hat m = \bar m$ almost surely and there is nothing to show; so assume $\sigma_{\alpha-1}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}$.
	Write $\mathcal G := \mathbf{E}[g(\overline{\bar m\hat m})]$, and set $A_1 := \sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\, \Sigma_{2\alpha-2}$ and $A_2 := \Sigma_\alpha$, so that $\mathcal G \leq c_\alpha(A_1 + A_2)$ by \cref{prp:power:excess}.

	\emph{Step 1 (conversion of the loss).}
	We apply \cref{prp:app:conversion} with $b = \sigma_{\alpha-1}$, so that $\psi = g$, and take the $\alpha$-th root of the resulting bound.
	Using $(1+x)^{\frac \phi\alpha}\leq c_\alpha(1 + x^{\frac \phi\alpha})$ for $x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq0}$ together with the exponent identities $\frac\phi2 - \frac\alpha2\cdot\frac \phi\alpha = 0$ and $\frac12 + \frac{\alpha-1}{2}\cdot\frac \phi\alpha = \frac1\alpha$, which hold because $(\alpha-1)\phi = 2-\alpha$, we obtain
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:sharp:phi}
		\left( \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\bar m\hat m}^\alpha \right] \right)^{\frac1\alpha}
		\leq
		c_\alpha\, \Phi(\mathcal G)
		\,,\qquad\text{where}\qquad
		\Phi\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq0},\ P\mapsto \sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\frac\phi2}\, P^{\frac12} + P^{\frac1\alpha}
		\,.
	\end{equation}
	As $t\mapsto t^{1/2}$ and $t\mapsto t^{1/\alpha}$ are nondecreasing and subadditive, so is $\Phi$, and $\Phi(cP)\leq\max(c^{\frac12}, c^{\frac1\alpha})\Phi(P)$ for $c\geq0$.
	Hence, $\mathcal G \leq c_\alpha(A_1+A_2)$ and \eqref{eq:power:sharp:phi} give
	\begin{equation*}
	\begin{aligned}
		\left( \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\bar m\hat m}^\alpha \right] \right)^{\frac1\alpha}
		&\leq
		c_\alpha\left( \Phi(A_1) + \Phi(A_2) \right)
		\,,\\
		\Phi(A_1) &= \sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\, \Sigma_{2\alpha-2}^{\frac12} + A_1^{\frac1\alpha}
		\,,\\
		\Phi(A_2) &= \sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\frac\phi2}\, \Sigma_\alpha^{\frac12} + \Sigma_\alpha^{\frac1\alpha}
		\,.
	\end{aligned}
	\end{equation*}
	It remains to bound $A_1^{1/\alpha}$ by the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:power:rate:sharp}.

	\emph{Step 2 (absorption of $A_1^{1/\alpha}$).}
	Set $\vartheta := \sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}}$.
	By \eqref{eq:power:interpol} with $s_0=\alpha-1$, $s_1=\alpha$, and $\lambda=2-\alpha$, $\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}\leq\Sigma_{\alpha-1}^{2-\alpha}\Sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}$, together with $\Sigma_{\alpha-1}\leq\sigma_{\alpha-1}$ and the identity $\phi + 2 - \alpha = \phi\alpha = \frac{\alpha(2-\alpha)}{\alpha-1}$,
	\begin{equation*}
		A_1
		=
		\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\, \Sigma_{2\alpha-2}
		\leq
		\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi\alpha}\, \Sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}
		=
		\vartheta^{2-\alpha}\, A_2^{\alpha-1}
		\leq
		\max\!\left( \vartheta, A_2 \right)
		\,,
	\end{equation*}
	the last step being the weighted arithmetic--geometric mean inequality with the weights $2-\alpha$ and $\alpha-1$, which are nonnegative and sum to $1$.
	If $A_1\leq A_2$, then $A_1^{\frac1\alpha}\leq\Sigma_\alpha^{\frac1\alpha}$.
	Otherwise $A_1\leq\vartheta$, and, using $\frac1\alpha = \frac12 + \frac{2-\alpha}{2\alpha}$ and $\vartheta^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2\alpha}} = \sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\frac\phi2}$,
	\begin{equation*}
		A_1^{\frac1\alpha}
		=
		A_1^{\frac12}\, A_1^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2\alpha}}
		\leq
		\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\frac\phi2}\, A_1^{\frac12}
		=
		\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\, \Sigma_{2\alpha-2}^{\frac12}
		\,.
	\end{equation*}
	This completes the three-term bound.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
Extend the finite sample by zero-weight coordinates. With $w_i=n^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{\{i\leq n\}}$, the identity is
 \begin{equation*}
 \Sigma_s=n^{-s/(\alpha-1)}\sum_{i=1}^n\mathbf{V}^{s}_{\!M}\!\left[ Y_i \right]
          =n^{1-s/(\alpha-1)}\bar V_s.
 \end{equation*}
 Apply \cref{thm:power:main}.
 The three terms are, up to constants depending only on $\alpha$,
 \[
 \sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\bar V_{2\alpha-2}^{1/2}n^{-1/2},\quad
 \sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi/2}\bar V_\alpha^{1/2}n^{-1/(2(\alpha-1))},\quad
 \bar V_\alpha^{1/\alpha}n^{-1/(\alpha(\alpha-1))}.
 \]
 This is the pairwise bound \eqref{eq:power:rate:iid}. The exponent comparisons stated in the corollary prove the rate under its uniform moment bounds. Centered-moment versions follow from $\bar V_s\leq2^{\max(s,1)-1}\sigma_s$.
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- lemma-26: body_md -->
\label{lmm:power:moments}
 In the setting of \cref{ssec:main:bound}, the following hold.
 \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item $T_1+T_2+T_3<\infty$ if and only if $\Sigma_\alpha<\infty$. For finitely many positive weights, this holds if and only if every active component has a finite $\alpha$-moment.
  For infinitely many weights, $\bar\mu\in\mathcal{P}^\alpha(\mathcal{Q})$ is not necessary: like \cref{ex:two:infinitevariance}\,(b), take $\mathcal{Q}=\mathbb{R}$, $w_i=2^{-i}$ and $\mu_i=\frac12(\delta_{-a_i}+\delta_{a_i})$ with $a_i=2^{i/\alpha}$; then $\sum_iw_i\mathbf{E}[\vertY_i\vert^\alpha]=\infty$, whereas $\sigma_{\alpha-1}=\sum_i2^{-i/\alpha}<\infty$ and $\Sigma_\alpha=2^{\alpha-2}\sum_i2^{-i/(\alpha-1)}<\infty$.
  \item Let $1<\alpha<2$. For every $M<\infty$ there are weights and laws with finite support on $\mathbb{R}$ such that $\mathbf{E}[\overline{\bar m\hat m}^\alpha]^{1/\alpha}>M(\Sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi}\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}^{1/2}+\Sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi/2}\Sigma_\alpha^{1/2}+\Sigma_\alpha^{1/\alpha})$; that is, $\sigma_{\alpha-1}$ cannot be replaced by $\Sigma_{\alpha-1}$ in \eqref{eq:power:rate:sharp} with a constant depending only on $\alpha$.
  \item Let $1<\alpha<2$. For each $j\in\{1,2,3\}$, the ratio $T_j/\sum_{k\neq j}T_k$ is unbounded over weights and laws with finite support on $\mathbb{R}$.
 \end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-26 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
\emph{(i).} Since $\Sigma_{\alpha-1}\leq\sigma_{\alpha-1}<\infty$, interpolation gives $\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}^{1/(\alpha-1)}\leq\Sigma_{\alpha-1}^\phi\Sigma_\alpha$ whenever $\Sigma_\alpha<\infty$, so $\Sigma_\alpha<\infty$ implies $T_1+T_2+T_3<\infty$; the converse follows from $T_3$.
 For finitely many positive weights, a finite pairwise moment gives an ordinary moment by Fubini's theorem, as in Step 7 of the proof of \cref{prp:power:excess}, and conversely.
 In the example for infinitely many weights, $\bar m=0$ by symmetry, $\mathbf{E}[\vertY_i\vert^{s}]=a_i^{s}$ and $\mathbf{V}^{s}_{\!M}(\mu_i)=2^{s-2}a_i^{s}$, so $w_i\mathbf{E}[\vertY_i\vert^\alpha]=1$, $w_ia_i^{\alpha-1}=2^{-i/\alpha}$ and $w_i^{\alpha/(\alpha-1)}\mathbf{V}^{\alpha}_{\!M}(\mu_i)=2^{\alpha-2}2^{-i/(\alpha-1)}$.
 In (ii) and (iii), constants in $\asymp$ may depend on $\alpha$.

 \emph{(ii), and (iii) for $j=1$.}
 Take weights $w,(1-w)/2,(1-w)/2$ and laws $\frac12(\delta_{-1}+\delta_1),\delta_{-1},\delta_1$, respectively, with $w\searrow0$.
 The population mixture is uniform on $\{-1,1\}$, so $\bar m=0$, $\sigma_{\alpha-1}=1$, and $\Sigma_s=2^{s-2}w^{s/(\alpha-1)}$ for every $s>0$.
 Thus
 \[
  T_1\asymp w,\qquad T_2\asymp w^{\alpha/(2(\alpha-1))},\qquad T_3\asymp w^{1/(\alpha-1)},
 \]
 and $T_1/(T_2+T_3)\to\infty$, which is (iii) for $j=1$.
 The empirical mean has a random sign and deterministic absolute value
 \[
  |\hat m|=
  \frac{(1+w)^{1/(\alpha-1)}-(1-w)^{1/(\alpha-1)}}{(1+w)^{1/(\alpha-1)}+(1-w)^{1/(\alpha-1)}}
  \sim\frac{w}{\alpha-1}\qquad(w\searrow0).
 \]
 Replacing $\sigma_{\alpha-1}$ by $\Sigma_{\alpha-1}=2^{\alpha-3}w$ makes all three terms of order $w^{1/(\alpha-1)}=\mathrm{o}(w)$ as $w\searrow0$, which proves (ii).

 \emph{(iii) for $j=2$.}
 Take weights $1/2,1/4,1/4$ and laws $\mu_1=(1-\epsilon)\delta_0+\frac\epsilon2(\delta_{-R}+\delta_R)$, $\delta_{-1}$, $\delta_1$, where $R\to\infty$ and $\epsilon=R^{-\alpha-1}$.
 Symmetry gives $\bar m=0$ and $\sigma_{\alpha-1}=(1+\epsilon R^{\alpha-1})/2\to1/2$.
 For each fixed $s>0$, $\mathbf{E}[|Y_1-Y_1^\prime|^s]\asymp\epsilon R^s$, so
 \[
  T_1\asymp R^{(\alpha-3)/2},\qquad
  T_2\asymp R^{-1/2},\qquad T_3\asymp R^{-1/\alpha}.
 \]
 Hence $T_2/(T_1+T_3)\to\infty$.

 \emph{(iii) for $j=3$.}
 Take a single observation with law $(1-\epsilon)\delta_0+\epsilon\delta_1$, where $\epsilon\searrow0$.
 Its population mean is $m=\epsilon^{1/(\alpha-1)}/(\epsilon^{1/(\alpha-1)}+(1-\epsilon)^{1/(\alpha-1)})$.
 The first-order condition gives $\sigma_{\alpha-1}=2\epsilon(1-m)^{\alpha-1}\sim2\epsilon$, and $\Sigma_s=\epsilon(1-\epsilon)$ for every $s>0$.
 Thus
 \[
  T_1\asymp\epsilon^{\phi+1/2},\qquad
  T_2\asymp\epsilon^{1/(2(\alpha-1))},\qquad T_3\asymp\epsilon^{1/\alpha}.
 \]
 Since $\phi+1/2>1/\alpha$ and $1/(2(\alpha-1))>1/\alpha$, $T_3/(T_1+T_2)\to\infty$.
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- remark-14: body_md -->
\label{rem:power:calpha}
 The proof of \cref{thm:power:main} gives a constant $c_\alpha$ that is bounded on every compact subinterval of $(1,2]$, since the only $\alpha$-dependent input that is not explicit, the Rosenthal constant of \cref{lmm:rosenthal} at $s=\phi$, can be chosen locally bounded in $s$.
 Conversely, for $1<\alpha<2$, every constant $c_\alpha$ for which \eqref{eq:power:rate:sharp} holds for all weights and laws satisfies
 \begin{equation*}
  c_\alpha\geq\max\left\lbrace 2^\phi,\frac{2^{2-\alpha}}{\alpha-1} \right\rbrace
  \,,
 \end{equation*}
 so that $c_\alpha\to\infty$ as $\alpha\searrow1$.
 For the first bound, let $1<\alpha<2$ and take weights $\frac12,\frac12$ and laws $\mu_1=(1-\epsilon)\delta_0+\epsilon\delta_1$, $\mu_2=\delta_0$ on $\mathbb{R}$, with $\epsilon\searrow0$.
 Then $\bar m\sim(\epsilon/2)^{1/(\alpha-1)}$, the empirical mean is $\frac12$ with probability $\epsilon$ and $0$ otherwise, so the risk is $\epsilon^{1/\alpha}/2\,(1+\mathrm o(1))$, while $T_3=2^{-1/(\alpha-1)}(\epsilon(1-\epsilon))^{1/\alpha}$, $T_1=\operatorname{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{\phi+1/2})$, and $T_2=\operatorname{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{1/(2(\alpha-1))})$, both $\mathrm o(\epsilon^{1/\alpha})$; hence the ratio of the risk to $T_1+T_2+T_3$ tends to $2^{1/(\alpha-1)-1}=2^\phi$.
 For the second bound, in the example for (ii) and (iii) with $j=1$ in the proof of \cref{lmm:power:moments}, $T_1=2^{\alpha-2}w$ and $T_2+T_3=\mathrm o(w)$, while the risk is $w/(\alpha-1)\,(1+\mathrm o(1))$ as $w\searrow0$.
<!-- end remark-14 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
Put $m_x:=m_\alpha(\mu_x)$, $m_i:=m_\alpha(\mu_{x_i})$,
 $\bar\mu:=\sum_iw_i\mu_{x_i}$, and $\bar m:=m_\alpha(\bar\mu)$.
 Set $\mathcal B:=Lh^\beta$, $M:=u^{1/\alpha}$, $a:=\Vertw\Vert_2\leq1$, and
 $\epsilon:=\max_{i:w_i>0}\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu_{x_i},\mu_x)$.
 For each active index with $x_i\ne x$, put $b_i:=L\rho(x_i,x)^\beta\leq\mathcal B$.
 By \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:smooth} and monotonicity of $\Psi_{\mu_x}$,
 \begin{equation*}
 \widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu_{x_i},\mu_x)
 \leq b_i\Lambda_\alpha\!\left( \mu_x;b_i \right)=\Psi_{\mu_x}(b_i)\leq\Psi_{\mu_x}(\mathcal B).
 \end{equation*}
 Indices with $x_i=x$ have zero transport cost, so $\epsilon\leq\Psi_{\mu_x}(\mathcal B)$.
 Thus \cref{lmm:power:cp:mixture} gives
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:localfrechet:bias}
 \max_{i:w_i>0}\overline{m_im_x}+\overline{\popfmm_x}
 \leq c_\alpha\Psi_{\mu_x}^{-1}(\epsilon)\leq c_\alpha\mathcal B.
 \end{equation}
 By \cref{lmm:variances},
 $\int\overline{ym_i}^\alpha\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{x_i}(y)\leq2u=2M^\alpha$.
 The minimizing property of $\bar m$, the power triangle inequality, and \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:bias} yield
 \begin{equation*}
 \sigma_\alpha^\alpha(\bar\mu)
 \leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_i\int\overline{ym_x}^\alpha\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{x_i}(y)
 \leq c_\alpha(M+\mathcal B)^\alpha,
 \qquad
 \sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\bar\mu)\leq c_\alpha(M+\mathcal B)^{\alpha-1},
 \end{equation*}
 the second by Lyapunov's inequality.
 For independent copies $Y_i^\prime$, the definition of $u$ gives
 $\mathbf{E}[\overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^\alpha]\leq2M^\alpha$ and, since $2\alpha-2\leq\alpha$,
 $\mathbf{E}[\overline{Y_iY_i^\prime}^{2\alpha-2}]\leq(2M^\alpha)^{(2\alpha-2)/\alpha}$.
 Set $\tau:=\alpha/(\alpha-1)\geq2$. Then
 \begin{equation*}
 \Sigma_{2\alpha-2}^{1/2}\leq c_\alpha M^{\alpha-1}a,
 \qquad
 \Sigma_\alpha\leq M^\alpha\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_i^\tau\leq M^\alpha a^\tau.
 \end{equation*}
 Inserting these bounds into \cref{thm:power:main}, and using $\tau/2\geq1$, $1/(\alpha-1)\geq1$, and $a\leq1$, gives
 \begin{align*}
 \left( \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\hat m_h\!(x)\bar m}^\alpha \right] \right)^{1/\alpha}
 &\leq c_\alpha\left( (M+\mathcal B)^{2-\alpha}M^{\alpha-1}a
              +(M+\mathcal B)^{(2-\alpha)/2}M^{\alpha/2}a^{\tau/2}
              +Ma^{1/(\alpha-1)} \right)\\
 &\leq c_\alpha(M+\mathcal B)a.
 \end{align*}
 The triangle inequality in $L^\alpha$ and $\mathcal Ba\leq\mathcal B$ give the more general bound
 \begin{equation*}
 \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{\hat m_h\!(x)m_\alpha(\mu_x)}^\alpha \right]^{1/\alpha}
 \leq c_\alpha\left( Lh^\beta+u^{1/\alpha}\left\Vertw\right\Vert_2 \right).
 \end{equation*}
 The bound $\Vertw\Vert_2^2\leqC_{\mathsf{Kmi}}C_{\mathsf{Kma}}/N_n(x,h/2)$ and the design assumption give \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet}.
 The bandwidth calculation is the same as for \cref{cor:two:localreg}, with $v=u^{2/\alpha}$, and proves \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:optimal}.
<!-- end proof-39 -->
<!-- proof-40: body_md -->
In $\mathcal{H}$, translation equivariance gives $m_\alpha[\gamma(x)+\varepsilon]=\gamma(x)+m_\alpha[\varepsilon]=\gamma(x)$; this point lies in $\mathcal{Q}$ and minimizes $q\mapsto\mathbf{E}[\Vert\gamma(x)+\varepsilon-q\Vert^\alpha]$ over all of $\mathcal{H}$, hence over $\mathcal{Q}$, so $m_\alpha(\mu_x)=\gamma(x)$ and
 \[
 \Lambda_\alpha\!\left( \mu_x;t \right)=\mathbf{E}\!\left[ (\left\Vert\varepsilon\right\Vert+t)^{\alpha-2} \right],\qquad t>0,
 \]
 independently of $x$.
 For an independent copy $\varepsilon^\prime$, the power triangle inequality gives
 $\mathbf{V}^{\alpha}_{\!M}(\mu_x)=\frac12\mathbf{E}[\Vert\varepsilon-\varepsilon^\prime\Vert^\alpha]\leq2^{\alpha-1}\mathbf{E}[\Vert\varepsilon\Vert^\alpha]$.
 Fix distinct $x,x^\prime$.
 If $\Vert\gamma(x)-\gamma(x^\prime)\Vert=0$, the two laws agree and their transport cost vanishes.
 Otherwise, couple $Y_x=\gamma(x)+\varepsilon$ and $Y_{x^\prime}=\gamma(x^\prime)+\varepsilon$ using the same noise, so that $\overline{Y_xY_{x^\prime}}=\Vert\gamma(x)-\gamma(x^\prime)\Vert$.
 Put $a:=\gamma(x)-\gamma(x^\prime)$. The four points entering the diameter in \eqref{eq:power:Wtilde} are $\gamma(x)+\varepsilon$, $\gamma(x^\prime)+\varepsilon$, $\gamma(x)$ and $\gamma(x^\prime)$, so that diameter is at least $\max\{\Vert\varepsilon+a\Vert,\Vert\varepsilon-a\Vert\}$, and the parallelogram law $\Vert\varepsilon+a\Vert^2+\Vert\varepsilon-a\Vert^2=2(\Vert\varepsilon\Vert^2+\Verta\Vert^2)$ gives
 \begin{equation*}
  \mathsf{diam}\left\lbrace Y_x,Y_{x^\prime},\gamma(x),\gamma(x^\prime) \right\rbrace
  \geq\sqrt{\left\Vert\varepsilon\right\Vert^2+\left\Vert\gamma(x)-\gamma(x^\prime)\right\Vert^2}
  \geq2^{-1/2}\left( \left\Vert\varepsilon\right\Vert+\left\Vert\gamma(x)-\gamma(x^\prime)\right\Vert \right)
  \,.
 \end{equation*}
 Hence
 \begin{align*}
 \widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu_x,\mu_{x^\prime})
 &\leq2^{(2-\alpha)/2}\left\Vert\gamma(x)-\gamma(x^\prime)\right\Vert\,
     \mathbf{E}\!\left[ (\left\Vert\varepsilon\right\Vert+\left\Vert\gamma(x)-\gamma(x^\prime)\right\Vert)^{\alpha-2} \right]\\
 &=2^{(2-\alpha)/2}\Psi_{\mu_x}(\left\Vert\gamma(x)-\gamma(x^\prime)\right\Vert)\\
 &\leq\Psi_{\mu_x}(2^{\phi/2}\left\Vert\gamma(x)-\gamma(x^\prime)\right\Vert).
 \end{align*}
 The last step uses \eqref{eq:power:Psi:scaling} and $(\alpha-1)\phi=2-\alpha$.
 Since $2^{\phi/2}\Vert\gamma(x)-\gamma(x^\prime)\Vert\leq2^{\phi/2}L_\gamma\rho(x,x^\prime)^\beta=b$ and both laws have the same $\Lambda_\alpha$, monotonicity proves \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:smooth} with $L=2^{\phi/2}L_\gamma$. \Cref{cor:power:localfrechet} then gives \eqref{eq:power:additive}, the factors $2^{\phi/2}$ and $2^{(\alpha-1)/\alpha}$ being absorbed into $c_\alpha$.
<!-- end proof-40 -->
<!-- proof-41: body_md -->
For the symmetric two-point example, take $x=0$ and $x^\prime=t>0$, so that the means are $0$ and $t$.
 The coupling of corresponding atoms pairs $-1$ with $t-1$ and $1$ with $1+t$; both diameters in \eqref{eq:power:Wtilde} equal $1+t$, so it gives $\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu_0,\mu_t)\leq t(1+t)^{\alpha-2}$ for every $t>0$, and $\Lambda_\alpha(\mu_0;t)=(1+t)^{\alpha-2}$.
 The two cross costs are $(2-t)^{\alpha-1}$ and $(2+t)^{\alpha-1}$.
 For $0<t\leq1/2$, each corresponding-atom cost is smaller than either cross cost; thus the corresponding-atom coupling is optimal.
 The same comparison for ordinary transport gives $W_{\alpha-1}(\mu_0,\mu_t)=t^{\alpha-1}$.
 Its ratio to $Lt\Lambda_\alpha(\mu_0;Lt)=Lt(1+Lt)^{\alpha-2}$ tends to infinity as $t\searrow0$ for every fixed $L>0$ when $\alpha<2$.

 To see that the moment-only criterion \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:sufficient} is strictly stronger for $\alpha<2$, let
 $\mathcal L(\varepsilon)=\frac12\delta_0+\frac14\delta_{-1}+\frac14\delta_1$ and $\gamma(x)=x$.
 Then $m_\alpha[\varepsilon]=0$ and $\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\mu_x)=1/2$.
 For small $t>0$, each source atom is cheapest to serve by its synchronous partner: the cost is $t\cdot t^{\alpha-2}=t^{\alpha-1}$ for the atom at $0$ and $t(1+t)^{\alpha-2}$ for those at $\pm1$, whereas every other destination costs at least $1-t$.
 Summing these per-atom minima against the source masses $\frac12,\frac14,\frac14$ gives a lower bound equal to the synchronous cost, so
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:atomexample}
  \widetilde W_{\alpha-1}(\mu_0,\mu_t)=\frac{t^{\alpha-1}}2+\frac{t(1+t)^{\alpha-2}}2=\Psi_{\mu_0}(t)\sim\frac{t^{\alpha-1}}2
  \qquad(t\searrow0)
  \,.
 \end{equation}
 For $\alpha<2$, the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:sufficient} with $b=Lt$ is at most $2^{\phi}Lt=\mathrm{o}(t^{\alpha-1})$ as $t\searrow0$, so the criterion fails at $\beta=1$ for every finite $L$, although \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:smooth} holds by \cref{cor:power:additive}, and in fact with $L=1$ and with equality, by \eqref{eq:power:atomexample}.
<!-- end proof-41 -->
<!-- proof-42: body_md -->
Let $L_\gamma,L_\zeta$ be H\"older constants of $\gamma,\zeta$.
	Spherical symmetry of $Z$ makes the law in \eqref{eq:power:hyperbolic:model} independent of the choice of $I_x$ and invariant under every isometry fixing $\gamma(x)$.
	Its unique $\alpha$-Fr\'echet mean is therefore fixed by that whole isotropy group; since the group acts transitively on each sphere of $T_{\gamma(x)}\mathbb H^k$ and $\exp_{\gamma(x)}$ is a bijection onto $\mathbb H^k$, the only common fixed point is $\gamma(x)$ itself, so the mean is $\gamma(x)$.
	The exponential map preserves distance from its base point, so
	$\overline{Y_x\gamma(x)}=\zeta(x)\VertZ\Vert_2$.
	The radial density of $\VertZ\Vert_2$ is of order $r^{-\theta-1}$ at infinity; hence $\mathbf{E}[\VertZ\Vert_2^\alpha]<\infty$ and $\mathbf{E}[\VertZ\Vert_2^2]=\infty$.
	Moreover
	\begin{equation*}
		\sup_x\mathbf{V}^{\alpha}_{\!M}\!\left( \mu_x \right)
		\leq2^{\alpha-1}\zeta_+^\alpha\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \left\VertZ\right\Vert_2^\alpha \right]<\infty
		\,.
	\end{equation*}

	It remains to verify the symmetric smoothness condition. We first bound the cost $W_{\alpha-1}$.
	For any $\alpha\in(1,2]$, densities $f,g$ with respect to a common measure $\lambda$, and $o\in\mathcal{Q}$, retaining their common mass and coupling the remainders gives
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:power:localfrechet:commonmass}
		W_{\alpha-1}(f\lambda,g\lambda)
		\leq\int d(y,o)^{\alpha-1}\left| f(y)-g(y) \right|\,\mathrm{d}\lambda(y)
		\,.
	\end{equation}
	Indeed, $d(y,z)^{\alpha-1}\leq d(y,o)^{\alpha-1}+d(z,o)^{\alpha-1}$, and the two remainder marginals sum to $\vertf-g\vert\lambda$.

	For $r=d(y,m)$, the density of $\exp_m(\zeta IZ)$ with respect to hyperbolic volume is
	\begin{equation*}
		f_{m,\zeta}(y)
		=c_{k,\theta}\zeta^{-k}
		\left( 1+\frac{r^2}{\theta \zeta^2} \right)^{-(k+\theta)/2}
		\left( \frac r{\sinh(r)} \right)^{k-1}
		\,,
	\end{equation*}
	with the last factor interpreted continuously at $r=0$.
	Here $c_{k,\theta}$ is the normalizing constant of the standard $k$-dimensional Student density; the last factor is the reciprocal of the Jacobian $(\sinh(r)/r)^{k-1}$ of the hyperbolic exponential map.
	Direct differentiation gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\partial_r\log f_{m,\zeta}
		=-\frac{(k+\theta)r}{\theta \zeta^2+r^2}
		+(k-1)\left( \frac1r-\coth(r) \right)
		\,,\qquad
		\partial_\zeta\log f_{m,\zeta}
		=\frac1\zeta\left( -k+\frac{(k+\theta)r^2}{\theta \zeta^2+r^2} \right)
		\,.
	\end{equation*}
	Since $0\leq\coth(r)-1/r\leq1$ for $r>0$, these derivatives satisfy
	\begin{equation*}
		\left| \partial_r\log f_{m,\zeta} \right|
		\leq A_r:=\frac{k+\theta}{2\sqrt\theta\,\zeta_-}+k-1
		\,,\qquad
		\left| \partial_\zeta\log f_{m,\zeta} \right|
		\leq A_\zeta:=\frac{\max(k,\theta)}{\zeta_-}
		\,.
	\end{equation*}
 Fix $x,x^\prime\in[0,1]$ and put $\ell:=\vertx-x^\prime\vert\leq1$.
 Join $\gamma(x)$ to $\gamma(x^\prime)$ by a constant-speed geodesic $m_t$, $t\in[0,1]$, and set $\zeta_t=(1-t)\zeta(x)+t\zeta(x^\prime)$ and $f_t=f_{m_t,\zeta_t}$.
 Since $t\mapsto d(y,m_t)$ is Lipschitz with constant $d(\gamma(x),\gamma(x^\prime))\leq L_\gamma\ell^\beta$, $t\mapsto\zeta_t$ is Lipschitz with constant $\vert\zeta(x)-\zeta(x^\prime)\vert\leq L_\zeta\ell^\beta$, and $\log f_{m,\zeta}$ is Lipschitz in $(r,\zeta)$ by the bounds above, $t\mapsto f_t(y)$ is absolutely continuous for every $y$, and for almost every $t$,
 \[
 \left| \partial_t f_t(y) \right|\leq B\ell^\beta f_t(y),
 \qquad B:=A_rL_\gamma+A_\zeta L_\zeta.
 \]
 Apply \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:commonmass} with $o=\gamma(x)$, write $f_1-f_0=\int_0^1\partial_tf_t\,\mathrm{d} t$, and use Tonelli's theorem:
 \begin{align*}
 W_{\alpha-1}(\mu_x,\mu_{x^\prime})
 &\leq B\ell^\beta\int_0^1\int d(y,\gamma(x))^{\alpha-1}f_t(y)\,\mathrm{d}\operatorname{vol}(y)\,\mathrm{d} t\\
 &\leq B\ell^\beta\left( \zeta_+^{\alpha-1}\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \left\VertZ\right\Vert_2^{\alpha-1} \right]+(L_\gamma\ell^\beta)^{\alpha-1} \right)
 \leq C\ell^\beta.
 \end{align*}
 Put $\bar\sigma_{\sup}:=\sup_x\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\mu_x)\leq\zeta_+^{\alpha-1}\mathbf{E}[\VertZ\Vert_2^{\alpha-1}]<\infty$, which bounds the pairwise maximum $\bar\sigma$ of \cref{lmm:power:sufficient} for every pair $\mu_x,\mu_{x^\prime}$.
 Since $\widetilde W_{\alpha-1}\leq W_{\alpha-1}\leq C\ell^\beta$, the criterion \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:sufficient} holds with $b=L\ell^\beta$ for every $L\geq\max\{C\bar\sigma_{\sup}^\phi,C^{1/(\alpha-1)}\}$, because $\ell\leq1$.
 Thus \cref{lmm:power:sufficient}\,\ref{lmm:power:sufficient:itm:tworegime} proves \eqref{eq:power:localfrechet:smooth} with the H\"older constant $2^{\phi/(\alpha-1)}L$.
 This verifies the claimed smoothness condition for arbitrary H\"older location and scale functions.
 Finally, the curves $\gamma=\exp_o\circ\gamma_0$ suggested in \cref{ex:power:hyperbolic} are $\beta$-H\"older whenever $\gamma_0$ is $\beta$-H\"older, and hence bounded, on $[0,1]$.
 By \eqref{eq:power:hyperbolic:exp}, $\exp_o$ is smooth as a map into $\mathbb{R}^{k+1}$, since $\cosh(\Vertv\Vert)$ and $\sinh(\Vertv\Vert)/\Vertv\Vert$ are power series in $\Vertv\Vert^2$; hence it is Lipschitz on bounded subsets of $T_o\mathbb H^k$ for the Euclidean distance of $\mathbb{R}^{k+1}$.
 Moreover, $d(p,q)\leq\Vertp-q\Vert_2$ for all $p,q\in\mathbb H^k$: from $\langle p,p\rangle_{1,k}=\langle q,q\rangle_{1,k}=-1$ we get $-\langle p,q\rangle_{1,k}=1+\frac12\langle p-q,p-q\rangle_{1,k}\leq1+\frac12\Vertp-q\Vert_2^2$, and $\operatorname{arcosh}(1+t)\leq\sqrt{2t}$ for $t\geq0$ because $\cosh(\sqrt{2t})\geq1+t$.
<!-- end proof-42 -->
<!-- proof-43: body_md -->
Abbreviate $\tau:=\alpha/(\alpha-1)=2+\phi\geq2$ throughout this proof, so that $H_\tau=H_{2+\phi}$.
 For $V\sim\operatorname{Beta}(1,a)$ and $s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}$, beta integration gives
 \[
 \mathbf{E}\!\left[ V^s \right]=\frac{\Gamma(s+1)\Gamma(a+1)}{\Gamma(a+s+1)},
 \qquad \mathbf{E}\!\left[ (1-V)^s \right]=\frac a{a+s}.
 \]
 Independence of the stick breaks and summation of a geometric series prove \eqref{eq:power:dirichlet:weights}.
 For any $o\in\mathcal{Q}$, Tonelli's theorem gives
 $\mathbf{E}[\int\overline{yo}^\alpha\,\mathrm{d} P(y)]=\int\overline{yo}^\alpha\,\mathrm{d}\mu(y)<\infty$.
 Thus $P\in\mathcal{P}^\alpha(\mathcal{Q})$ almost surely, and its power Fr\'echet mean exists.

 Conditionally on the weights, the atoms in \eqref{eq:dirichlet:stickbreaking} are iid with law $\mu$ and the population mixture is $\mu$.
 Write $S_t:=\sum_iW_i^t$ and $v_t:=\mathbf{V}^{t}_{\!M}(\mu)$.
 Then $\Sigma_{2\alpha-2}=v_{2\alpha-2}S_2$ and $\Sigma_\alpha=v_\alpha S_\tau$.
 Applying \cref{thm:power:main} conditionally gives, with $\sigma_{\alpha-1}:=\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\mu)$ and the constant $c_\alpha$ of that theorem,
 \[
 \left( \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \overline{m_\alpha(P)m_\alpha(\mu)}^\alpha\,\middle|\,W \right] \right)^{1/\alpha}
 \leq c_\alpha\left( 
 \sigma_{\alpha-1}^\phi v_{2\alpha-2}^{1/2}S_2^{1/2}
 +\sigma_{\alpha-1}^{\phi/2}v_\alpha^{1/2}S_\tau^{1/2}
 +v_\alpha^{1/\alpha}S_\tau^{1/\alpha} \right).
 \]
 Take the $L^\alpha$ norm over the weights on both sides and use Minkowski's inequality.
 Since $\alpha/2\leq1$, Jensen's inequality yields
 $\VertS_t^{1/2}\Vert_{L^\alpha}\leq(\mathbf{E}[S_t])^{1/2}$,
 while $\VertS_\tau^{1/\alpha}\Vert_{L^\alpha}=(\mathbf{E}[S_\tau])^{1/\alpha}$.
 Substitution of $\mathbf{E}[S_2]=H_2(a)$ and $\mathbf{E}[S_\tau]=H_\tau(a)$ proves \eqref{eq:power:dirichlet:prior}.
 The conjugacy statement in \cref{ssec:dirichlet} and $\tilde\mu_n\in\mathcal{P}^\alpha(\mathcal{Q})$ give \eqref{eq:power:dirichlet:posterior} by the same argument.

 To prove the simpler bound, first observe that
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:dirichlet:Hbound}
 H_\tau(b)\leq\Gamma(\tau)(b+1)^{1-\tau},\qquad b>0,
 \end{equation}
 because a gamma random variable $Z$ with shape $b+1$ and unit scale satisfies
 $\Gamma(b+\tau)/\Gamma(b+1)=\mathbf{E}[Z^{\tau-1}]\geq(\mathbf{E}[Z])^{\tau-1}=(b+1)^{\tau-1}$ by Jensen's inequality.
 Put $\mathcal M:=\mathbf{V}^{\alpha}_{\!M}(\nu)$.
 Lyapunov's inequality and \cref{lmm:variances} imply
 $\sigma_\alpha^{\alpha-1}(\nu)\leq\sigma_\alpha^\alpha(\nu)^{(\alpha-1)/\alpha}=\mathbf{V}^{\alpha}_{\!F}(\nu)^{(\alpha-1)/\alpha}\leq(2\mathcal M)^{(\alpha-1)/\alpha}$,
 and Lyapunov's inequality gives
 $\mathbf{V}^{2\alpha-2}_{\!M}(\nu)\leq c_\alpha\mathcal M^{(2\alpha-2)/\alpha}$.
 Consequently each term of \eqref{eq:power:dirichlet:R} is bounded by $c_\alpha\mathcal M^{1/\alpha}$ times its weight factor.
 Since $\tau-1=1/(\alpha-1)$, $1/(2(\alpha-1))\geq1/2$, and $1/(\alpha(\alpha-1))\geq1/2$, \eqref{eq:power:dirichlet:Hbound} gives
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:power:dirichlet:coarse}
 \mathcal R_\alpha(b,\nu)
 \leq c_\alpha\frac{\mathbf{V}^{\alpha}_{\!M}(\nu)^{1/\alpha}}{\sqrt{b+1}}.
 \end{equation}
 The case $\mathcal M=0$ is immediate, including at $\alpha=2$ under the exponent-zero convention.
 Apply this with $b=a+n$ and $\nu=\tilde\mu_n$.
 The computation leading to \eqref{eq:dirichlet:bayesrisk} applies verbatim with $d^\alpha$ in place of $d^2$ and gives $\mathbf{E}[\mathbf{V}^{\alpha}_{\!M}(\tilde\mu_n)]=\frac{a(a+n+1)}{(a+1)(a+n)}\mathbf{V}^{\alpha}_{\!M}(\mu)$.
 Raise the conditional bound to the $\alpha$-th power and take expectations to obtain \eqref{eq:power:dirichlet:bayes}.
 The prior case follows directly from \eqref{eq:power:dirichlet:prior} and \eqref{eq:power:dirichlet:coarse}.
<!-- end proof-43 -->
<!-- proof-44: body_md -->
Write $\Gamma:=\gamma(\mathbb{R})$ and let $\lambda$ be the image of the Lebesgue measure under $\gamma$, the arc-length measure on $\Gamma$.

\emph{(i) Transport smoothness.}
The coupling $(\gamma(x+\tau U),\gamma(x^\prime+\tau U))$ of $\mu_x$ and $\mu_{x^\prime}$ has cost $\mathbf{E}[\overline{\gamma(x+\tau U)\gamma(x^\prime+\tau U)}]=\vertx-x^\prime\vert$, so $W_1(\mu_x,\mu_{x^\prime})\leq\vertx-x^\prime\vert$.
For the reverse inequality and the formula for the mean, note that $\Gamma$ is closed and convex in $\mathcal{Q}$: it is complete as an isometric image of $\mathbb{R}$, and the unique geodesic between $\gamma(s)$ and $\gamma(t)$ is $\gamma|_{[s,t]}$.
The metric projection $P\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\Gamma$ is nonexpansive with $P|_\Gamma=\mathrm{id}$ \cite[Proposition II.2.4]{BridsonHaefliger1999NPC-Springer}, so $\overline{P(q)y}\leq\overline{qy}$ for all $y\in\Gamma$ and $\mathbf{E}[\overline{Y_xP(q)}^2]\leq\mathbf{E}[\overline{Y_xq}^2]$ for all $q\in\mathcal{Q}$.
Hence the unique minimizer $m_2(\mu_x)$ \cite[Proposition 4.3]{Sturm2003NPC-ContempMath} lies in $\Gamma$; as $\gamma$ is an isometry of $\mathbb{R}$ onto $\Gamma$, $\mathbf{E}[\overline{Y_x\gamma(t)}^2]=\mathbf{E}[(x+\tau U-t)^2]$ is minimized exactly at $t=x$, i.e., $m_2(\mu_x)=\gamma(x)$.
Now \eqref{eq:cat0:2fm:CP} gives $\vertx-x^\prime\vert=\overline{\gamma(x)\gamma(x^\prime)}\leq W_1(\mu_x,\mu_{x^\prime})$, which proves the first identity in \eqref{eq:two:phase:w1}.
For an independent copy $U^\prime$ of $U$, $\mathbf{V_{\!M}}(\mu_x)=\frac12\mathbf{E}[\overline{\gamma(x+\tau U)\gamma(x+\tau U^\prime)}^2]=\frac{\tau^2}2\mathbf{E}[(U-U^\prime)^2]=\tau^2\mathbf{E}[U^2]$.
For the design $x_i=i/n$, $x\in[0,1]$, and $4/n\leq h\leq1$, one of the half-windows $[x,x+h/2]$ and $[x-h/2,x]$ lies in $[0,1]$; it has length $h/2$ and therefore contains at least $\lfloor nh/2\rfloor\geq nh/2-1\geq nh/4$ of the points $i/n$, $i\in\{1,\dots,n\}$.
Thus $N_n(x,h/2)\geq nh/4$, which is \eqref{eq:localfrechet:design} with $D=1$ and $C_{\mathsf{dns}}=4$, and inserting $L=1$, $\beta=1$, $v=\tau^2\mathbf{E}[U^2]$, $D=1$, and $C_{\mathsf{dns}}=4$ into \eqref{eq:localfrechet:designbound} yields \eqref{eq:two:phase:bound}.

\emph{(ii) Density smoothness, uniform noise.}
Let $\tau>0$ and $U\sim\mathsf{Unif}\!\left( [-1,1] \right)$, and put $A_x:=\gamma([x-\tau,x+\tau])$, so that $\mu_x=(2\tau)^{-1}\lambda|_{A_x}$.
Let $\nu$ be a probability measure with $\mu_x\ll\nu$ for all $x\in[0,1]$.
Every $s\in[-\tau,1+\tau]$ admits a rational $x\in[0,1]$ with $\verts-x\vert\leq\tau$, so $A:=\gamma([-\tau,1+\tau])$ is the union of the sets $A_x$ over $x\in T:=\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]$, and $\lambda|_A\ll\nu$; let $g$ be a density of $\lambda|_A$ with respect to $\nu$.
Then $f_x:=(2\tau)^{-1}g\mathbf{1}_{A_x}$ is a version of $\mathrm{d}\mu_x/\mathrm{d}\nu$, and every other version agrees with $f_x$ outside a $\nu$-null set.
Suppose $\tilde f(\cdot\mid x)$, $x\in[0,1]$, were versions such that $x\mapsto\tilde f(y\mid x)$ is continuous for all $y$ outside a $\nu$-null set $N_0$.
Then $N:=N_0\cup\bigcup_{x\in T}\{\tilde f(\cdot\mid x)\neq f_x\}$ is $\nu$-null.
The set $B:=\{\gamma(s):s\in(-\tau,1-\tau),\,g(\gamma(s))>0\}$ has positive $\nu$-measure, because $1=\lambda(\gamma((-\tau,1-\tau)))=\int_{\gamma((-\tau,1-\tau))}g\,\mathrm{d}\nu$.
Pick $y=\gamma(s)\in B\setminus N$.
For $x\in T$ we have $\tilde f(y\mid x)=(2\tau)^{-1}g(y)\mathbf{1}\{\verts-x\vert\leq\tau\}$, which equals $(2\tau)^{-1}g(y)>0$ for rational $x\nearrow s+\tau$ and $0$ for rational $x\searrow s+\tau$; since $s+\tau\in(0,1)$, this contradicts the continuity of $x\mapsto\tilde f(y\mid x)$.
Hence no such versions exist, and the assumption of \cite{Schoetz2022Regression-EJS} fails for every $\beta>0$ and every $\nu$.
For random covariates with a positive Lipschitz density $f_X$ on the interior of the covariate interval, the conditional density of $X$ given $Y=\gamma(s)$ is proportional to $x\mapsto f_X(x)\mathbf{1}\{\verts-x\vert\leq\tau\}$.
Whenever $s+\tau\in(0,1)$, it has a jump there, so the local constant Lipschitz condition on $g_y$ fails as well.

\emph{(ii) Density smoothness, triangular noise.}
Now let $U$ have the density $\psi(u):=(1-\vertu\vert)_+$, so that $\mathbf{E}[U^2]=1/6$, and let $\nu:=(1+2\tau)^{-1}\lambda|_A$ with $A=\gamma([-\tau,1+\tau])$ as before.
For $y=\gamma(s)\in A$, the density of $\mu_x$ with respect to $\nu$ is $f(y\mid x)=(1+2\tau)\tau^{-1}\psi((s-x)/\tau)$, and since $\psi$ is $1$-Lipschitz, $\vertf(y\mid x)-f(y\mid x^\prime)\vert\leq(1+2\tau)\tau^{-2}\vertx-x^\prime\vert$.
Thus the density smoothness assumption of \cite{Schoetz2022Regression-EJS} holds with $\beta=1$, the constant function $L\equiv(1+2\tau)/\tau^2$, $C_{\mathrm{SmD}}=\max\{1,(1+2\tau)/\tau^2\}$, and the continuous map $x\mapsto m_2(\mu_x)=\gamma(x)$.
For its remaining constants, take $C_{\mathrm{Len}}=1$, which bounds $\sup_{x,x^\prime\in[0,1]}\overline{m_2(\mu_x)m_2(\mu_{x^\prime})}$, and
\[
 C_{\mathrm{Int}}=\max\left\{1,\frac{1+\tau+\tau^2}{3}\right\},
 \qquad
 \int\overline{y\gamma(0)}^2\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y)
 =\frac{(1+\tau)^3+\tau^3}{3(1+2\tau)}
 =\frac{1+\tau+\tau^2}{3}.
\]

For the lower bound with $\beta=1$, let $\nu$ be any admissible reference measure, with Lipschitz constants $L(y)$.
As in the uniform case, $\lambda|_A\ll\nu$, since $\mu_x$ has a positive density with respect to $\lambda$ on $\gamma((x-\tau,x+\tau))$; let $g$ be a density of $\lambda|_A$ with respect to $\nu$.
For each $x\in T$, $f(\gamma(s)\mid x)=\tau^{-1}\psi((s-x)/\tau)\,g(\gamma(s))$ for $\nu$-almost every $\gamma(s)\in A$; as both sides are continuous in $x$ for $\nu$-almost every $y$, this holds for all $x\in[0,1]$ outside a single $\nu$-null set.
For $s\in(-\tau,1+\tau)$, the map $x\mapsto\tau^{-1}\psi((s-x)/\tau)$ has slope $\pm\tau^{-2}$ on a nondegenerate subinterval of $[0,1]$, so $L(y)\geq\tau^{-2}g(y)$ for $\nu$-almost every $y\in A$, and
\[
 C_{\mathrm{SmD}}\geq\VertL\Vert_{L^2(\nu)}\geq\VertL\Vert_{L^1(\nu)}\geq\tau^{-2}\int_Ag\,\mathrm{d}\nu=\tau^{-2}\lambda(A)=\frac{1+2\tau}{\tau^2}\,.
\]
For $\beta\in(0,1]$ and $\tau\leq\frac12$, let $\nu$ and $L$ be admissible with exponent $\beta$ and let $g$ be as above; as for $\beta=1$, $f(\gamma(s)\mid x)=\tau^{-1}\psi((s-x)/\tau)\,g(\gamma(s))$ for all $x\in[0,1]$ and $\nu$-almost every $\gamma(s)\in A$.
For $s\in[0,1]$, one of the intervals $[s-\tau,s]$ and $[s,s+\tau]$ lies in $[0,1]$, and between its endpoints $x\mapsto\tau^{-1}\psi((s-x)/\tau)$ changes by $\tau^{-1}$; hence $L(y)\geq\tau^{-1-\beta}g(y)$ for $\nu$-almost every $y\in\gamma([0,1])$, and
\[
 C_{\mathrm{SmD}}\geq\VertL\Vert_{L^1(\nu)}\geq\tau^{-1-\beta}\lambda(\gamma([0,1]))=\tau^{-1-\beta}\,.
\]
Conversely, $\vert\psi(u)-\psi(u^\prime)\vert\leq\min\{1,\vertu-u^\prime\vert\}\leq\vertu-u^\prime\vert^\beta$, so the normalized arc-length measure on $A$ is admissible with $L\equiv(1+2\tau)\tau^{-1-\beta}\geq1$.
Finally, for every admissible choice, the transport constant $C_{\mathrm{Int}}^{1/2}C_{\mathrm{SmD}}$ that the implication at the beginning of this subsection extracts from the density condition is at least $(1+2\tau)/\tau^2$, since $C_{\mathrm{Int}}\geq1$, against the true value $L=1$ in \eqref{eq:two:phase:w1}.
<!-- end proof-44 -->
<!-- lemma-27: body_md -->
\label{lmm:power:subadd:gen}
    Let $n\in\mathbb{N}$, $x_1,\dots,x_n \in\mathbb{R}_{\geq0}$ and $p\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq0}$, with the convention $0^0:=1$. Then
    \begin{equation*}
        \left( \sum_{i=1}^n x_i \right)^{\!p} \leq n^{\max(0, p-1)}\sum_{i=1}^n x_i^p
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-27 -->
<!-- proof-45: body_md -->
For $0<p\leq1$ this is concavity and subadditivity; for $p\geq1$ it is Jensen's inequality with equal weights; and $p=0$ follows from the convention.
<!-- end proof-45 -->
<!-- lemma-28: body_md -->
\label{lmm:power:ana:diff}
    Let $x_1, x_2 \in\mathbb{R}_{\geq0}$ and $p\in(1,2]$. Then
    \begin{equation*}
        \left| x_1^p - x_2^p \right|
        \leq
        \left| x_1 - x_2 \right|\left( x_1^{p-1} + x_2^{p-1} \right)
        \,.
    \end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-28 -->
<!-- proof-46: body_md -->
The case $x_1=x_2$ is immediate. By symmetry assume $x_1>x_2$ and put $r=p-1\in(0,1]$. The identity
 \[
 x_1^p-x_2^p-(x_1-x_2)(x_1^r+x_2^r)
 =x_2x_1^r-x_1x_2^r
 \]
 has a nonpositive right-hand side because $r\leq1$.
<!-- end proof-46 -->
<!-- lemma-29: body_md -->
\label{lmm:power:holder}
	Let $r\in(0,1]$ and $x,y\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq0}$. Then $\vertx^r - y^r\vert \leq \vertx-y\vert^r$.
<!-- end lemma-29 -->
<!-- proof-47: body_md -->
Assume $x\geq y$. By \cref{lmm:power:subadd:gen} with $p = r\in(0,1]$, so that $2^{\max(0,r-1)} = 1$, applied to $x - y$ and $y$,
	$x^r = ((x-y)+y)^r \leq (x-y)^r + y^r$.
<!-- end proof-47 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prp:app:conversion}
	Let $\alpha\in(1,2]$, $\phi = \frac{2-\alpha}{\alpha-1}$, and $b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}$, and set $\psi(x) := x^2(b+x^{\alpha-1})^{-\phi}$ for $x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq0}$.
	Then, for every nonnegative random variable $X$,
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:app:conversion}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ X^\alpha \right]
		\leq
		b^{\frac{\phi\alpha}{2}}\, \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \psi(X) \right]^{\frac\alpha2}\left( 1 + b^{-\frac\alpha2}\, \mathbf{E}\!\left[ \psi(X) \right]^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}} \right)^{\phi}
		\,.
	\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-48: body_md -->
Put $s=(\alpha-1)/\alpha$ and $\omega=(2-\alpha)/\alpha$. First take $b=1$ and bounded $X$.
 Set
 \[
 f(y)=\frac{y^{2/\alpha}}{(1+y^{(\alpha-1)/\alpha})^\phi}=yh(y),
 \qquad h(y)=\left( \frac{y^s}{1+y^s} \right)^\phi,\qquad f(0)=0.
 \]
 For $y>0$, direct differentiation yields
 \[
 f''(y)=\frac{\omega\,h(y)\left( 1+\omega+(1-s)y^s \right)}{y(1+y^s)^2}\geq0.
 \]
 Thus $f$ is convex and increasing on $\mathbb{R}_{\geq0}$; at $\alpha=2$ it is the identity.
 With $r=\mathbf{E}[\psi(X)]$, Jensen gives $f(\mathbf{E}[X^\alpha])\leq r$. If $r=0$, $X=0$ almost surely.
 Otherwise define $y_*=r^{\alpha/2}(1+r^{(\alpha-1)/2})^\phi$. Writing $t=r^{(\alpha-1)/2}$, the inequality $f(y_*)\geq r$ reduces to
 \[
 (1+t)^{1+\omega}\geq1+t(1+t)^\omega,
 \]
 which follows from $(1+t)^\omega\geq1$. Monotonicity implies $\mathbf{E}[X^\alpha]\leq y_*$.
 Applying the result to $X\wedge M$ and then taking $M\to\infty$ proves it for arbitrary nonnegative $X$, with extended expectations allowed.
 Finally write $\psi_b$ for the displayed loss at scale $b$ and set $X=b^{1/(\alpha-1)}Z$. Then $\psi_b(X)=b^{\alpha/(\alpha-1)}\psi_1(Z)$, and rescaling gives exactly \eqref{eq:app:conversion}.
<!-- end proof-48 -->
<!-- lemma-30: body_md -->
\label{lmm:rosenthal}
	Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be real-valued, independent, centered random variables and let $s \geq 2$. Then
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathbf{E}\!\left[ \left| \sum_{i=1}^n X_i \right|^s \right] \leq c_s \max\!\left(  \sum_{i=1}^n\mathbf{E}\!\left[ |X_i|^s \right],\ \left( \sum_{i=1}^n \mathbf{E}\!\left[ X_i^2 \right] \right)^{\frac s2} \right)
		\,,
	\end{equation*}
 where $c_s<\infty$ depends only on $s$.
 The constant can be chosen locally bounded in $s$: for symmetric $X_i$ and $s>2$, Johnson, Schechtman, and Zinn \cite[Theorem 4.1]{JohnsonEtAl1985MomentInequalities-AOP} give the inequality with $c_s\leq(Ks/\max\{1,\log(s)\})^s$ for a universal constant $K\leq7.35$, and replacing $X_i$ by $X_i-X_i^\prime$ for independent copies $X_i^\prime$ extends this to centered $X_i$ with $2K$ in place of $K$; for $s=2$, $c_2=1$.
<!-- end lemma-30 -->
